Exotic diffeomorphisms on a contractible 4-manifold surviving two stabilizations
Sungkyung Kang, JungHwan Park, Masaki Taniguchi

Abstract
. We develop a Pin ⁡ ( 2 ) × ℤ 2 \mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2} -equivariant refinement of the lattice homotopy type for computing equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy types. As an application, we construct a relative exotic diffeomorphism on a compact contractible 4 4 –manifold that survives two stabilizations.



1.
Introduction

Exotic phenomena refer to differences that can be detected in the smooth category but remain indistinguishable in the topological category. Dimension 4 is the lowest dimension in which such phenomena occur, making it a subject of extensive study since the 1980s
[
Freedman:1982
,
Donaldson:1983
]
. There are three main cases of exotic phenomena in dimension 4:

•

Exotic manifolds: smooth 4-manifolds
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
that are homeomorphic but not diffeomorphic.

•

Exotic diffeomorphisms: diffeomorphisms
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
of a 4-manifold that are topologically isotopic but not smoothly isotopic.

•

Exotic surfaces: smoothly embedded surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
in a 4-manifold that are topologically isotopic but not smoothly isotopic.

A foundational principle in 4-dimensional topology, discovered by Wall in the 1960s
[
Wa64
,
Wa64-2
]
, states that exotic phenomena vanish after finitely many stabilizations, that is, after taking the connected sum with finitely many copies of
S^{2}\times S^{2}
. In other words, 4-dimensional exotic phenomena are unstable. In the case of diffeomorphisms, we will give a precise formulation below, and analogous statements hold for manifolds and for surfaces. For an excellent overview of these topics, see
[
Lin:2023-1
, Section 1]
.

Given a 4-manifold
X
with possibly nonempty boundary, we say that a diffeomorphism
f\colon X\to X
is
exotic
if
f
is topologically, but not smoothly, isotopic to the identity while fixing the boundary pointwise. Combining results from many works
[
kreck2006isotopy
,
Qu86
,
cochran1990homotopy
,
saeki2006stable
,
Orson-Powell:2022-1
,
gabai2023pseudo
,
gabai3
]
(see also
[
KMPW24
, Theorem 2.5]
), it is known that any such exotic diffeomorphism acting as the identity on
\partial X
is
stably isotopic
to the identity rel. boundary whenever
X
is simply connected,
\partial X
is connected, and
b_{1}(\partial X)=0
; that is, there exists a positive integer
n
such that the stabilized diffeomorphism

f\#\mathrm{id}_{(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#n}}\colon X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#n}\longrightarrow X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#n}

is smoothly isotopic to the identity rel. boundary, where
(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#n}
denotes the connected sum of
n
copies of
S^{2}\times S^{2}
. Here, the
stabilized diffeomorphism
is obtained by first isotoping the diffeomorphism
f
so that it fixes a small ball pointwise, and then taking the connected sum with the identity map on
(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#n}
along the ball. This construction is proved to be well defined in
[
auckly2015stable
, Theorem 5.3]
, that is, it depends only on the isotopy class of
f
.

Naturally, one can ask how many stabilizations are needed to eliminate a given exotic phenomenon. For a long time, there was no evidence suggesting the need for more than one stabilization; on the contrary, many results indicated that one is sufficient
[
mandelbaum1979decomposing
,
akbulut2002variations
,
baykur2013round
,
baykur2018dissolving
,
auckly2015stable
,
auckly2019isotopy
]
. Lin’s groundbreaking work
[
Lin:2023-1
]
provided the first instances in which more than one stabilization is necessary, using the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-equivariant version of the families Bauer–Furuta invariant. Since then, there has been an explosion of results showing that one stabilization is insufficient to trivialize various 4-dimensional exotica
[
LM21
,
kang2022one
,
hayden2023one
,
konno2022exotic
,
guth2024invariant
]
(see also
[
guth2022exotic
,
Auckly:2023-1
]
for internal stabilizations of exotic surfaces).

In this article, we provide the first example in which even
two
stabilizations are not sufficient. Moreover, this yields the first instance of a diffeomorphism on a
contractible

4
-manifold that persists under stabilization.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a smooth compact contractible
4
-manifold
X
with nonempty boundary, and an infinite family of relative diffeomorphisms
\{f_{i}\colon X\to X\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the following properties:

•

f_{i}
is topologically isotopic to the identity rel. boundary;

•

f_{i}
and
f_{j}
are not smoothly isotopic rel. boundary for
i\neq j
;

•

the stabilized diffeomorphism

f_{i}\#\mathrm{id}_{(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}}\colon X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}\longrightarrow X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}

is not smoothly isotopic to the identity rel. boundary.

We now describe the 4-manifold and the diffeomorphisms appearing in the main theorem.
In
[
fintushel1981exotic
]
, celebrated for establishing an exotic orientation-reversing free involution on
S^{4}
,
Fintushel and Stern showed that the Brieskorn sphere
\Sigma(3,5,19)
bounds a
Mazur manifold
, a smooth, compact, contractible 4-manifold admitting a handle decomposition with a single 1-handle and a single 2-handle (see also
[
Fickle:1984
, Proposition 4.2]
). For
X
in
1.1
, we may take any smooth compact contractible manifold bounded by
\Sigma(3,5,19)
. For instance,
X
can be taken as the Mazur manifold of Fintushel and Stern; see
Figure

1
for its Kirby diagram.

Figure 1.
The Mazur manifold bounded by
\Sigma(3,5,19)
.

For the diffeomorphism, we consider the 4-dimensional Dehn twist.
Let
Y
be a closed, oriented 3-manifold, and let
\phi\in\pi_{1}\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(Y)
be a nontrivial element based at the identity.
The
4-dimensional Dehn twist
associated to
\phi
is the diffeomorphism

\Phi\colon Y\times[0,1]\longrightarrow Y\times[0,1];\qquad(s,t)\longmapsto(\phi_{t}(s),t).

If
Y
bounds a compact, smooth 4-manifold
X
, then the diffeomorphism
\Phi
induces a diffeomorphism of
X
supported in a collar neighborhood of the boundary, called the
boundary Dehn twist of
X
. More generally, if
Y
is smoothly embedded in
X
with an orientable normal bundle, then
\Phi
induces a diffeomorphism of
X
supported in a tubular neighborhood of
Y
, called the
Dehn twist of
X
along
Y
.

For the diffeomorphisms in
1.1
, we choose them to be odd iterates of the boundary Dehn twist of a smooth compact contractible filling
X
of
\Sigma(3,5,19)
, where
\phi
is given by rotation around the Seifert fibers. We remark that in
[
Lin:2023-1
]
, Lin proves that the Dehn twist of
\mathrm{K3}\#\mathrm{K3}
along the separating
S^{3}
remains exotic after a single stabilization (see also
[
Giansiracusa:2008-1
,
KM20
,
Krannich-Kupers:2021-1
,
Baraglia-Konno:2022-1
,
Orson-Powell:2022-1
,
KMT23
,
KPT24
,
KLMM24
,
Qiu24
,
Miyazawa24
]
for other related results on of 4-dimensional Dehn twists).

To prove
1.1
, we use the fact that if any odd iterate of the boundary Dehn twist of a smooth filling of
Y=\Sigma(3,5,19)
is isotopic to the identity rel. boundary, then the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action induced by the Seifert
S^{1}
-action on
Y
extends to a smooth homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on the filling (see
[
KPT24
, Section 3]
for a more detailed explanation). The nonexistence of such a homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on a smooth compact contractible filling
X
of
Y
was established in
[
KPT24
]
, together with the fact that all of these iterates are distinct up to isotopy rel. boundary. The main part of the proof of
1.1
is to show that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
Y
still does not extend to a homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}
.

A key topological step in the proof is the development of the “connected sum technique”, which reduces the stabilization problem for the boundary Dehn twist of a
4
-manifold
X
to the corresponding problem for
X^{\#4}
. More precisely, in
6.4
we prove that for any nonnegative integer
k
, if the boundary Dehn twist on
X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#k}
is isotopic rel. boundary to the identity, then the relative diffeomorphism obtained by performing the boundary Dehn twist on each boundary component of
X^{\#2n}\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#k}
is also isotopic rel. boundary to the identity for any positive integer
n
. Such a phenomenon is unexpected and counterintuitive, as it implies that even when the manifold becomes more complicated by taking connected sums, the number of copies of
S^{2}\times S^{2}
needed to kill the exotic phenomenon stays the same, or may even decrease. On the obstruction side, namely the algebraic side, one does not expect such behavior. In fact, as we see in
5.42
and
6.10
, taking connected sums produces a strictly stronger obstruction. For our specific example, it turns out that taking a connected sum is
necessary
, as shown in
5.42
, and in fact the minimal number of connected sums required to obtain the desired obstruction is four, as noted in
6.11
.

For the algebraic obstruction, we use the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant local equivalence class of the chain group of Montague’s
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant spectrum

SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\left(\bigsqcup_{4}\Sigma(3,5,19)\right)=\bigwedge_{4}SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Sigma(3,5,19)),

corresponding to the Seifert
(-1)
-action on the fiber, which is an
even
action.
1
1

1

For background on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer theory for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
3-manifolds, see
[
montague2022seiberg
,
baraglia2024brieskorn
,
Baraglia-Hekmati:2024-1
,
iida2024monopoles
]
. There has been various preceding studies of
G
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten theory for
G
-equivariant closed 4-manifolds, see
[
cho2002cyclic
,
nakamura2002free
,
baldridge2004seiberg
,
math0602654
,
cho2007finite
,
kiyono2011nonsmoothable
,
baraglia2024equivariant
]
for example.

Moreover, we use the homotopy coherent Bauer–Furuta invariant and
[
KPT24
, Section 3]
to obtain
2.40
. Together with
5.40
and the “connected sum technique”, that is,
6.4
, we obtain the key obstruction
6.6
. This lemma states that if the boundary Dehn twist on
X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}
is smoothly isotopic to the identity rel. boundary, then there exists a
local map of level 2
(see
5.35
for the precise definition) of the form

C^{*}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(S^{0})\longrightarrow C^{*}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\bigwedge_{2n}SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Sigma(3,5,19))\right),

for any positive integer
n
. For our purposes, we set
n=2
.

To calculate the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant spectrum, we develop a
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant version of the lattice homotopy type introduced in
[
dai2023lattice
]
.
To this end, we analyze how a sequence of equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures can be constructed using Donnelly’s equivariant Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem
[
donnelly1978eta
]
, combined with Némethi’s combinatorial construction
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
]
of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures on plumbing graphs.
We summarize our result on
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lattice homotopy for Seifert
S^{1}
-actions here.

Theorem 1.2

(
4.36
)
.

For any Seifert homology 3-sphere
Y
with a Seifert
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action,
there is a combinatorial algorithm to find a sequence of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures
\gamma=(\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n})
on a negative definite plumbing
W_{\Gamma}
of
Y
,
which carries the non-equivariant lattice homology of
Y
, such that there exists an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant stable map

\mathcal{T}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\colon\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\gamma)\longrightarrow SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y)

that is an
S^{1}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence. Here,
\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\gamma)
denotes the
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lattice homotopy type combinatorially defined from
\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}
, and
SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y)
is the metric-independent
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer spectrum of
Y
, defined in a manner similar to Montague’s spectrum, as described in
Section

3.1
.

Remark 1.3
.

Since our equivariant spectrum
SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y)
recovers the Baraglia–Hekmati equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer cohomology
[
baraglia2024brieskorn
]
, it follows from
1.2
that their cohomology can be computed from

\tilde{H}^{*}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\gamma)),

and hence the equivariant Frøyshov invariants introduced in
[
baraglia2024brieskorn
]
can be computed combinatorially.
In particular, we prove in
Section

4.9
that for any Seifert fibered rational homology sphere
Y
where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts as a subaction of the Seifert
S^{1}
-action and any self-conjugate
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
Y
, the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Frøyshov invariant of
(Y,\mathfrak{s})
is given by

\delta^{(p)}_{0}(Y,\mathfrak{s})=\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s})+\mathrm{dim}HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s}),

whenever
p
is a sufficiently large prime.

Remark 1.4
.

Note that in
1.2
, we may take
G=S^{1}
as the full symmetry group.
By developing
S^{1}\times G
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy types,
we expect that an
S^{1}\times S^{1}
-equivariant lattice homotopy type should exist without any essential modification.

Finally, a certain
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
refinement of
1.2
for chain models will be discussed in
5.34
, which provides a computation of the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant local equivalence class of the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant chain group of

\bigwedge_{4}SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\left(\Sigma(3,5,19)\right).

This computation is used in
Section

6.3
to conclude that there is no local map

C^{*}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(S^{0})\longrightarrow C^{*}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\bigwedge_{4}SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Sigma(3,5,19))\right)

of level
2
. By
6.6
, we therefore conclude that the boundary Dehn twist on
X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}
is
not
smoothly isotopic to the identity rel. boundary. On the other hand, since the contractible filling
X
has trivial homology, the boundary Dehn twist is topologically isotopic to the identity rel. boundary
[
Orson-Powell:2022-1
, Corollary C]
, which completes the proof.

Remark 1.5
.

As stated in
1.2
, we provide a metric-independent definition of the
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
–equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy type of a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
–equivariant Spin
c
rational homology
3
–sphere in
Section

3.1
.
Within this framework, for a knot
K\subset S^{3}
, a prime
p
, and an element
[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we define an orbifold version of the Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy type of
K
:

SWF_{\mathrm{ofd}}^{(p),[k]}(K),

which is realized as a certain fixed-point (
S^{1}
–equivariant pointed) spectrum obtained from the
p
th branched covering space along
K
. This is a genuine invariant of the knot
K
.
Moreover, for a properly embedded surface
S
equipped with suitable orbifold Spin
c
structures
\mathfrak{s}
, one obtains the corresponding surface cobordism maps; see
3.20
and
3.24
for details. This invariant may also be of independent interest.

Organization

The structure of the paper is as follows.

Section 2 collects background materials and unifies notations.
It reviews Némethi’s computation sequences and graded roots, as well as Montague’s equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer theory, which serve as the technical foundation of the paper. We also introduce
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-equivariant homotopy coherenet Bauer–Furuta invariant here.

In Section 3, we develop the framework of equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy types for equivariant Spin
c
3-manifolds and Bauer–Furuta theory for equivariant Spin
c
4-manifolds. Some gluing formula is also provided.

Section 4 defines the
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lattice homotopy type, and provides a combinatorial algorithm for Seifert fibered 3-manifolds, which allows for explicit computations of equivariant Frøyshov invariants.

In Section 5, we refine the construction to the chain level, developing the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lattice chain homotopy type.
We compute the local equivalence class of Montague’s spectrum, yielding algebraic obstructions that play a decisive role in our main application.

Section 6 is devoted to proving the main theorem, stating that odd iterates of the boundary Dehn twist on the Mazur manifold bounded by
\Sigma(3,5,19)
remain exotic after two stabilizations.
A key step is the “connected sum technique”, which exploits a difference between algebraic and topological aspects of the stabilization problem in order to get a stronger algebraic obstruction.

Finally, three appendices provide supporting materials:
Appendix A states Atiyah–Segal–Singer’s equivariant index theorem for manifolds with boundary,
Appendix B describes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-coefficient singular cochain dga of
B\mathrm{Pin}(2)
,
and Appendix C estimates the stable local triviality of Seifert homology spheres in a certain general setting.

2.
Background materials

2.1.
Notations

Throughout the paper, we unify the notation as follows:

•

All Seifert fibered rational homology spheres
Y
are oriented with the unique orientation satisfying the following property: the negative definite almost rational starshaped plumbing graph bounding
Y
gives a negative-definte cobordism from
\emptyset
to
Y
.

•

All tensor products of dgas, dg-modules,
A_{\infty}
-modules, bimodules, and
E_{\infty}
-modules are derived tensor products unless explicitly mentioned otherwise.

•

For a topological space
X
, we will sometimes identify its singular cochain complex
C^{\ast}(X)
with the
normalized
singular cochain complex, that is, the quotient of
C^{\ast}(X)
by the subcomplex of degenerate singular simplices (which is acyclic).

•

\zeta_{p}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{p}}\in\mathbb{C}
denotes the primitive
p
-th root of unity.

•

The geometric
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action on the 3-manifold
Y
is denoted by
\tau\colon Y\to Y
.

•

For a given Spin or
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
, we write
P(\mathfrak{s})
for the corresponding principal bundle. Denote by
S
the spinor bundle for
\mathfrak{s}
and
by
{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{A}:\Gamma(S^{+})\to\Gamma(S^{-})
the plus part of the 4-dimensional Dirac operator for a fixed
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
connection
A
and
S=S^{+}\oplus S^{-}
. The notation
{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B}:\Gamma(S)\to\Gamma(S)
denotes the 3-dimensional Dirac operator for a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
connection
B
.

•

\tilde{\tau}
denotes a lift of
\tau
to the principal or spinor bundle. A
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure with such a lift is written as
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
.

•

\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
denote the trivial and the standard representations of
S^{1}
, respectively.

•

\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}_{[i]}
denotes the representation of
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts by
\frac{i}{p}
-fold rotation and
j
acts by
-1
.

•

{\mathbb{C}}_{[i]}
denotes the representation of
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts by
\frac{i}{p}
-fold rotation and
S^{1}
acts in the standard way.

•

\mathbb{H}_{[i]}
denotes the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
representation where
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
acts via the quaternions and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts by
\frac{i}{p}
-fold rotation on each component
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{H}
. When
p=2
, we denote
\mathbb{H}_{[0]}
and
\mathbb{H}_{[1]}
by
\mathbb{H}_{+}
and
\mathbb{H}_{-}
, respectively.

•

For a compact Lie group
G
, we write
R(G)
and
RO(G)
for the complex and real representation rings of
G
. We also consider the quaternionic representation ring of
G
, denoted
RQ(G)
.

•

For a
G
-vector space
V
, we denote by
V^{+}
the
G
-sphere obtained as the one point compactification.

•

The subsets
RQ(G)_{\geq 0}
,
R(G)_{\geq 0}
, and
RO(G)_{\geq 0}
denote the classes represented by actual quaternionic, complex, and real
G
-representations, respectively.

•

The notation for equivariant cohomology is

H^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,\theta]&\text{if }p=2,\\
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,R,S]/(R^{2})&\text{if }p>2,\end{cases}

where
U
and
\theta
are the degree two and one elements coming from the generators of
H^{\ast}(BS^{1};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
H^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
respectively, and
R
and
S
are degree one and two elements generating
H^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.
In the case
p=2
, we sometimes write
\theta^{2}
as
S
. We shall also use

H^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,V]/(Q^{3}),

where
\deg V=4
and
\deg Q=1
.

•

The CW structure on
E\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
B\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is fixed as in
[
KPT24
]
, so that the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action on
E\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is cellular.

•

For a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}

4
-manifold
(X,\mathfrak{s})
with boundary
Y
, equipped with a Riemannian metric
g
which is a product metric
dt^{2}+g_{Y}
near the boundary and with a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
connection
A_{0}
that is flat near the boundary, we write the
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
Dirac index with Atiyah–Patodi–Singer boundary conditions as

\mathrm{ind}^{\mathrm{APS}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s},A_{0},g}\in\mathbb{Z}.

The topological part of the index is defined by

\mathrm{ind}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s}}:=\mathrm{ind}^{\mathrm{APS}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s},A_{0},g}-n(Y,A_{0}|_{Y},g,\mathfrak{s}|_{Y})\in\mathbb{Q},

which is independent of the choices of Riemannian metrics and connections, where
n(Y,A_{0}|_{Y},g,\mathfrak{s}|_{Y})
denotes Manolescu’s correction term introduced in
[
Man03
]
. In other words,

\mathrm{ind}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s}}=\frac{1}{8}\big(c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-\sigma(X)\big).

•

Suppose
X
has a smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action, preserving the connected components of
\partial X
. If such
(X,\mathfrak{s})
lifts to a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure and
A_{0}
and
g
are taken so that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant, we write the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
Dirac index with Atiyah–Patodi–Singer boundary conditions as

\mathrm{ind}^{\mathrm{APS}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s},A_{0},g}\in R(\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

For given element
[i]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, the trace index is written as

\mathrm{ind}^{\mathrm{APS}}_{[i]}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s},A_{0},g}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{[i]}\left(\mathrm{ind}^{\mathrm{APS}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s},A_{0},g}\right)\in\mathbb{C}.

In
Appendix

A
, we introduce their topological parts

\mathrm{ind}^{t}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s}}\in R(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\text{ and }\mathrm{ind}^{t}_{[i]}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{X,\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathbb{C}.

Since we will be doing stable equivariant homotopy theory throughout the paper, we will have to fix some universes that we will use, which are given as follows. Suppose
p
is an odd prime.
For
G=S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we take our universe to be

\mathcal{U}_{p}=\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{p-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}_{[i]}^{\infty}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{p-1}\mathbb{C}_{[i]}^{\infty}\right).

For
G=\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we take our universe to be

\mathcal{V}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\infty}\oplus\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{-}^{\infty}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{+}^{\infty}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{-}^{\infty}.

For any element
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we write the corresponding trace map as

\operatorname{Tr}_{h}\colon R(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\mathbb{Z}\left[\mathbb{C}_{[1]}\right]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}].

The augmentation maps on the
KO,K,KQ
theories, as well as the representation rings
RO,R,RQ
, are written as
\alpha_{\mathbb{R}},\alpha_{\mathbb{C}},\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}
respectively.
Note that a given element
[V]\in R(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is recovered from its traces by the formula

V=\sum_{l=0}^{p-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\operatorname{Tr}_{\zeta_{p}^{k}}(V)\cdot\zeta_{p}^{-kl}\right)\cdot\left[\mathbb{C}_{[l]}\right].

The augmentation map is itself a trace, namely

\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}(V)=\operatorname{Tr}_{1}(V)=\mathrm{dim}V.

2.2.
Computation sequences and graded roots

In this subsection, we will review the materials in
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
]
. For simplicity, we fix the following notations.

•

Let
\Gamma
be a negative definite, almost rational plumbing graph. Let
W_{\Gamma}
denote the associated plumbed 4-manifold, and assume that
Y=\partial W_{\Gamma}
is a rational homology sphere.

•

Let
D_{v}
denote the disk bundle associated to a node
v\in V(\Gamma)
, and let
S_{v}
be its zero-section.

•

Let
V(\Gamma)
be the set of nodes of
\Gamma
, and for each node
v\in V(\Gamma)
, denote its weight by
w(v)
.

•

Fix a “base node”
v_{0}\in V(\Gamma)
.

•

Identify
H_{2}(W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})
with
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
and
H^{2}(W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})
with
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})
, and regard
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
as a sublattice of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})
by mapping each node
v\in V(\Gamma)
to its dual
v^{*}
with respect to the intersection form on
W_{\Gamma}
, i.e.,
v^{*}(w)=v\cdot w
for all
w\in V(\Gamma)
.

•

Since the index of
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})
is
|H_{1}(\partial W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})|
, and hence finite, we canonically identify
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})
with a subgroup of
\mathbb{Q}V(\Gamma)
.

•

For
x=\sum_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\lambda_{v}v\in\mathbb{Q}V(\Gamma)
and
v\in V(\Gamma)
, denote the coefficient
\lambda_{v}
by
m_{v}(x)
.

•

We endow
\mathbb{Q}V(\Gamma)
with the partial order given by
x\leq y
if and only if
m_{v}(x)\leq m_{v}(y)
for all
v\in V(\Gamma)
.

We start by observing that, since
H^{2}(W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})
is free and hence has no 2-torsion, the first Chern class map

c_{1}\colon\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(W_{\Gamma})\longrightarrow H^{2}(W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})

is injective, and its image consists precisely of
characteristic elements
of
\Gamma
, i.e., elements
v\in H^{2}(W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})
satisfying
v(w)\equiv w\cdot w\pmod{2}
for all
w\in H_{2}(W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
. For any characteristic element
x
of
\Gamma
, we denote the corresponding
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
W_{\Gamma}
by
\mathrm{sp}(x)
.

Denote the set of characteristic elements by
\mathrm{Char}(\Gamma)
, which will be canonically identified with
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(W_{\Gamma})
. Note that it admits a transitive action of
2\cdot\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, every
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
\partial W_{\Gamma}
extends to some
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}_{W}
on
W_{\Gamma}
, and the first Chern classes of any two such extensions differ by an element of
2\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
. Thus, the following map is a bijection:

\tilde{c}_{1}\colon\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(\partial W_{\Gamma})\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}\mapsto[c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{X})]\bmod 2}\mathrm{Char}(\Gamma)/2\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)\quad(\cong H^{2}(\partial W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})).

The set
\mathrm{Char}(\Gamma)
contains a distinguished element
K
, called the
canonical class
, defined by

K(v)=-w(v)-2\qquad\text{for all }v\in V(\Gamma).

Hence, for any
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(\partial W_{\Gamma})
, we have a corresponding equivalence class
\tilde{c}_{1}(\mathfrak{s})\in\mathrm{Char}(\Gamma)/2\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
. Any representative
k
of this class can be written as

k=K+2l,\qquad l\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z}),

where
l
is unique modulo
2\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
.

In order to make a canonical choice of
l
, we consider the set

S_{\mathfrak{s}}=\{x\in\tilde{c}_{1}(\mathfrak{s})\mid x(v)\leq 0\text{ for all }v\in V(\Gamma)\}.

Notice that
S_{\mathfrak{s}}
inherits a partial order from
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})
; with respect to that partial ordering,
S_{\mathfrak{s}}
has a unique minimal element
l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}}

[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 5.4]
, which depends only on the given
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
\partial W_{\Gamma}
. Thus
\tilde{c}_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
has a canonical representative

k_{\mathfrak{s}}=K+2l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}}.

Using this representative, we define the
weight function

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}\colon\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
by

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x)=-\frac{k_{\mathfrak{s}}(x)+x\cdot x}{2}.

It is then straightforward to verify that the topological part of the index of the
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
Dirac operator
{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\mathrm{sp}(k_{\mathfrak{s}}+2x)}
for
(W_{\Gamma},\mathrm{sp}(k_{\mathfrak{s}}+2x))
, whose boundary is
(\partial W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s})
, is

\mathrm{ind}^{t}\,{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\mathrm{sp}(k_{\mathfrak{s}}+2x)}=-\frac{c_{1}(\mathrm{sp}(k_{\mathfrak{s}}+2x))^{2}-2\tilde{\chi}(W_{\Gamma})-3\sigma(W_{\Gamma})}{8}=-\frac{k_{\mathfrak{s}}^{2}+|V(\Gamma)|}{8}+\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x).

Now, for each integer
i\geq 0
, we consider elements of
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
whose coefficient at the base node
v_{0}
is exactly
i
. Define the following subset:

D_{i}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)\,\middle|\,m_{v_{0}}(x)=i,\;(x+l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}})(v)\leq 0\text{ for all }v\in V(\Gamma)\smallsetminus\{v_{0}\}\right\}\subset\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma).

Since
\Gamma
is negative definite, there exists a unique minimal element in
D_{i}
with respect to the partial ordering on
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)

[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 7.6]
, which we denote by
x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i)
. Moreover, the elements
x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i)
and
x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i+1)
can be connected by a
computation sequence
, which is a sequence

x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,0},x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,1},\dots,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,n_{i}}

in
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
defined as follows:

•

x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,0}=x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i)+v_{0}
.

•

Suppose that
x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,0},\dots,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,k}
have been defined. If
x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,k}=x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i+1)
, then the sequence terminates, and we set
n_{i}=k
.

•

Otherwise, there exists some
v\in V(\Gamma)\smallsetminus\{v_{0}\}
such that
(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,k}+l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}})(v)>0

[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 7.7]
. Choose such a vertex
v
and define
x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,k+1}=x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,k}+v
.

By concatenating the cycles
x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i)
for
i\geq 0
with the computation sequences connecting them, we obtain the following infinite sequence in
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
:

x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0),\,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,0},\,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,1},\,\dots,\,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,n_{0}-1},\,x_{\mathfrak{s}}(1),\,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{1,0},\,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{1,1},\,\dots,\,x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{1,n_{1}-1},\,x_{\mathfrak{s}}(2),\,\dots

Note that
x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,n_{i}}=x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i+1)
. Furthermore, since
\Gamma
is almost rational, we have

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,0})=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,1})=\cdots=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,n_{i}})

for each
i

[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 9.1]
. Finally, there exists an integer
N>0
such that

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(n+1))\geq\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(n))

for all
n>N

[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Theorem 9.3]
.

Remark 2.1
.

We note that the above sequence can also be regarded as a sequence of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures on
W_{\Gamma}
, which we describe as follows. For each
x\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
, let us write
\mathrm{sp}(k_{\mathfrak{s}}+2x)
as
\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x)
. Then the corresponding sequence of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures becomes

\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0)),\,\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,0}),\,\dots,\,\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,n_{0}-1}),\,\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(1)),\,\dots

We refer to this as the
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
computation sequence of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
. This sequence has the following properties:

•

\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,0})=\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i))+PD[S_{v_{0}}]
;

•

\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,j+1})=\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,j})+PD[S_{v}]
for some node
v\in V(\Gamma)\smallsetminus\{v_{0}\}
;

•

Each
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure in the sequence restricts to
\mathfrak{s}
on
\partial W_{\Gamma}
.

In fact, any two successive terms in the sequence differ only in the interior of the disk bundle associated to some node of
\Gamma
.

Remark 2.2
.

Recall that, for any
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
, its conjugate
\overline{\mathfrak{s}}
satisfies

c_{1}(\overline{\mathfrak{s}})=-c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}).

Since
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures on
W_{\Gamma}
are uniquely determined by their first Chern class, and

c_{1}(\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(-k_{\mathfrak{s}}-x))=k_{\mathfrak{s}}+2(-k_{\mathfrak{s}}-x)=-(k_{\mathfrak{s}}+2x)=-c_{1}(\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x)),

it follows that

\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(-k_{\mathfrak{s}}-x)=\overline{\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x)}

for any
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(\partial W_{\Gamma})
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
.

To convert this into a graded root, we first recall a (slightly modified) definition.

Definition 2.3
.

A
planar graded root
is an infinite tree
R=(V,E)
embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where each edge is mapped to a straight line segment. For each vertex
v\in V
, we denote its
y
-coordinate (as a point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
) by
\chi(v)
, called the
weight
of
v
. The following conditions are required:

•

The weight
\chi(v)
is an integer for all
v\in V
, and the resulting weight function
\chi\colon V\to\mathbb{Z}
is bounded below.

•

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the set
\chi^{-1}(n)
is finite, and contains exactly one element for all sufficiently large
n
.

•

For any edge connecting two vertices
v,w\in V
, we have
\chi(v)-\chi(w)=\pm 1
.

Two planar graded roots
(R,\chi)
and
(R^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
are said to be
equivalent
if one can be isotoped to the other in the horizontal direction, up to an overall vertical shift.

We also define simple angles of planar graded roots.

Definition 2.4
.

Given a planar graded root
(R,\chi)
and its vertices
v,v^{\prime},w
, where
v
and
v^{\prime}
are leaves of
R
, we say that
v
and
v^{\prime}
form an angle at
w
if the following conditions are satisfied.

•

On the unique (up to reparametrization) simple path
[v,w]
from
v
to
w
through edges of
R
, the
y
-coordinate is strictly increasing, and the same statement holds when replace
v
with
v^{\prime}
.

•

Near the vertex
w
, the path
[v,w]
is on the left of
[v^{\prime},w]
.

•

[v,w]\cap[v^{\prime},w]=\{w\}
.

We then say that
v
and
v^{\prime}
form a simple angle at
w
if there is no leaf
v^{\prime\prime}
of
R
such that the path
[v^{\prime\prime},w]
lies in the middle of
[v,w]
and
[v^{\prime},w]
. Then we define

\mathrm{Angle}(w)=\left\{(v,v^{\prime})\mid v,v^{\prime}\in V(\Gamma)\text{ for a simple angle at }w\right\}.

We call elements of
\mathrm{Angle}(w)
the
simple angles
of
(R,\chi)
at
w
; these are preserved under equivalences of planar graded roots. We also say that leaves
v,v^{\prime}
of
R

form a simple angle of weight
n
if they form a simple angle at some
w\in V
(in which case
w
is uniquely determined) and
\chi(w)=n
.

The following obvious lemma describes a quick and easy way to describe planar graded roots in terms of weights of their leaves and simple angles.

Lemma 2.5
.

Given a planar graded root
R
, its equivalence class is determined uniquely from the following data up to overall weight shift.

•

Weights of leaves of
R
;

•

Pairs of leaves of
R
which form a simple angle, and the weights of those angles.

We say that an infinite sequence
n_{0},n_{1},\dots
of integers is
eventually increasing
if there exists some integer
N>0
such that
n_{k+1}\geq n_{k}
for all
k>N
. Such sequences can be used to define planar graded roots in the following way.

Definition 2.6
.

Given an eventually increasing sequence
\mathbf{n}=(n_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of integers, we extract the following data:

•

Define

I_{0}=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\mid\mathbf{n}\text{ achieves a local minimum at }n_{i}\}.

Since
\mathbf{n}
is eventually increasing, the set
I_{0}
is finite.

•

Using
I_{0}
, define

I=\{i\in I_{0}\mid i+1\notin I_{0}\},

and write

I=\{i_{0},\dots,i_{m}\},\qquad 0\leq i_{0}<\cdots<i_{m}.

Note that for each
k=1,\dots,m
, we have
i_{k}\geq i_{k-1}+2
, and hence
\mathbb{Z}\cap(i_{k-1},i_{k})\neq\emptyset
.

•

For each
k=1,\dots,m
, choose an integer
j_{k}
such that

i_{k-1}<j_{k}<i_{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad n_{j_{k}}=\min_{i_{k-1}<j<i_{k}}n_{j}.

The
planar graded root
R_{\mathbf{n}}
associated to the sequence
\mathbf{n}
is defined as the equivalence class of a planar graded root uniquely determined by
2.5
, satisfying the following conditions:

•

The leaves of
R_{\mathbf{n}}
correspond to elements of
I
; the leaf corresponding to
i_{k}\in I_{0}
has weight
n_{i_{k}}
.

•

Two leaves of
R_{\mathbf{n}}
, corresponding to
i_{k},i_{s}\in I
, form a simple angle
(i_{k},i_{s})
if and only if
s=k+1
.

•

For each
k=1,\dots,m
, the leaves corresponding to
i_{k-1}
and
i_{k}
form a simple angle of weight
n_{j_{k}}
.

Recall that the infinite sequence

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0)),\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,1}),\dots,\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,n_{0}-1}),\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(1)),\dots

is an eventually increasing sequence of integers. Hence it induces a planar graded root
R_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}
, whose equivalence class depends only on
(\partial W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s})
, since it can be read off from the
lattice homology

\mathbb{H}^{+}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
, which is isomorphic to
HF^{+}(-\partial W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s})

[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Theorem 2.4.6]
. Since
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,0})=\cdots=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{i,n_{i}-1})=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i+1))
for all
i\geq 0
, it follows from
2.6
that
R_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}
is equivalent to
R_{\mathbf{n}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})}
, where
\mathbf{n}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
is the sequence
(\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i)))_{i\geq 0}
.

2.3.
Graded roots from
\Delta
-sequences

Let
Y
be a Seifert fibered rational homology sphere with

\chi_{\mathrm{orb}}(Y/S^{1})=e<0,

and denote its singular fibers by
\{(p_{l},q_{l})\}_{l=0}^{\nu}
. Then we can construct the corresponding plumbing graph
\Gamma
as follows: assuming that
0<q_{l}<p_{l}
for each
l
,
\Gamma
is given as the
\nu
-armed starshaped plumbing graph where the central node
v_{c}
has weight
e_{0}=e-\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\frac{q_{i}}{p_{i}}
and the
l
th arm is given by

-k^{l}_{1}
-k^{l}_{2}
-k^{l}_{3}
-k^{l}_{s_{l}}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

where
k^{l}_{1},\cdots,k^{l}_{s_{l}}
are uniquely determined positive integers satisfying
k^{l}_{1},\dots,k^{l}_{s_{l}}\geq 2
and

\frac{p_{l}}{q_{l}}=[k^{l}_{1},\dots,k^{l}_{s_{l}}]=k^{l}_{1}-\cfrac{1}{k^{l}_{2}-\cfrac{1}{\ddots-\cfrac{1}{k^{l}_{s_{l}}}}}.

Note that
e_{0}<\nu
and the Seifert relation is given by

e_{0}+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\frac{q_{l}}{p_{l}}=-\frac{|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|}{p_{1}\cdots p_{l}}.

We denote by
v^{l}_{i}
the node on the
l
th arm whose weight is
-k^{l}_{i}
, that is, the
i
th node from the central node.

The resulting plumbing graph
\Gamma
is negative definite and almost rational; note that
Y\cong\partial W_{\Gamma}
. Furthermore, if we consider the set

SI_{red}(Y)=\left\{(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{\nu})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu+1}\,\middle|\,a_{0}\geq 0,\,0\leq a_{i}<p_{i},\,1+a_{0}+ie_{0}+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\left\lfloor\frac{iq_{l}+a_{l}}{p_{l}}\right\rfloor\leq 0\text{ for }i=1,\dots,\nu\right\},

then there exists a bijective correspondence between
SI_{red}(Y)
and
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(Y)

[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Corollary 11.7]
, which is constructed in the following way. Given
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(Y)
, the corresponding element
(a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0},a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\dots,a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\nu})\in SI_{red}(Y)
is determined as follows
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Proposition 11.5]
.

•

We have
a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}=-l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}}(v_{c})
.

•

For each
l=1,\dots,\nu
and any
i=1,\dots,s_{l}
, define
n^{l}_{i}
and
d^{l}_{i}
to be the unique coprime positive integers satisfying

\frac{n^{l}_{i}}{d^{l}_{i}}=[k^{l}_{i},\dots,k^{l}_{s_{l}}].

Then, for each
l=1,\dots,\nu
, we have

a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{l}=-l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}}(v_{s_{l}}^{l})-\sum_{t=1}^{s_{l}-1}n^{l}_{t+1}\,l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}}(v^{l}_{t}).

Remark 2.7
.

The zero vector
(0,\dots,0)
is always contained in
SI_{\mathrm{red}}(Y)
.
If we denote by
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}
the corresponding
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
, then it follows from
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Proposition 11.6]
that
l^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}=0
, and thus
k_{\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}=K
.
In other words,
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}
is the restriction to the boundary of the
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
W_{\Gamma}
whose first Chern class is the canonical class
K
.
Hence, we call
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}
the
canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
of
Y
.
We note that
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}
is self-conjugate if and only if
K\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
, which is equivalent to
m_{v}(K)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
v\in V(\Gamma)
.

Choose any
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
Y
, and write the corresponding element of
SI_{red}(Y)
as
(a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0},a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\dots,a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\nu})
. To compute the planar graded root
R_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}
(which depends only on
Y
and
\mathfrak{s}
), it suffices to compute the planar graded root associated to the sequence
(\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i)))_{i\geq 0}
, which additionally depends on the choice of a base node
v_{0}
of
\Gamma
. For simplicity, it is natural to take the base node to be the central node, i.e.,
v_{0}=v_{c}
.

It is clear that
x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0)=0
, and thus
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0))=0
. After that, we consider the
\Delta
-sequence for
(Y,\mathfrak{s})
, defined as follows:

\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}}(i)=1+a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}-e_{0}i+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\left\lfloor\frac{-iq_{l}+a^{\mathfrak{s}}_{l}}{p_{l}}\right\rfloor\quad\text{for each}\quad i\geq 0.

Then, for any integer
i\geq 0
, the following equation holds
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Section 11.12]
:

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i+1))-\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i))=\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}}(i).

This gives a completely combinatorial way to compute the planar graded root
R_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}
, which recovers
HF^{+}(-Y,\mathfrak{s})
.

Remark 2.8
.

The
\Delta
-sequence for
(Y,\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y})
can be computed in a much simpler way, since, as noted in
2.7
, the corresponding element of
SI_{\mathrm{red}}(Y)
is the zero vector. Indeed, for each integer
i\geq 0
, the formula for
\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}}(i)
simplifies to:

\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}(i)=1-e_{0}i-\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\left\lceil\frac{iq_{l}}{p_{l}}\right\rceil.

Using the inequality
\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}\right\rceil\leq\frac{q+p-1}{p}
for integers
p,q
with
p>0
, we obtain the following lower bound:

\begin{split}\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}(i)&\geq 1-i\left(e_{0}+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\frac{q_{l}}{p_{l}}\right)-\nu+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\frac{1}{p_{l}}\\
&=1-\nu+\frac{|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|\cdot i+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}p_{1}\cdots\widehat{p}_{l}\cdots p_{\nu}}{p_{1}\cdots p_{\nu}}.\end{split}

Hence, if we define the number

N_{Y}=\frac{(\nu-2)p_{1}\cdots p_{\nu}-\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}p_{1}\cdots\widehat{p}_{l}\cdots p_{\nu}}{|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|},

which is an integer if
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}
is self-conjugate (as will be shown in
5.3
), then we obtain
\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}(i)>-1
, and hence
\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}(i)\geq 0
, for all integers
i>N_{Y}
. Consequently, the sequence
(\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}}(i)))_{i\geq 0}
is increasing for
i>N_{Y}
.

2.4.
Montague’s equivariant spectrum class

We review the construction of
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy types and interpret them as isomorphism classes of objects in a suitable category. Our approach primarily follows Montague’s formulation
[
montague2022seiberg
]
of equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy types.
We begin by formulating the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy type. We just briefly review her theory. For the precise arguments, see
[
montague2022seiberg
]
.

Let
Y
be a rational homology 3-sphere equipped with a smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action
\tau\colon Y\to Y
and a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant Spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
.

Definition 2.9
.

We say that
(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tau)
is
even
(respectively,
odd
) if a lift
\tilde{\tau}
of
\tau
to the principal
\mathrm{Spin}(3)
-bundle
P(\mathfrak{s})
has order
p
(respectively, order
2p
). In this paper, we assume that
(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tau)
is even.

A sufficient condition for obtaining even Spin structures is as follows:

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
Y
be an oriented
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homology 3-sphere. Then, for any free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action
\tau\colon Y\to Y
, the unique Spin structure on
Y
is even.

Proof.

For a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action
\tau\colon Y\to Y
, the structure is even if and only if the corresponding Spin structure arises as the pull-back of a Spin structure on
Y/\tau
. Since
Y
admits a unique Spin structure, it must be the pull-back of any Spin structure on
Y/\tau
. This completes the proof.
∎

Note that Montague
[
montague2022seiberg
]
treated both of even and odd spin structures, including both free and non-free group actions. In this paper, we focus on even free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Spin structures on rational homology 3-spheres.
We fix a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant Riemannian metric
g
, a lift of the action to the Spin bundle, and a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant Spin connection
B_{0}
.
This data yields an action of
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
on the global Coulomb slice

V=(B_{0}+i\ker d^{*})\oplus\Gamma(S)\subset i\Omega^{1}(Y)\oplus\Gamma(S),

along with a formally self-adjoint elliptic operator

l\colon V\longrightarrow V;\qquad(a,\phi)\longmapsto(*da,{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}\phi),

where
d^{*}
is the
L^{2}
-formal adjoint of
d
,
S
is the spinor bundle associated to
\mathfrak{s}
,
*
is the Hodge star operator with respect to the metric
g
, and
{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}
is the Spin Dirac operator associated to the Spin connection
B_{0}
.
As usual, we take a finite-dimensional approximation
V^{\mu}_{\lambda}(g)
, defined as the direct sum of all eigenspaces of
l
with eigenvalues in the range
(\lambda,\mu]
. This space also carries a natural
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action.

By applying a finite-dimensional approximation of the Seiberg–Witten equations, we obtain a
G_{\mathfrak{s}}
-equivariant Conley index
I^{\mu}_{\lambda}(g)
for sufficiently large real numbers
\mu
and
-\lambda
, where
G_{\mathfrak{s}}
denotes the group of unitary automorphisms
u\colon S\to S
that preserve
A_{0}
and lift the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action on
Y
.
We then define a metric-dependent equivariant Floer homotopy type as

SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g)=\Sigma^{-V^{0}_{\lambda}(g)}I^{\mu}_{\lambda}(g),

where
V^{0}_{\lambda}(g)
is regarded as a
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-representation space, and the desuspension is taken in a suitable category to be defined later. This homotopy type depends on the choice of Riemannian metric and is referred to as the
metric-dependent
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy type of
(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g)
.

To eliminate this metric dependence, Montague
[
montague2022seiberg
]
introduced
equivariant correction terms
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, the
k
-th
equivariant correction term

n(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g)_{k}\in\mathbb{C}

is defined by

n(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g)_{k}=\begin{cases}\overline{\eta}_{\mathfrak{s},g}-\frac{1}{8}\eta_{\mathrm{sign},g},&\text{if }k=0,\\
\overline{\eta}^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g},&\text{if }k\neq 0,\end{cases}

where:

•

The equivariant eta invariant
\eta^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g}
of
{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}
is defined as the value at
s=0
of the meromorphic continuation of

\eta^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g}(s)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\neq 0\\
\lambda\text{ eigenvalue of }{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\operatorname{sign}(\lambda)\cdot\operatorname{Trace}\big((\tilde{\tau}^{k})^{*}\colon V_{\lambda}\to V_{\lambda}\big)}{|\lambda|^{s}}\in\mathbb{C},\quad s\in\mathbb{C},

where
V_{\lambda}
is the eigenspace of
{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}
corresponding to
\lambda
.
Also, we put
\eta_{\mathfrak{s},g}:=\eta^{0}_{\mathfrak{s},g}
which coincides with the non-equivariant eta invariant of
{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}
.

•

The quantity
\eta_{\mathrm{sign},g}
is the non-equivariant eta invariant for the signature operator

D_{\mathrm{sign}}:=\begin{pmatrix}*d&-d\\
-d^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}:\Omega^{1}_{Y}\oplus\Omega^{0}_{Y}\to\Omega^{1}_{Y}\oplus\Omega^{0}_{Y}.

•

The reduced equivariant eta invariant
\overline{\eta}^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g}
is

\overline{\eta}^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g}-c^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g}\right),

where
c^{k}_{\mathfrak{s},g}=\operatorname{Trace}\big((\tilde{\tau}^{k})^{*}\colon\ker{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}\to\ker{\partial\mkern-9.5mu/}_{B_{0}}\big)\in\mathbb{C}
. Similarly, we put

\overline{\eta}_{\mathfrak{s},g}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta^{0}_{\mathfrak{s},g}-c^{0}_{\mathfrak{s},g}\right).

Definition 2.11
.

The
equivariant correction term
is defined by

\mathbf{n}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g):=\sum_{l=0}^{p-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}}n(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g)_{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}^{-kl}\right)\otimes[\mathbb{H}_{[l]}]\in RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{C},

where
\zeta_{p}
is a fixed primitive
p
-th root of unity.
2
2

2

We use the identification
RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{p}]
; note that the scalar extension map
-\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H}\colon R(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is an isomorphism. We denote by
\mathbb{H}_{[l]}
the
1
-dimensional quaternionic representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
corresponding to the element
[l]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Montague observed that
\mathbf{n}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g)
actually lies in
RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and established its relation to equivariant spectral flows.

Next, we introduce the stable homotopy category defined by Montague.

Definition 2.12
.

A pointed
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
–equivariant CW complex
X
is called a
space of type
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
–SWF
if
X^{S^{1}}
is
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariantly homotopy equivalent to
V^{+}
for some
V\in RO(\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{\geq 0}
. Here,
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
acts on
V
via the composition

\mathrm{Pin}(2)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pin}(2)/S^{1}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\{\pm 1\}\;\xhookrightarrow{\;\;\;}\;GL(V).

We now define the category of spaces of type SWF.

Definition 2.13
.

We define the category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
as follows:

•

The objects are triples
(X,a,b)
, where
X
is a space of type
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
–SWF,
a\in RO(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, and
b\in RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

•

Given two objects
(X,a,b)
and
(X^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
, the morphism set between them is defined by

\mathrm{Mor}((X,a,b),(X^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))=\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}=a-a^{\prime}\\
\beta-\beta^{\prime}=b-b^{\prime}\end{subarray}}[X\wedge\alpha^{+}\wedge\beta^{+},\;X^{\prime}\wedge(\alpha^{\prime})^{+}\wedge(\beta^{\prime})^{+}]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\right)\big/\sim,

where:

–

\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
are finite-rank real
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-representations in which
S^{1}\subset\mathrm{Pin}(2)
acts trivially and
j\in\mathrm{Pin}(2)
acts by
-1
;

–

\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{\geq 0}
;

–

The equivalence relation
\sim
is defined as follows: two morphisms
[f]
and
[g]
with representatives

\begin{split}f\colon&X\wedge\alpha_{1}^{+}\wedge\beta_{1}^{+}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}\wedge(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{+}\wedge(\beta_{1}^{\prime})^{+},\\
g\colon&X\wedge\alpha_{2}^{+}\wedge\beta_{2}^{+}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}\wedge(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{+}\wedge(\beta_{2}^{\prime})^{+}\end{split}

are identified, i.e.
f\sim g
, if there exist finite-rank real representations
a,a^{\prime}
and complex representations
b,b^{\prime}
such that

\alpha_{1}\oplus a\cong\alpha_{2}\oplus a^{\prime},\quad\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\oplus a\cong\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\oplus a^{\prime},\quad\beta_{1}\oplus b\cong\beta_{2}\oplus b^{\prime},\quad\beta_{1}^{\prime}\oplus b\cong\beta_{2}^{\prime}\oplus b^{\prime},

and the maps

f\wedge\mathrm{id}_{a^{+}\wedge b^{+}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g\wedge\mathrm{id}_{(a^{\prime})^{+}\wedge(b^{\prime})^{+}}

are
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariantly homotopic.

The smash product
-\wedge-
, defined by

(X,a,b)\wedge(X^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime}):=(X\wedge X^{\prime},\,a\oplus a^{\prime},\,b\oplus b^{\prime}),

endows the category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
with the structure of a symmetric monoidal category. Moreover, there is a suspension operation on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. Given an object
(X,a,b)
and elements
a^{\prime}\in RO(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
b^{\prime}\in RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, we define the suspension by

\Sigma^{a^{\prime}\oplus b^{\prime}}(X,a,b):=(X,\,a\oplus a^{\prime},\,b\oplus b^{\prime}).

We now define local maps and local equivalence for objects in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
(X,a,b)
and
(X^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
be objects in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
.
A morphism
f
between them, represented by a
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant map

f\colon X\wedge\alpha^{+}\wedge\beta^{+}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}\wedge(\alpha^{\prime})^{+}\wedge(\beta^{\prime})^{+},

is called a
local map of order
0
if its fixed-point map

f^{S^{1}}\colon X^{S^{1}}\wedge\alpha^{+}\wedge\beta^{+}\longrightarrow(X^{\prime})^{S^{1}}\wedge(\alpha^{\prime})^{+}\wedge(\beta^{\prime})^{+}

is a non-equivariant homotopy equivalence.
We say that
(X,a,b)
and
(X^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
are
locally equivalent
if there exist local maps of order
0
between them in both directions.

Remark 2.15
.

In our definition of local maps, we do not require the fixed-point maps to be
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant homotopy equivalences. This differs from Montague’s original formulation
[
montague2022seiberg
]
, in which
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariance is imposed on the homotopy equivalence.

We can now define the space-level local equivalence group.

Definition 2.16
.

We set

\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}:=\frac{\left\{\text{isomorphism classes of objects in }\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\right\}}{\text{local equivalence}},

where the group operation is induced by the smash product
-\wedge-
.
Since the smash product endows
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
with a symmetric monoidal structure, the set
\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is a well-defined abelian group.

Note that we can make sense of the functor

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^{\mathrm{op}}_{C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})}

as follows:

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}((X,a,b);\mathbb{Z}_{p}):=\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})[\alpha_{\mathbb{R}}(a)+4\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}(b)].

Here,
\alpha
denotes the augmentation map (extended
\mathbb{Q}
-linearly if necessary) defined on

RO(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
RQ(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

We are now ready to define the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant spectrum class.

Definition 2.17
.

We define

SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau}):=\big[(SWF(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g),\,0,\,\mathbf{n}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g))\big]

as an isomorphism class in the category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
.
If
Y
is a disjoint union of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant even Spin rational homology
3
-spheres

Y=\bigsqcup_{1\leq i\leq n}(Y_{i},\mathfrak{s}_{i},\tilde{\tau}_{i}),

we set

SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau}):=\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y_{i},\mathfrak{s}_{i},\tilde{\tau}_{i}).

Since the invariants
\mathbf{n}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g)
capture equivariant versions of spectral flows, Montague used this to prove the following invariance:

Theorem 2.18

(
[
montague2022seiberg
]
)
.

The spectrum class
SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau})
is independent of the choice of a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant Riemannian metric and a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant finite-dimensional approximation.

Remark 2.19
.

Montague also treated the non-free case, which requires certain modifications to the correction terms. Since we will consider a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
version in the non-free setting, we will revisit this construction later.

We have a chain complex

\tilde{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\big(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau})\big):=\tilde{C}^{*+\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{n}(Y,\mathfrak{s},g))}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\big(SWF(Y,\mathfrak{s},\tilde{\tau},g);\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big),

whose chain homotopy type over the differential graded algebra
C^{*}(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is independent of the choice of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant Riemannian metric.
Note, however, that the chain homotopy type of the module
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF(Y,\mathfrak{s}))
does depend on the choice of Spin lift.

2.5.
Homotopy coherent Bauer–Furuta invariants

We shall also need a certain cobordism map in the context of homotopy coherent group actions.
To state homotopy coherent bordism maps, we factor through the Borel functor.
In this subsection, we construct the monoidal functor

\mathcal{B}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}},

which can be regarded as a stable version of the Borel construction.
This functor provides a comparison between equivariant Seiberg–Witten theory and families Seiberg–Witten theory via the Borel construction.
For closed
4
-manifolds, a similar perspective was developed by Baraglia
[
baraglia2024equivariant
]
; see also
[
KPT24
]
for a construction of the homotopy coherent Bauer–Furuta invariants.

2.5.1.
Families categories

Definition 2.20
.

Let
X
and
B
be Hausdorff topological spaces with
B
compact, and let

p\colon X\to B
be a fibration with a section
s\colon B\to X
.
Suppose
X
is equipped with a continuous
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-action such that both
p
and
s
are
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-equivariant (where
B
carries the trivial action).
We say that
(X,p,s)
is a
space of family
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
–SWF over the base
B
if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

The fibers of
p
are homotopy equivalent to finite CW complexes;

•

The map
X^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\to B
, obtained by restricting
p
to
X^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
, is a fibration whose fibers are homotopy equivalent to
S^{0}
;

•

The map
X^{S^{1}}\to B
, obtained by restricting
p
to
X^{S^{1}}
, is a fibration that is (parametrically over
B
) homotopy equivalent to the fiberwise one-point compactification of some finite-rank
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-vector bundle over
B
;

•

The action of
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
on
X\smallsetminus X^{S^{1}}
is free.

Given two spaces
\mathcal{X}=(X,p_{X},s_{X})
and
\mathcal{Y}=(Y,p_{Y},s_{Y})
of family
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
–SWF over a common base
B
, we define their product

\mathcal{X}\wedge_{B}\mathcal{Y}

to be the fiberwise smash product
X\wedge_{B}Y
, equipped with the natural maps
p_{X\wedge_{B}Y}
and
s_{X\wedge_{B}Y}
induced by
p_{X}
,
p_{Y}
, and
s_{X}
,
s_{Y}
, respectively.
It is straightforward to verify that
\mathcal{X}\wedge_{B}\mathcal{Y}
is again a space of family
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
–SWF over
B
.
Furthermore, for any finite-rank
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-vector bundle
E
over
B
, its fiberwise one-point compactification
E^{+}
also defines a space family
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
–SWF.

We also introduce the following terminology: a real
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-vector bundle over a compact base
B
is called
admissible
if its fibers, regarded as real
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-representations, are contained in the universe
\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\infty}
.
All real
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-vector bundles in this section are assumed to be admissible.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
B
be a compact Hausdorff space.
We define the category
\mathcal{F}^{B}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
as follows:

•

Objects are pairs
(X,r)
, where
X=(X,p_{X},s_{X})
is a space of family
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
–SWF over the base
B
, and
r\in\mathbb{Q}
.

•

The morphism set between
(X,r)
and
(Y,s)
is

\mathrm{Mor}((X,r),(Y,s)):=\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}E,F\ \mathrm{admissible}\\
\mathrm{rank}(E)-\mathrm{rank}(F)=r-s\end{subarray}}[X\wedge_{B}E^{+},\,Y\wedge_{B}F^{+}]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}_{B}\right)\big/\sim,

where:

–

For spaces
S,T
of family
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
–SWF over
B
, the set
[S,T]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}_{B}
consists of
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-equivariant maps
f\colon S\to T
satisfying
p_{T}\circ f=p_{S}
and
s_{T}=f\circ s_{S}
;

–

Given two elements

f\in[X\wedge_{B}E_{1}^{+},\,Y\wedge_{B}E_{2}^{+}]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}_{B},\quad g\in[X\wedge_{B}F_{1}^{+},\,Y\wedge_{B}F_{2}^{+}]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}_{B},

we declare
f\sim g
if there exist admissible
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-vector bundles
E,F
over
B
such that

E_{1}\oplus E\cong F_{1}\oplus F,\qquad E_{2}\oplus E\cong F_{2}\oplus F,

and, under these identifications, the maps

f\wedge\mathrm{id}_{E}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad g\wedge\mathrm{id}_{F}

are homotopic through maps in

[X\wedge_{B}(E_{1}\oplus E)^{+},\,Y\wedge_{B}(F_{1}\oplus F)^{+}]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}_{B}.

Then the following properties are immediate:

•

For any finite-rank
\mathbb{H}
-vector bundle
E
over
B
and any
r\in\mathbb{Q}
, the pair
(E^{+},r)
is an object of
\mathcal{F}^{B}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
.

•

The fiberwise smash product
-\wedge_{B}-
endows
\mathcal{F}^{B}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
with a symmetric monoidal structure.

•

For any compact Hausdorff space
B
and closed subspace
B_{0}\subset B
, there is a restriction functor

\mathrm{res}_{B,B_{0}}\colon\mathcal{F}^{B}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{B_{0}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)},

which is monoidal with respect to the fiberwise smash product.

Now we define the families categories that will be used throughout the paper.

Definition 2.22
.

Fix a CW complex structure on
B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as in
[
KPT24
]
.
This yields a sequence of restriction functors

\cdots\;\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{2},(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{1}}}\;\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{1}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\;\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{1},(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{0}}}\;\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{0}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}.

We define the category
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
as the inverse homotopy limit of this diagram:

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}:=\mathrm{holim}\left[\cdots\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{1}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{0}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\right]

It is easy to check that this homotopy limit exists. Since all the restriction functors involved are monoidal, the fiberwise smash product
-\wedge_{B}-
induces a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

Remark 2.23
.

In this paper, it is sufficient to use the category
\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
for a sufficiently large
n
as it is done in
[
KPT24
]
. However, just for the simplicity of notations, we consider the limit.

Observe that, for each integer
n\geq 0
, we have a functor

\mathcal{B}_{n}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)};\qquad(X,a,b)\longmapsto\left([X\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(E\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}\to(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}],\ \mathrm{rank}(a)+4\,\mathrm{rank}(b)\right).

Since we have commutative diagrams

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}_{n-1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{n-1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n-1}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}}

for all integers
n>0
, we can take their inverse limit.

Definition 2.24
.

We define the limit functor
\mathcal{B}
, referred to as the
Borel construction functor
, by

\mathcal{B}:=\mathrm{holim}\,\mathcal{B}_{n}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.

We can also make sense of “taking the cochain complex” for objects in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
as follows.
Given an object
X
, observe that it is specified by a sequence
\{(X_{n},r)\}_{n\geq 0}
, where each
(X_{n},r)
is an object of
\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
and satisfies the compatibility condition

\mathrm{res}_{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n},(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n-1}}\big((X_{n},r)\big)=(X_{n-1},r).

Then we have the following commutative diagram of
E_{\infty}
-algebras over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
3
3

3

For any space
X
, its normalized singular cochain complex
C^{\ast}(X;k)
admits a natural structure of an
E_{\infty}
-algebra over
k
for any commutative coefficient ring
k
; see
[
mcclure2003multivariable
]
for a detailed explanation.
:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{n-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}

Hence, we obtain a well-defined morphism

C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\mathrm{holim}_{n}\,C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\mathrm{holim}_{n}\,C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

so that
\mathrm{holim}_{n}\,C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
naturally acquires the structure of a module over
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

Thus, we define the functor

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\colon\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^{\mathrm{op}}_{C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})},

where on objects, we set

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}):=\mathrm{holim}_{n}\,C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[r].

The definition on morphisms can be carried out similarly using Thom quasi-isomorphisms; we omit the details. The following properties are then immediate:

•

The functors
\mathcal{B}
and
C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
are monoidal.

•

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
s\in\mathbb{Q}
, the suspension operation
\Sigma^{s}
on
\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
defined by

\Sigma^{s}(X,r)=(X,r+s)

induces a well-defined endofunctor
\Sigma^{s}
on
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

•

The following diagram of categories and functors is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-45.52458pt\,C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Mod}^{\mathrm{op}}_{C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})}}

We now define the notion of families local maps.

Definition 2.25
.

Given a compact Hausdorff space
B
and two objects
(X,r)
and
(Y,s)
of
\mathcal{F}^{B}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
, we say that a morphism
[f]\in\mathrm{Mor}((X,r),(Y,s))
is
local
if, for some (or equivalently, any) representative

f\colon X\wedge_{B}E^{+}\longrightarrow Y\wedge_{B}F^{+}

of
[f]
, the following hold:

•

There exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector bundle
F_{0}
and its
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector sub-bundle
E_{0}
such that the fibers of
F_{0}/E_{0}
are given by
\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{rank}(F_{0})-\mathrm{rank}(E_{0})}
, where
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
acts through
\mathrm{Pin}(2)/S^{1}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

•

There exist maps

g_{E}\in[E_{0}^{+},(X\wedge E^{+})^{S^{1}}]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}_{B},\qquad g_{F}\in[F_{0}^{+},(Y\wedge F^{+})^{S^{1}}]^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}_{B},

such that
g_{E}
and
g_{F}
are fiberwise homotopy equivalences.

•

The following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{E_{0}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
inclusion
\scriptstyle{g_{E}}
\textstyle{F_{0}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{F}}
\textstyle{(X\wedge E^{+})^{S^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{S^{1}}}
\textstyle{(Y\wedge F^{+})^{S^{1}}}

If we denote
\mathrm{rank}(F_{0})-\mathrm{rank}(E_{0})
by
k
, we say that
f
is
local of level
k
.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
f\colon(X,r)\to(Y,s)
be a morphism in
\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
; note that it corresponds to a sequence of morphisms
\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, where
f_{n}\colon(X_{n},r)\to(Y_{n},s)
is a morphism in
\mathcal{F}^{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}
. We say that
f
is a
local map of level
k
if each
f_{n}
is a local map of level
k
. Moreover, two objects are
locally equivalent
if there exist local maps of level 0 between them in both directions.

Remark 2.27
.

It is immediate that every isomorphism in
\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is a local map of level
0
.

Finally, we define the families local equivalence group.

Definition 2.28
.

The
families local equivalence group

\mathfrak{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is defined by

\mathfrak{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=\frac{\left\{\text{isomorphism classes of objects in }\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\right\}}{\text{local equivalence}}.

The group operation is given by

[(X,r)]+[(Y,s)]:=[(X\wedge_{B}Y,\,r+s)].

Since
-\wedge_{B}-
endows
\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
with a symmetric monoidal structure, this operation makes
\mathfrak{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
into a well-defined abelian group.

We also define the notion of stable local triviality.

Definition 2.29
.

Given an integer
k\geq 0
, we say that an element
[X]\in\mathfrak{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is
k
-stably locally trivial
if, for some (or equivalently, any) object
X
of
\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
representing the given local equivalence class
[X]
, there exist local maps of level
k
between
(X,-k)
and
\mathfrak{B}(S^{0},0,0)
in both directions.

Since the Borel functor
\mathfrak{B}
clearly sends local maps of level 0 in
\mathcal{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
to local maps of level 0 in
\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, it induces a group homomorphism

\mathfrak{B}\colon\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.

Definition 2.30
.

We define the image of
\mathfrak{B}
to be the
strict families local equivalence group
, denoted

\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.

2.5.2.
Homotopy coherent bordism maps

Fix a compact spin
4
-manifold
X
with possibly disconnected boundary denoted by
Y
.
Let
\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X)
be the group of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of
X
.
Suppose we have a
homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
on
X
, that is, a continuous map

\displaystyle B\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow B\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X).

Assume that each connected component of
Y=\partial X
has
b_{1}=0
.

Definition 2.31
.

Given a homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action,
a
spin homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
is a lift to a family of spin structures

\textstyle{B\mathrm{Aut}(X;\mathfrak{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X,[\mathfrak{s}])}

where
\mathrm{Aut}(X;\mathfrak{s})
denotes the group of automorphisms of the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
.

Note that a spin homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action induces a family of spin
4
-manifolds

(X,\mathfrak{s})\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow B\mathbb{Z}_{2}

parametrized over
B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as the pullback of the universal bundle.

Definition 2.32
.

We say that a spin homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action is
strict on the boundary
if the induced boundary family

(\partial X=Y,\mathfrak{t}:=\mathfrak{s}|_{Y})\longrightarrow E^{\partial}\longrightarrow B\mathbb{Z}_{2}

is obtained as the Borel construction of an even
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant spin structure on
(Y,\mathfrak{t})
; that is, there exists a smooth involution
\tau\colon Y\to Y
of order
2
together with a lift

\tilde{\tau}\colon P(\mathfrak{t})\longrightarrow P(\mathfrak{t})

covering
\tau
and satisfying
\tilde{\tau}^{2}=\mathrm{id}
, such that

E^{\partial}\cong P(\mathfrak{t})\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}E\mathbb{Z}_{2},

where
P(\mathfrak{t})
denotes the principal spin bundle of
\mathfrak{t}
.

Thus, by truncating the family obtained from the homotopy coherent action, we obtain a family over the
n
-skeleton
(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}
:

X\longrightarrow E_{n}\longrightarrow(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}.

We apply the families Bauer–Furuta invariants to this truncation. In order to describe these invariants, we introduce the following two virtual bundles:

\displaystyle H^{+}_{E_{n}}\in KO\left((B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}_{f}^{t}\left({\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{E_{n},\{g_{b}\}}\right)\in KQ\left((B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}\right),

satisfying the compatibilities

r_{n}\left(H^{+}_{E_{n}}\right)=H^{+}_{E_{n-1}},\qquad r_{n}\left(\mathrm{ind}_{f}^{t}\left({\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{E_{n},\{g_{b}\}}\right)\right)=\mathrm{ind}_{f}^{t}\left({\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{E_{n-1},\{g_{b}\}}\right),

where
r_{n}
denotes the restriction map in
KO
- or
KQ
-theory.
Here, for a topological space
X
,
KQ(X)
denotes the Grothendieck group of the semigroup of isomorphism classes of quaternionic vector bundles over
X
under direct sum, called quaternionic
K
-cohomology.

The above two invariants are defined as follows.

Definition 2.33
.

Given a fiber bundle

X\longrightarrow E_{n}\longrightarrow(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n},

consider its principal
\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X)
-bundle

\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X)\longrightarrow P_{n}\longrightarrow(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}.

The group
\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X)
acts on the space
\operatorname{Gr}\left(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{R})\right)
of maximally positive-definite subspaces of
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{R})
, which is known to be contractible. Therefore, one can choose a section

(1)

\displaystyle s\colon(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}\longrightarrow P_{n}\times_{\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X)}\operatorname{Gr}\left(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{R})\right),

unique up to homotopy.
This section
s
determines a real vector bundle

H^{+}(X;\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow H^{+}_{E_{n}}\longrightarrow(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n},

which defines
H^{+}_{E_{n}}\in KO\left((B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}\right)
.

Next, we introduce the class

\mathrm{ind}_{f}^{t}\left({\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{E_{n},\{g_{b}\}}\right)\in KQ\left((B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}\right).

Definition 2.34
.

For the family of spin
4
-manifolds

(X,\mathfrak{s})\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow B\mathbb{Z}_{2}

obtained from a spin homotopy coherent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action whose boundary family is strict, we say that a fiberwise Riemannian metric
g_{b}
parametrized by
b\in B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is
admissible
if the following two conditions are satisfied:

•

For each
b\in B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, near
\partial E_{b}
,
g_{b}
is the product metric

\displaystyle g_{Y}+dt^{2},

where
t
denotes the normal coordinate to the boundary
Y
.

•

The family metric
g_{b}
on
E^{\partial}
, obtained as
g_{Y}
appearing as above, coincides with the family of Riemannian metrics on
E^{\partial}
coming from the Borel construction of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Riemannian metric
g_{Y}
on
Y
.

We note that the space of fiberwise admissible metrics on a fixed bundle
E
is non-empty and contractible.

Let us fix an admissible metric
\{g_{b}\}
for the family
E
and an integer
k\geq 3
.
We consider the family of spin Dirac operators with respect to
\{g_{b}\}
and with the fiberwise APS boundary condition:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(g_{b})\colon L^{2}_{k}\left(S^{+}_{E}\right)\longrightarrow L^{2}_{k-1}\left(S^{-}_{E}\right)\times\left(E\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}L^{2}_{k-\frac{1}{2}}\left(S\right)^{0}_{-\infty}\right),

which is a family of
\mathbb{H}
-linear Fredholm operators parametrized over
B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where:

•

S^{+}_{E}
and
S^{-}_{E}
are the fiberwise positive and negative spinor bundles for the parametrized spin structure.

•

S
denotes the spinor bundle of the unique spin structure on
Y
.

•

L^{2}_{k}\left(S^{\pm}_{E}\right)
denotes the fiberwise
L^{2}_{k}
-sections of
S^{\pm}_{E}
.

•

L^{2}_{k-\frac{1}{2}}\left(S\right)
is the space of spinors on
Y
with
L^{2}_{k-\frac{1}{2}}
completion, induced from the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant metric
