249.89577pt\raise-7.05554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right]

where
N
is a sufficiently large integer.

Then we have the following theorem.

Theorem 4.36
.

There exists an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant stable map

\mathcal{T}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\colon(\mathrm{ind}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{1}})^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-\partial W_{\Gamma},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})

that is an
S^{1}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence. Here,
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
is the first term of the sequence

\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots

appearing in the discussion above.

Proof.

From the discussions above, we observe that

\bigl(\mathrm{ind}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{1}}\bigr)^{+}\wedge V_{i}^{+}\simeq\bigl(\mathrm{ind}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\bigr)^{+}

for all integers
i>0
. Hence we define
\mathcal{T}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
as follows:

•

For each
i
, set

\mathcal{T}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}|_{V_{i}}=BF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{i}).

•

For each
i
, define
\mathcal{T}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}|_{W_{i}\wedge[0,1]}
using any
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant homotopy between

BF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{i})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad BF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}),

which exists by the discussions above.

By construction,
\mathcal{T}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant. The fact that it is an
S^{1}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence is precisely
[
dai2023lattice
, Theorem 1.1]
.
∎

Furthermore, it can be seen that the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant stable homotopy equivalence class of
\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
can be read off from the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-labelled planar graded root
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}=(R,\lambda_{V},\lambda_{A})
defined in
4.27
; the process is given as follows.

•

For each leaf
v
of
R
, define the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representation

V_{v}=\mathbb{C}^{-\lambda_{V}(v)}.

•

For each simple angle
(v,v^{\prime})
of
R
, define the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representation

W_{(v,v^{\prime})}=\mathbb{C}^{-\lambda_{V}(v)-\lambda_{A}(v,v^{\prime})}.

Note that
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}
is naturally a subrepresentation of both
V_{v}
and
V_{v^{\prime}}
.

•

Define

\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})=\left(\bigvee_{\text{leaf }v}V_{v}^{+}\right)\;\vee\;\left(\bigvee_{\text{simple angle }(v,v^{\prime})}(W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\wedge[0,1])\right)\Big/\sim,

where
\sim
is defined by attaching
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\times\{0\}
to
V_{v}^{+}
and
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\times\{1\}
to
V_{v^{\prime}}^{+}
via the natural inclusions

W_{(v,v^{\prime})}\hookrightarrow V_{v}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad W_{(v,v^{\prime})}\hookrightarrow V_{v^{\prime}}.

Lemma 4.37
.

Possibly after a cyclic permutation of elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
applied to all leaf and angle labels of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
, there exists an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representation
V
such that there is a
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant map

V^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})\longrightarrow SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})

which is an
S^{1}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Let
v_{0}
be the leftmost leaf of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
, i.e., there is no leaf
v^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
such that
(v^{\prime},v_{0})
forms a simple angle. Then it is straightforward to see that there exists an “inclusion”

f\colon\left(\mathbb{C}^{\lambda_{V}(v_{0})}\right)^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})\;\hookrightarrow\;\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}).

We claim that
f
is an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant homotopy equivalence.

To see this, choose leaves
v,v^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
such that
(v,v^{\prime})
forms a simple angle at some vertex
w
. The part of
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
corresponding to
v,v^{\prime},w
is

\mathcal{H}(v,v^{\prime})=\bigl(V_{v}^{+}\vee V_{v^{\prime}}^{+}\vee(W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\wedge[0,1])\bigr)\big/\sim,

where
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\times\{0\}
and
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\times\{1\}
are attached to
V_{v}^{+}
and
V_{v^{\prime}}^{+}
via inclusions.

On the other hand, in the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
computation sequence

\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0)),\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,0}),\dots,\;\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,n_{0}-1}),\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(1)),\dots

used to define
\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, there exist indices
0\leq i<j<k
such that the vertices
v,w,v^{\prime}
correspond respectively to

\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i)),\qquad\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(j)),\qquad\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(k)).

By construction of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
, the sequence

\bigl\{\chi_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(\ell))\bigr\}_{\ell\geq 0}

is increasing for
i\leq\ell\leq j
and decreasing for
j\leq\ell\leq k
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(i))}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}^{\lambda_{V}(v_{0})}\oplus V_{v},

\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(j))}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}^{\lambda_{V}(v_{0})}\oplus W_{(v,v^{\prime})},

\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{t}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(k))}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}^{\lambda_{V}(v_{0})}\oplus V_{v^{\prime}}.

For simplicity, denote the
m
th term in the subsequence from
i
to
j
by
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{m}
, and let its length be
N
. Define

V_{m}=\mathbb{C}^{-\lambda_{V}(v_{0})}\oplus\mathrm{ind}^{t}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{m}}.

Then we obtain a chain of inclusions

W_{(v,v^{\prime})}=V_{N}\;\hookrightarrow\;V_{N-1}\;\hookrightarrow\;\cdots\;\hookrightarrow\;V_{1}=V_{v},

whose composition is precisely the inclusion
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}\hookrightarrow V_{v}
.

Now, in
(\mathbb{C}^{-\lambda_{V}(v_{0})})^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, the piece corresponding to the subsequence from
i
to
j
is

\bigl(V_{1}^{+}\vee(V_{2}^{+}\wedge[0,1])\vee\cdots\vee(V_{N}^{+}\wedge[0,1])\bigr)\big/\sim,

where
\sim
attaches
V_{m+1}^{+}\times\{0\}
to
V_{m}^{+}
for
1\leq m<N
. Up to
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant homotopy equivalence, this simplifies to

\bigl(V_{1}^{+}\vee(V_{N}^{+}\wedge[0,1])\bigr)\big/\sim,

where
\sim
attaches
V_{N}^{+}\times\{0\}
directly to
V_{1}^{+}
via the composition of inclusions, i.e. the inclusion
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}\hookrightarrow V_{v}
. A similar argument applies to the subsequence between
j
and
k
.

Thus, the contribution of the computation sequence between
i
and
k
in
(\mathbb{C}^{-\lambda_{V}(v_{0})})^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is

\bigl(V_{v}^{+}\vee V_{v^{\prime}}^{+}\vee(W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\wedge[0,1])\bigr)\big/\sim,

where
\sim
attaches
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\times\{0\}
to
V_{v}^{+}
and
W_{(v,v^{\prime})}^{+}\times\{1\}
to
V_{v^{\prime}}^{+}
via inclusions. But this is exactly the corresponding part of
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
. Applying this argument to all simple angles of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
, we obtain an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant homotopy equivalence

\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})\simeq\left(\mathbb{C}^{-\lambda_{V}(v_{0})}\right)^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}).

This proves the claim, and the lemma follows from
4.36
.
∎

4.5.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lattice chain

In this subsection, we construct a chain model (in fact, a finite-dimensional bounded
A_{\infty}
-bimodule model) for the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lattice homotopy type
\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, which computes its
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant cohomology. Recall that, for any topological space
X
, the singular cochain complex
C^{\ast}(X)
is an
E_{\infty}
-algebra. Moreover, if a topological group
G
acts on a topological space
X
, then the reduced equivariant singular cochain complex
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)
is naturally an
E_{\infty}
-module over
C^{\ast}_{G}(\ast)\cong C^{\ast}(BG)
.
The “lattice chain model” constructed here will be quasi-isomorphic to

C^{\ast}\!\left(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})\right),

and hence also to
C^{\ast}\!\left(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})\right)
, as a
C^{\ast}(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-module.

Lemma 4.38
.

Let
G
be a topological group acting on a topological space
X
. Suppose that we have two open subsets
U,V\subset X
, which are setwise
G
-invariant, satisfying
U\cup V=X
. Consider the inclusion maps

i_{U}\colon U\cap V\hookrightarrow U,\qquad i_{V}\colon U\cap V\hookrightarrow V.

Then there exists a quasi-isomorphism of
C^{\ast}(BG)
-modules (with any coefficient ring):

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathrm{Cone}\!\left(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U)\;\oplus\;\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(V)\xrightarrow{(i_{U}\sqcup i_{V})^{\ast}}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U\cap V)\right).

Proof.

Denote the mapping cone

\operatorname{Cone}\!\left(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U)\oplus\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(V)\xrightarrow{(i_{U}\sqcup i_{V})^{\ast}}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U\cap V)\right)

by
\mathcal{C}
. Then we have

\mathcal{C}=\bigl(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U\sqcup V)\oplus\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U\cap V)[-1],\,d_{\mathrm{Cone}}\bigr).

Observe that we have the following homotopy-commutative diagram, where all arrows are induced by inclusions:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U\sqcup V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(U\cap V)}

Choosing such a commutation homotopy induces the desired map

f\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}.

In order to show that this map is a quasi-isomorphism, we observe that
f
induces the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{\ast-1}_{G}(U\cap V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{\ast}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{\ast}_{G}(U\sqcup V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{\ast-1}_{G}(U\cap V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{\ast}(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{H}^{\ast}_{G}(U\sqcup V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}

Therefore
f
is a quasi-isomorphism by the five-lemma.
∎

Observe that we have the following quasi-isomorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-dgas:

C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq C^{\ast}(BS^{1};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Since
H^{\ast}(BS^{1};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is generated by a single element, we can construct a quasi-isomorphism from
H^{\ast}(BS^{1};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
to
C^{\ast}(BS^{1};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, which implies that
C^{\ast}(BS^{1};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is formal. The same argument applies to
C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Unfortunately, for
every
prime
p>2
,
C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is not formal: it was computed in
[
benson2021massey
, Theorem 1.3]
that we have a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
12
12

12

We work with
A_{\infty}
-algebras and
A_{\infty}
-modules, rather than
E_{\infty}
-algebras and (
E_{\infty}
-)modules, for simplicity.
C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is easy to describe as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra but not so much as a dga.
:

C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[R,S]/(R^{2}),\,m_{\ast}\bigr),\qquad m_{p}(R,\dots,R)=S.

Note that we are implicitly taking
m_{2}
to be the multiplication operation in the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[R,S]/(R^{2})
and all other
A_{\infty}
operations to be zero. Hence we get

C^{\ast}\!\bigl(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p}\bigr)\simeq\begin{cases}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,\theta],\,m_{\ast}=0\text{ for }\ast\neq 2\bigr),&\text{if }p=2,\\
\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,R,S]/(R^{2}),\,m_{p}(R,\dots,R)=S\bigr),&\text{if }p>2.\end{cases}

Here,
\deg\theta=\deg R=1
and
\deg U=\deg S=2
. For simplicity, we will use the following conventions from now on.

•

We denote
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,\theta],\,m_{\ast}=0\bigr)
and, for
p>2
,
\mathcal{R}_{p}=\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,R,S]/(R^{2}),\,m_{p}(R,\dots,R)=S\bigr)
, so that
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathcal{R}_{p}
for all primes
p
.

•

When
p=2
, we will denote
\theta^{2}
by
S
.

Remark 4.39
.

For any prime
p
, the dga
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is homotopy equivalent, as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, to
\mathcal{R}_{p}
. Hence, in general, we will not distinguish between
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
\mathcal{R}_{p}
. However, in contexts where we explicitly need the commutativity of
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, we shall denote the corresponding
E_{\infty}
-algebra
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
(over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
) by
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
.

We then compute
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(V^{+})
for various
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representations
V
.

Lemma 4.40
.

Let

f\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}

be an
E_{\infty}

C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-module endomorphism, regarded as an
A_{\infty}

\mathcal{R}_{p}
-
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-bimodule endomorphism, such that the induced map

f^{\ast}\colon H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}_{p})\longrightarrow H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}_{p})

is given by

f^{\ast}=(U+kS)\cdot\mathrm{id}_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}_{p})}\qquad\text{for some }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

Then
f
is homotopic to
(U+kS)\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}
.

Proof.

Denote the
A_{\infty}
bimodule endomorphism
(U+kS)\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}
by
g
. Then
f
and
g
are both
A_{\infty}
bimodule endomorphisms of
\mathcal{R}_{p}
. Observe that homotopy classes of
A_{\infty}
bimodule endomorphisms of
\mathcal{R}_{p}
are in bijective correspondence with homology classes of

\mathcal{R}_{p}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\mathcal{R}_{p}\simeq\mathcal{R}_{p},

which is simply
H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}_{p})=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,R,S]/(R^{2})
. The correspondence is given by

[\varphi]\longmapsto\varphi^{\ast}(1).

By assumption, we know that
f^{\ast}=g^{\ast}
, and thus
f
and
g
correspond to the same homology class in
H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}_{p})
. The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 4.41
.

For any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{p}]
, we have

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}((\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathcal{R}_{p}[-|\mathbf{n}|].

Under this identification, the pullback of the map
U_{[k]}\colon(\mathbb{C}^{0})^{+}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{C}_{[k]})^{+}
satisfies

(U_{[k]})^{\ast}\sim(U+kS)\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}

for all
k=0,\ldots,p-1
. Hence, the pullback of the map
(\mathbb{C}^{0})^{+}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+}
is given by
U^{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}
.

Proof.

We may assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathbf{n}\geq 0
, so that the statement is now about pointed
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-spaces rather than
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant spectra. Consider the sphere bundle

\xi\colon(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+}\times_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}E(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})\longrightarrow B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})

as the fiberwise one-point compactification of

\xi_{0}\colon\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}\times_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}E(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})\longrightarrow B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}),

and denote the zero section of
\xi_{0}
by
s
. Since
\xi_{0}
is oriented (the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
is orientation-preserving), choosing a cocycle
\Omega\in C^{|\mathbf{n}|}(\xi_{0},\partial\xi_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
representing the Thom class of
\xi
gives a chain-level Thom map

\mathrm{Th}_{\Omega}\colon C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\xleftarrow{\simeq}C^{\ast}(\xi_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\xrightarrow{\cup\Omega}C^{\ast}(\xi_{0},\partial\xi_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq C^{\ast}(\xi,s;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}((\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+};\mathbb{Z}_{p}),

which is
C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-linear. Note that such a cocycle
\Omega
can be constructed by choosing a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant volume form on
(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+}
and applying the Borel construction. Since
\mathrm{Th}_{\Omega}
induces the Thom isomorphism in homology, it is a quasi-isomorphism. Hence the first statement of the lemma follows.

To prove the second statement, first note that the case
k=0
is obvious. For simplicity, we will abuse notation and denote the element
(U_{[k]})^{\ast}(1)\in\mathcal{R}_{p}
by
U_{[k]}
. For each
[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we have an automorphism

\varphi_{[k]}\colon S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}\xrightarrow{(z,[n])\mapsto\left(e^{\frac{2\pi nki}{p}}z,[n]\right)}S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

Under the new parametrization of
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
given by
\varphi_{[-k]}
, the representation
\mathbb{C}_{[k]}
becomes
\mathbb{C}_{[0]}
. Hence,

U=U_{[0]}=(B\varphi_{[-k]})^{\ast}(U_{[k]}),

that is,

U_{[k]}=\bigl((B\varphi_{[-k]})^{-1}\bigr)^{\ast}(U)=(B\varphi_{[k]})^{\ast}(U),

since
\varphi_{[-k]}=\varphi_{[k]}^{-1}
.
To compute
(B\varphi_{[k]})^{\ast}
, note that we may write
\varphi_{[k]}
as

\varphi_{[k]}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}_{S^{1}}&i_{[k]}\\
0&\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\end{pmatrix},

where
i_{[k]}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to S^{1}
is the map
i_{[k]}([n])=e^{\frac{2\pi kni}{p}}
. To compute
(Bi_{[k]})^{\ast}
, observe that
(Bi_{[1]})^{\ast}(U)=S
, and we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{i_{[k]}}
\textstyle{S^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z\mapsto z^{k}}
\textstyle{S^{1}}

Since the pullback along
B(z\mapsto z^{k})
maps
U\in H^{\ast}(BS^{1};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
to
kU
, we obtain
(Bi_{[k]})^{\ast}(U)=kS
. Thus,

U_{[k]}=(B\varphi_{[k]})^{\ast}(U)=U+(Bi_{[k]})^{\ast}(U)=U+kS,

as desired. The lemma therefore follows from
4.40
.
∎

We can now construct the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lattice chain
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
as follows. Recall that, in
Section

4.4
, we considered the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
computation sequence

\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0)),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,0}),\,\dots,\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,n_{0}-1}),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(1)),\,\dots

and rewrote it as

\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{3},\dots.

We then used their
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Dirac indices to define virtual
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-representations
V_{i}
and
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant virtual linear maps
f_{i}
, each either from
V_{i}
to
V_{i+1}
or from
V_{i+1}
to
V_{i}
. We also defined
W_{i}
as the domain of
f_{i}
, as well as integers
\Delta_{i}
, and when
\Delta_{i}\neq 0
, the nonnegative integer
k_{i}
. Using these data, we define the
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-module

\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})=(C_{V}\oplus C_{W},m_{\ast})

as follows:

•

C_{V}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{V}_{V_{i}}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{V_{i}}=\mathcal{R}_{p}[-1-2\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}V_{i}]=\mathcal{R}_{p}\bigl[-1-2\cdot\sum_{l=1}^{i}\Delta_{l}\bigr]
;

•

C_{W}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{V}_{W_{i}}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{W_{i}}=\mathcal{R}_{p}[-\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}W_{i}]=\mathcal{R}_{p}\bigl[-2\cdot\sum_{l=1}^{i}\Delta_{l}\bigr]
;

•

m_{1}|_{C_{V}}=0
;

•

The image of
m_{1}|_{\mathcal{V}_{W_{i}}}
is contained in
\mathcal{V}_{V_{i}}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{V_{i+1}}
, and its value, as an element of
\mathcal{V}_{V_{i}}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{V_{i+1}}
, is given by

m_{1}|_{\mathcal{V}_{W_{i}}}(1)=\begin{cases}(1,1)&\text{if}\quad\Delta_{i}=0,\\
(1,(U+k_{i}S)^{\Delta_{i}})&\text{if}\quad\Delta_{i}>0,\\
((U+k_{i}S)^{-\Delta_{i}},1)&\text{if}\quad\Delta_{i}<0.\end{cases}

•

All other
A_{\infty}
operations are inherited from
\mathcal{R}_{p}
.

Then we have the following theorem.

Theorem 4.42
.

For each
[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra automorphism
\psi_{[k]}
of
\mathcal{R}_{p}
that fixes
R
and
S
and maps
U
to
U+kS
.
13
13

13

This is precisely the pullback map
(B\varphi_{[k]})^{\ast}
, where
\varphi_{[k]}
is the group automorphism of
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
defined in the proof of
4.41
.
Then, under the identification
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathcal{R}_{p}
of
A_{\infty}
-modules up to quasi-isomorphism, the chain complex
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is quasi-isomorphic to
C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
as an
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-module, after a degree shift and a reparametrization of
\mathcal{R}_{p}
via
\psi_{[k]}
for some
[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Proof.

By
4.38
and
4.41
, there exists a quasi-isomorphism

C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{p})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}),

since
\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is constructed by gluing cylinders to spheres. The theorem then follows from
4.36
.
∎

Remark 4.43
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
. As shown in
4.17
, for any two
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lifts
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y)
of
\mathfrak{s}
, there exists some
[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
such that their
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer spectra are related by the automorphism
\varphi_{[k]}
defined in the proof of
4.41
. Consequently, their cochain complexes

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{p})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}_{p})

are related, as
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-modules, by the automorphism
\psi_{[k]}
of
\mathcal{R}_{p}
.

Thus, once we compute
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
for one
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lift
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
of
\mathfrak{s}
, we can obtain
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
for any other lift
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{s}
simply by replacing every occurrence of
U
with
U+kS
.

For simplicity, from now on we use the following notation: given an element
\mathbf{n}=\sum_{[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}}n_{[k]}\cdot[k]
, we define

U^{\mathbf{n}}:=\prod_{[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(U+kS)^{n_{[k]}}.

Remark 4.44
.

In the proof of
4.42
, we apply
4.38
to glue cylinders to spheres. For clarity, we present here a simplified case in a more explicit form. Suppose that we have three complex virtual
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representations

V_{1}=\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}_{1}},\qquad V_{2}=\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}_{2}},\qquad W=\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{m}},

where
\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\geq 0
. Consider the inclusions

i_{1}\colon W\hookrightarrow V_{1},\qquad i_{2}\colon W\hookrightarrow V_{2}.

Define

X=\bigl(V_{1}^{+}\vee V_{2}^{+}\vee(W^{+}\wedge[0,1])\bigr)\big/\sim,

where
(w,0)\sim i_{1}^{+}(w)
and
(w,1)\sim i_{2}^{+}(w)
for
w\in W^{+}
.
By
4.41
, under the identifications

\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(V_{1}^{+};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(V_{2}^{+};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(W^{+};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\mathcal{R}_{p}

(up to suitable degree shifts), the pullback maps along
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are given by

(i_{1}^{+})^{\ast}=U^{\mathbf{n}_{1}},\qquad(i_{2}^{+})^{\ast}=U^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}.

Hence, applying
4.38
shows that
C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is quasi-isomorphic to the following module:

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}}

Observe that whenever we encounter a sequence of differentials of the form

x\xrightarrow{\;\;a\;\;}y\xleftarrow{\;\;1\;\;}z\xrightarrow{\;\;b\;\;}w,

we can quotient out the acyclic submodule
(z\xrightarrow{1}y+bw)
to replace it with
x\xrightarrow{ab}w
. Applying this simplification yields the following module:

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{U^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}}

which is precisely the part of
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
that we wanted
C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
to correspond to.

We now describe how to read off
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, up to quasi-isomorphism, from
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-labelled planar graded roots. Given a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-labelled planar graded root
\mathcal{R}=(R,\lambda_{V},\lambda_{A})
, we define the
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-module

\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{R})=(C_{V}\oplus C_{A},m_{\ast}),

where
C_{V},C_{A}
, and
m_{\ast}
are defined as follows:

•

C_{V}=\bigoplus_{\text{leaf }v}\mathcal{V}_{v}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{v}=\mathcal{R}_{p}[-1+2|\lambda_{V}(v)|]
;

•

C_{A}=\bigoplus_{\text{simple angle }(v,v^{\prime})}\mathcal{V}_{(v,v^{\prime})}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{(v,v^{\prime})}=\mathcal{R}_{p}[2|\lambda_{V}(v)+\lambda_{A}(v,v^{\prime})|]
;

•

m_{1}|_{C_{V}}=0
;

•

m_{1}|_{\mathcal{V}_{(v,v^{\prime})}}
is contained in
\mathcal{V}_{v}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{v^{\prime}}
, and

m_{1}|_{\mathcal{V}_{(v,v^{\prime})}}(1)=\bigl(U^{\lambda_{A}(v,v^{\prime})},\,U^{\lambda_{A}(v,v^{\prime})+\lambda_{V}(v)-\lambda_{V}(v^{\prime})}\bigr)\in\mathcal{V}_{v}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{v^{\prime}};

•

all other
A_{\infty}
operations are inherited from
\mathcal{R}_{p}
.

Then the following lemma is immediate.

Lemma 4.45
.

Under the identification
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathcal{R}_{p}
of
A_{\infty}
-algebras up to quasi-isomorphism, the complex
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
is quasi-isomorphic to
C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
, up to a degree shift and a reparametrization of
\mathcal{R}_{p}
via
\psi_{[k]}
for some
[k]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from
4.37
and
4.42
.
∎

Remark 4.46
.

Note that
4.42
can also be proven directly from the following two facts:

•

\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is a homotopy colimit, as observed in
4.35
;

•

The singular cochain complex functor
C^{\ast}(-;k)\colon\mathrm{Top}\longrightarrow E_{\infty}\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
preserves homotopy colimits for any commutative coefficient ring
k
.

We nevertheless included this section because it offers a more explicit explanation.

Similarly, taking the fixed point locus with respect to the action of

(that is,

, where

) yields

168.37518pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right]\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{+}.

In both cases, the fixed point locus is a complex sphere spectrum. It then follows from
4.42
together with a localization theorem for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-actions on finite
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-CW complexes (see, for example,
[
may1998equivariant
, Theorem 2.1, p. 44]
) that

\begin{split}H^{\ast}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{0});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\theta]}\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\theta,\theta^{-1}]&\cong H^{\ast}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})^{\tau};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\theta]}\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\theta,\theta^{-1}]\\
&\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\theta,\theta^{-1}].\end{split}

In particular,
H^{\ast}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{0});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
has rank one over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\theta]
. Since the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
Y
is the deck transformation for the branched double cover of
S^{3}
along the torus knot
T_{3,19}
, this agrees with
[
iida2024monopoles
, Theorem 1.16]
. Hence, we have passed a basic sanity check.

Remark 4.47
.

Note that this computation is carried out for some
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
. However, since
Y
is a homology sphere (which admits a unique nonequivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure) and
|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|=1
is not divisible by any prime, it follows from
4.16
that any two
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures are related by twisting. Therefore, the computations in this subsection apply to any
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
.

4.7.
The chain-level
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-local equivalence group and Frøyshov invariants

Recall that for any
E_{\infty}
-algebra
A
over a field, the derived category
\mathcal{D}(A)
of
A
-modules is well defined. The
perfect derived category

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{perf}}(A)
is defined as the closure of
A
itself, viewed as an
A
-module, inside
\mathcal{D}(A)
under degree shifts, extensions, and passing to direct summands. The objects of
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{perf}}(A)
are called
perfect
A
-modules
.

Remark 4.48
.

Using
4.38
, it is straightforward to see that for any topological group
G
, any finite
G
-CW complex
X
, and any field
\mathbb{F}
, the equivariant cochain complex
\widetilde{C}_{G}(X;\mathbb{F})
is perfect as a
C^{\ast}(BG;\mathbb{F})
-module.

We will use the fact that

\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p}),

which allows us to regard

U^{-1}\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})

as an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-algebra. Note that since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
is a localization of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U]
and hence flat,
U^{-1}\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
is also flat over
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
. We will also regard
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
as an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module by discarding
C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and then inverting
U
. More precisely, the following dga map gives
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
the structure of an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-algebra, where
\epsilon
denotes the augmentation map:

\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}C^{\ast}(B\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\epsilon}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}].

Definition 4.49
.

An
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module
M
is said to be of
weak SWF-type
if

M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][n]

as an (
A_{\infty}
)
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
-module for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Given two
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-modules
M,N
of weak SWF-type, an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module map
f\colon M\to N
is called
local
if

f\otimes\mathrm{id}\colon M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\longrightarrow N\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]

is a quasi-isomorphism of (dg)
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
-modules.

Finally, two
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-modules
M,N
of weak SWF-type are said to be
weakly locally equivalent
if there exist local maps
M\to N[n]
and
N\to M[m]
for some integers
m,n
.

Note that
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, regarded as an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module, is of weak SWF-type.

Definition 4.50
.

An
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module
M
of weak SWF-type is said to be of
SWF-type
if it is perfect and weakly locally equivalent to
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
. Two
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-modules
M,N
of SWF-type are
locally equivalent
if there exist local maps
M\to N
and
N\to M
.

Lemma 4.51
.

The following statements hold.

(1)

For any
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-modules
M,N
of SWF-type, the tensor product
M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}N
is also of SWF-type and is quasi-isomorphic to
N\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}M
.

(2)

Given an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module
M
of SWF-type, its dual module
M^{\vee}
(that is, the module such that the functor
M^{\vee}\otimes-
is corepresented by
M
), which exists and is perfect by the perfectness of
M

[
Lurie_HA
, Proposition 7.2.4.4]
, is also of SWF-type. Moreover,
M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}M^{\vee}
is locally equivalent to
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
.

Proof.

For the first part of (1), choose local maps

f\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow M[m],\quad f^{\prime}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}[m^{\prime}],\quad g\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow N[n],\quad g^{\prime}\colon N\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}[n^{\prime}].

Then the maps

f\otimes g\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow(M\otimes N)[m+n],\quad f^{\prime}\otimes g^{\prime}\colon M\otimes N\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}[m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}],

are also local maps; this follows from the fact that their composition

(f^{\prime}\otimes g^{\prime})[m+n]\circ(f\otimes g)=(f^{\prime}[m]\circ f)\otimes(g^{\prime}[n]\circ g)

is (obviously) local,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
is indecomposable (up to homotopy equivalence), and

M\otimes N\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\simeq M\otimes(N\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\simeq M\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]

as (
E_{\infty}
)
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
-modules. Moreover, a simple hom–tensor adjunction shows that
M^{\vee}\otimes N^{\vee}\otimes-
is corepresented by
M\otimes N
:

M^{\vee}\otimes N^{\vee}\otimes L\simeq\mathrm{Mor}(M,N^{\vee}\otimes L)\simeq\mathrm{Mor}(M,\mathrm{Mor}(N,L))\simeq\mathrm{Mor}(M\otimes N,L).

It then follows from
[
Lurie_HA
, Proposition 7.2.4.4]
that
M\otimes N
is perfect, hence of SWF-type.

For the second part, namely that
M\otimes N
is quasi-isomorphic to
N\otimes M
, note that
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
is quasi-isomorphic to a commutative dga, over which the derived tensor product of modules is symmetric monoidal.

For the first part of (2), choose local maps

f\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow M[m],\qquad f^{\prime}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}[m^{\prime}].

Since
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
is self-dual, we obtain their shifted duals

(f^{\prime})^{\vee}[m^{\prime}]\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow M^{\vee}[m^{\prime}],\qquad f^{\vee}[m]\colon M^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}[m].

To see that these are local maps, observe that their composition satisfies

((f^{\prime})^{\vee}[m+m^{\prime}])\circ(f^{\vee}[m])=((f^{\prime}[m])\circ f)^{\vee}.

Since
(f^{\prime}[m])\circ f
is local, its dual is also local. Therefore, the left-hand side is local, i.e. the composition

(M^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\xrightarrow{(f^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id})[m]}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][m]\xrightarrow{((f^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id})[m+m^{\prime}]}(M^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])[m+m^{\prime}]

is a quasi-isomorphism. Taking homology, we see that
H^{\ast}(M^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])[m]
is a direct summand of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
as a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
-module. However,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
is indecomposable as a module over itself, so the summand is either
0
or
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
.

A similar argument (using the reverse composition) shows that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
is a direct summand of
H^{\ast}(M^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])[m^{\prime}]
. Hence,

H^{\ast}(M^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\not\cong 0,

so in fact

H^{\ast}(M^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][-m].

This implies that the maps
(f^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id})[m]
and
((f^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id})[m+m^{\prime}]
induce isomorphisms on homology, i.e. they are quasi-isomorphisms. In other words,
f^{\vee}[m]
and
(f^{\prime})^{\vee}[m+m^{\prime}]
are local maps. Hence
M^{\vee}
is of SWF-type.

Finally, for the second part of (2), observe that for any perfect module
L
over an
E_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, the trace and cotrace maps

\mathrm{tr}_{L}\colon L^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}L\longrightarrow\mathcal{A},\qquad\mathrm{cotr}_{L}\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow L^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}L,

are naturally defined. Recall from the proof of the first part of (2) that the tensored dual map

f^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id}=(f\otimes\mathrm{id})^{\vee}\colon M^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][m]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]

is a quasi-isomorphism; we denote its homotopy inverse by
(f^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id})^{-1}
. Furthermore, by naturality of the trace, the composition

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]&\xrightarrow{\makebox[68.28644pt][c]{\tiny$(f^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id})^{-1}\otimes(f^{\vee}\otimes\mathrm{id})$}}&\bigl(M^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\bigr)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[U,U^{-1}]}\bigl(M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\bigr)\\[4.30554pt]
&\xrightarrow{\makebox[68.28644pt][c]{\scriptsize$\simeq$}}&\bigl(M^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}M\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\\[4.30554pt]
&\xrightarrow{\makebox[68.28644pt][c]{\scriptsize$\mathrm{tr}_{M}\otimes\mathrm{id}$}}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\end{array}

agrees with
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]}
,
14
14

14

Here we use

M^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]=(M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])^{\vee}
, which follows from the fact that for any algebras
A,B
and a map
\phi\colon A\to B
, the scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{A}B\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{A}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{B}
is left adjoint to the forgetful functor
\phi^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{B}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{A}
defined as
\phi^{\ast}M={{}_{A}}B_{B}\otimes_{B}M
, and thus

\mathrm{Mor}_{B}(M^{\vee}\otimes_{A}B,N)\simeq\mathrm{Mor}_{A}(M^{\vee},\phi^{\ast}N)\simeq M\otimes_{A}(B\otimes_{B}N)\simeq(M\otimes_{A}B)\otimes_{B}N

for any perfect
A
-module
M
and any
B
-module
N
. This is a standard fact, see
[
Lurie_HA
, Proposition 4.6.2.17]
.
which is a quasi-isomorphism. Hence
\mathrm{tr}_{M}\otimes\mathrm{id}
is a quasi-isomorphism, i.e.
\mathrm{tr}_{M}
is a local map. Similarly, one shows that
\mathrm{cotr}_{M}
is also a local map. Since
M^{\vee}\otimes M
is of SWF-type by (1) and the first part of (2), we conclude that
M^{\vee}\otimes M
is locally equivalent to
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
.
∎

We now define the chain-level
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-local equivalence group
\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. First, set

\mathfrak{C}^{ch,\mathbb{Z}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=\frac{\left\{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\text{-modules of SWF-type}\right\}}{\text{local equivalence}},

and endow it with the group operation given by tensor product. By
4.51
,
\mathfrak{C}^{ch,\mathbb{Z}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is an abelian group. Moreover,
\mathbb{Z}
acts on
\mathfrak{C}^{ch,\mathbb{Z}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
by translation.

Definition 4.52
.

The
chain-level
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-local equivalence group

\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is defined as the fiber product

\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=\mathfrak{C}^{ch,\mathbb{Z}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}.

In particular, elements of
\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
are pairs
(M,r)
with

M\in\mathfrak{C}^{ch,\mathbb{Z}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
r\in\mathbb{Q}
, subject to the identification

(M[n],r)\sim(M,r+n)\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}.

It is clear that
\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is also an abelian group. With these formalisms in place, we can now show that the equivariant cochain functor induces a group homomorphism from the space-level local equivalence group to the chain-level local equivalence group.

Lemma 4.53
.

The monoidal functor

C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\colon\mathcal{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^{op}_{C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})}

induces a group homomorphism

C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\colon\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}.

Proof.

Clearly, any local map between spaces of type
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-SWF induces a local map between their
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-coefficient
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant cochains. Hence it suffices to prove that
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module of SWF-type.

Since
X
is
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariantly homotopy equivalent to a finite
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-CW complex, it follows from
4.38
that
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is a perfect
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-module (and thus an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module). Furthermore, the inclusion

i\colon X^{S^{1}}\hookrightarrow X

induces an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module map

i^{\ast}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{S^{1}};\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Since
i
itself is a local map, it follows that
i^{\ast}
is also a local map. Moreover, because
X^{S^{1}}
is homotopy equivalent to a sphere of some dimension
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have a Thom quasi-isomorphism

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{S^{1}};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathcal{R}_{p}[-m].

Hence the composition

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\xrightarrow{i^{\ast}}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{S^{1}};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Thom}}\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}[-m]

is a local map.

Now let
(X^{\vee},-r)
be the additive inverse of
(X,r)
in
\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. By definition, there exists a (space-level) local map

T_{X}\colon X\wedge X^{\vee}\longrightarrow S^{0}.

Passing to equivariant cochains yields a local map

T^{\ast}_{X}\colon\mathcal{R}_{p}\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{\vee};\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Since
X^{\vee}
is also a space of type
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-SWF, we have already established the existence of a local map

f\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{\vee};\mathbb{Z}_{p})\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}[n]

for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence the composition

\mathcal{R}_{p}[-n]\xrightarrow{T^{\ast}_{X}[-n]}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{\vee};\mathbb{Z}_{p})[-n]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes f[-n]}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})

is a local map, as desired.
∎

While
4.53
appears quite natural and elementary, it has the following consequence, which may be of independent interest.

Corollary 4.54
.

For any space
X
of type
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-SWF and its additive inverse
X^{\vee}
in
\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
, the
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-modules

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\vee}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{\vee};\mathbb{Z}_{p})

are locally equivalent.

Proof.

In
\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
it follows from
4.51
that the additive inverse operation is given by taking duals of perfect
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-modules. On the other hand,
4.53
shows that the functor
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
commutes with taking additive inverses up to local equivalence. The result follows.
∎

We now define chain-level equivariant Frøyshov invariants.

Definition 4.55
.

Given an element
\mathcal{X}=(X,r)\in\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
, where
X
is an
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-module of SWF-type, we set

\begin{split}\delta^{(p)}_{0}(\mathcal{X})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(r+\min\bigl\{n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\bigm|\,\text{there exists a local map }\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\to M[n]\bigr\}\right),\\[4.30554pt]
\delta(\mathcal{X})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(r+\min\bigl\{n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\bigm|\,\exists\,x\in H^{n}(M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\text{ with }U^{k}x\neq 0\text{ for all }k>0\bigr\}\right).\end{split}

These define functions

\delta,\,\delta^{(p)}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}.

Since
X
being of SWF-type implies the existence of a local map
\mathcal{R}_{p}\to M[n]
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, it follows that
\delta^{(p)}_{0}
is well defined.

Lemma 4.56
.

For any
X\in\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
we have

\delta(X)=\delta\bigl(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\bigr)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\delta^{(p)}_{0}(X)=\delta^{(p)}_{0}\bigl(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\bigr),

where the invariant
\delta^{(p)}_{0}(X)
is the equivariant Frøyshov invariant introduced in
[
Baraglia-Hekmati:2024-1
]
; see also
Section

3.1.1
for our formulation.

Proof.

Since the first equality is obvious, we only prove the second. Without loss of generality, assume that
X
is a space of type
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-SWF. Write
\delta^{(p)}_{0}(X)=n
. Then there exists a cohomology class
x\in\widetilde{H}^{2n}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
such that its pullback

i^{\ast}(x)\in H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}),

where
i\colon X^{S^{1}}\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion and we identify
\widetilde{H}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X^{S^{1}};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
with
H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})
via the Thom isomorphism, satisfies

i^{\ast}(x)=U^{k}\pmod{S}\qquad\text{ for some }k\in\mathbb{Z}.

The class
x
defines an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module map

f_{x}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow M[2n]

up to homotopy. For simplicity, write
C_{X}=\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Then the composition

\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\xrightarrow{f_{x}^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}}H^{\ast}(C_{X})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][2n]\xrightarrow{i^{\ast}[2n]\otimes\mathrm{id}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][2(n+k)]

is an isomorphism. Hence we obtain the following commutative diagram, where for any
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}
-module
L
we denote by
\mathfrak{T}_{L}
the natural map

H^{\ast}(L)\otimes_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\longrightarrow H^{\ast}(L\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]).

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i^{\ast}[2n]\otimes\mathrm{id})^{\ast}\circ(f_{x}^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][2(n+k)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})\otimes_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{x}^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(M)\otimes_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][2n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i^{\ast}[2n])^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}_{M}[2n]}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})\otimes_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][2(n+k)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}[2(n+k)]}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f_{x}\otimes\mathrm{id})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])[2n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i^{\ast}[2n]\otimes\mathrm{id})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])[2(n+k)]}

Since the left vertical maps, the right vertical maps, and the top map are isomorphisms, the composition of the two bottom maps must also be an isomorphism. Because

H^{\ast}(M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][\text{some degree shift}],

we conclude that
f_{x}\otimes\mathrm{id}
and
i^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}
are quasi-isomorphisms. In other words,
f_{x}
is a local map. Hence

\delta^{(p)}_{0}(C_{X})\leq n=\delta^{(p)}_{0}(X).

Now write
\delta^{(p)}_{0}(C_{X})=m
. Then there exists a local map

g\colon\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\longrightarrow M[2m].

Since
i^{\ast}
is also a local map, we obtain the following commutative diagram.

\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})\otimes_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(M)\otimes_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][2m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i^{\ast}[2m])^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}_{M}[2m]}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})\otimes_{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p})}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][2(m+k)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}[2(m+k)]}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g\otimes\mathrm{id})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])[2m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i^{\ast}[2n]\otimes\mathrm{id})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])[2(m+k)]}

We know that the left map, the right map, and the two bottom maps are isomorphisms. Hence, if we define

y=f^{\ast}(1)\in H^{2m}(M),

then

((i^{\ast}[2n])^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id})(y\otimes 1)=U^{2(m+k)},

which is equivalent to
i^{\ast}(y)=U^{2(m+k)}\pmod{S}
. Thus,

\delta^{(p)}_{0}(X)\leq m=\delta^{(p)}_{0}(C_{X}).

Therefore,
\delta^{(p)}_{0}(X)=\delta^{(p)}_{0}(C_{X})
, as desired.
∎

Hence we obtain the following commutative diagram of functions; note, however, that
\delta^{(p)}_{0}
is not a group homomorphism.

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
\scriptstyle{\delta,\delta^{(p)}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta,\delta^{(p)}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}}

Using
4.42
, we see that

C^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s});\mathbb{Z}_{p})

is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
,
which is generated over
\mathcal{R}_{p}
by elements
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
with

-5\leq i,j\leq 5
and
j\neq 0
. The
A_{\infty}
-operations are inherited from

\mathcal{R}_{p}
, together with the following differentials (i.e., the
m_{1}
operations):

\begin{split}\partial x_{0}&=(U+58S)y_{-1}+(U+60S)y_{1},\\
\partial x_{1}&=(U+61S)y_{1}+(U+72S)y_{2},\\
\partial x_{-1}&=(U+57S)y_{-1}+(U+46S)y_{-2},\\
\partial x_{2}&=(U+73S)y_{2}+(U+75S)(U+87S)y_{3},\\
\partial x_{-2}&=(U+45S)y_{-2}+(U+43S)(U+31S)y_{-3},\\
\partial x_{3}&=(U+88S)y_{3}+(U+90S)(U+95S)(U+102S)y_{4},\\
\partial x_{-3}&=(U+30S)y_{-3}+(U+28S)(U+23S)(U+16S)y_{-4},\\
\partial x_{4}&=(U+103S)y_{4}+(U+105S)(U+110S)(U+114S)(U+117S)y_{5},\\
\partial x_{-4}&=(U+15S)y_{-4}+(U+13S)(U+8S)(U+4S)(U+1S)y_{-5},\\
\partial x_{5}&=(U+118S)y_{5},\\
\partial x_{-5}&=Uy_{-5}.\end{split}

We now consider local homology classes of

H^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s});\mathbb{Z}_{p})

for various primes
p
. Before proceeding with computations, we define the notion of
local homology classes; note that
\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})

satisfies the assumptions below.

Definition 4.57
.

Let
M
be an
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-module such that

M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
is quasi-isomorphic to

\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][r]
for some
r\in\mathbb{Q}
. A homology class

\alpha\in H^{\ast}(M)
is called
local
if its image under the map

H^{\ast}(M)\longrightarrow H^{\ast}(M\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][r]

generates
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}][r]
as a module over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,U^{-1}]
.

For various primes
p
, we compute the minimal degree of local homology classes in

H^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
,
which coincides with the value of

\delta_{0}^{(p)}(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}))
.
Note, however, that
\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
is defined only up to equivariant stable homotopy equivalence, so our computations are determined only up to an overall degree shift. To fix this ambiguity, we declare

\delta(\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}))=0,

which amounts to setting
\deg(x_{0})=0
.

Also observe that, although the chain model
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
carries nontrivial higher
A_{\infty}
operations inherited from those of
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, these play no role in computing its homology. Thus, for the purpose of the calculations in this subsection, it suffices to ignore the higher operations and consider only the differential (i.e., the
m_{1}
operations). In other words, we will pretend, falsely, that
\mathcal{R}_{p}
is a formal
A_{\infty}
algebra, that is, quasi-isomorphic to its homology.

Lemma 4.58
.

Choose any
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y)
such that
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})=\mathfrak{s}
. Then, for any integer
i\geq 0
, we have

\delta^{(p)}_{0}\bigl((\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(R_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}))^{\ast}\bigr)-\delta\bigl((\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(R_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}))^{\ast}\bigr)=\delta^{(p)}_{0}(Y,\mathfrak{s})-\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s}),

where
\delta^{(p)}_{0}(Y,\mathfrak{s})
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Frøyshov invariant introduced in
[
Baraglia-Hekmati:2024-1
]
, and
\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s})
denotes the monopole Frøyshov invariant with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
coefficients.

Proof.

The statement follows directly from
3.18
,
4.37
and
4.56
.
∎

Example 4.59
.

Suppose
p=2
(so that
S=\theta^{2}
). A minimal degree local homology class
\alpha
is given by

\alpha=\left[\begin{array}[]{l}(U+S)^{2}x_{0}+U(U+S)(x_{1}+x_{-1})+U^{2}(x_{2}+x_{-2})+U(U+S)^{2}(x_{3}+x_{-3})\\
\quad+U^{3}(U+S)^{2}(x_{4}+x_{-4})+U^{4}(U+S)^{4}(x_{5}+x_{-5})\end{array}\right].

Since we set
\delta(Y)=\deg(x_{0})=0
, it follows that
\deg\alpha=4
. Therefore, by
4.58
, we obtain

\delta_{0}^{(2)}(Y)-\delta(Y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\deg\alpha=2.

Example 4.60
.

Suppose
p>118
, so that the elements
[0],[1],\dots,[118]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
are pairwise disjoint. A minimal degree local homology class
\alpha
is given by

\alpha=\left[P(U,S)\cdot x_{0}\;+\;\text{other terms involving }x_{i}\text{ for }i=-5,\dots,-1,1,\dots,5\right],

where the homogeneous polynomial
P\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,S]
is

\begin{split}P(U,S)&=U(U+15S)(U+30S)(U+45S)(U+57S)\\
&\quad(U+61S)(U+73S)(U+88S)(U+103S)(U+118S).\end{split}

Since we set
\delta(Y)=\deg(x_{0})=0
, it follows that
\deg\alpha=20
. Therefore, by
4.58
,

\delta_{0}^{(p)}(Y)-\delta(Y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\deg\alpha=10.

Note that
10
is also the dimension of
HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s})
; in fact, this equality holds in a much more general sense, as we will see in
4.63
.

4.9.
Behavior of
\delta^{(p)}_{0}
for large primes
p

In this subsection we study the behavior of the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant Frøyshov invariants
\delta^{(p)}_{0}(Y,\mathfrak{s})
, where
Y
is a Seifert fibered homology sphere equipped with the Seifert
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action that is not an
L
-space and
\mathfrak{s}
is a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
. Since the value of
\delta^{(p)}_{0}
is clearly invariant under twisting operations, and any two
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant lifts of a given
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
are related by twisting by
4.16
, we will deliberately conflate
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures with nonequivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures throughout this section.

Lemma 4.61
.

Let
k>0
and let
n_{1}^{\pm},\dots,n_{k}^{\pm}\geq 0
be integers. Consider the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded cochain complex
C
generated freely over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]
(with
\deg U=2
) by elements

x_{0},\dots,x_{k},\qquad y_{1},\dots,y_{k},

with differential

\partial y_{1}=\cdots=\partial y_{k}=0,\quad\partial x_{0}=U^{n_{1}^{-}}y_{1},\quad\partial x_{k}=U^{n_{k}^{+}}y_{k},\quad\partial x_{i}=U^{n_{i}^{+}}y_{i}+U^{n_{i+1}^{-}}y_{i+1}\quad(1\leq i<k).

Suppose that
\ell\in\{0,\dots,k\}
satisfies

\deg x_{\ell}=\max_{0\leq i\leq k}\deg x_{i}.

Then

\mathrm{dim}\,H^{\ast}(C)_{\mathrm{tor}}\;=\;\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}n_{i}^{-}\;+\;\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}n_{i}^{+},

where
H^{\ast}(C)_{\mathrm{tor}}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]
-torsion submodule of
H^{\ast}(C)
.
15
15

15

Here
H^{\ast}(C)_{\mathrm{tor}}
is viewed as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]
-module, but we are only counting its dimension as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector space. For instance,
\mathrm{dim}\,\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]/(U^{n})=n
.

Proof.

Define the nonnegative quantity

K_{C}=k+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}n_{i}^{-}+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}n_{i}^{+}.

If
K_{C}=0
, then
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]
is generated by
x_{0}
(with zero differential), so the lemma is clear. We now assume the statement holds whenever
K<K_{0}
for some
K_{0}>0
, and take
K_{C}=K_{0}
.

Case 1:
n_{1}^{-}
and
n_{k}^{+}
are not both positive.

Without loss of generality assume
n_{1}^{-}=0
(the case
n_{k}^{+}=0
is analogous). Then we have an acyclic summand

C_{0}=[x_{0}\to y_{0}]\subset C.

It follows that
C/C_{0}
is isomorphic to a chain complex
C^{\prime}
freely generated over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]
by
x^{\prime}_{0},\dots,x^{\prime}_{k-1}
and
y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{k-1}
(with
\deg y^{\prime}_{k-1}=\deg y_{k}
), where

\partial y^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=\partial y^{\prime}_{k-1}=0,\quad\partial x^{\prime}_{0}=U^{n_{2}^{-}}y^{\prime}_{1},\quad\partial x^{\prime}_{k-1}=U^{n_{k}^{+}}y^{\prime}_{k},\quad\partial x^{\prime}_{i}=U^{n_{i+1}^{+}}y^{\prime}_{i}+U^{n_{i+2}^{-}}y^{\prime}_{i+1}\quad(1\leq i<k-1).

Clearly
\deg x^{\prime}_{\ell-1}=\max_{0\leq i\leq k-1}\deg x^{\prime}_{i}
. Since
K_{C^{\prime}}=K_{C}-1
and
C_{0}
is acyclic,
C
is homotopy equivalent to
C^{\prime}
. By the inductive hypothesis,

\mathrm{dim}H^{\ast}(C)_{\mathrm{tor}}=\mathrm{dim}H^{\ast}(C^{\prime})_{\mathrm{tor}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}n_{i+1}^{-}+\sum_{i=\ell}^{k-1}n_{i+1}^{+}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}n_{i}^{-}+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}n_{i}^{+}.

Thus the lemma holds in this case.

Case 2:
n_{1}^{-}
and
n_{k}^{+}
are both positive.

We may assume
\ell\neq 1
(the case
\ell\neq k
is similar). Define a chain complex
C^{\prime}
generated over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]
by
x^{\prime}_{0},\dots,x^{\prime}_{k}
and
y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{k}
(with
\deg y^{\prime}_{i}=\deg y_{i}
), with

\partial y^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=\partial y^{\prime}_{k}=0,\quad\partial x^{\prime}_{0}=U^{m_{1}^{-}}y^{\prime}_{1},\quad\partial x^{\prime}_{k}=U^{n_{k}^{+}}y^{\prime}_{k},\quad\partial x^{\prime}_{i}=U^{n_{i}^{+}}y^{\prime}_{i}+U^{m_{i+1}^{-}}y^{\prime}_{i+1}\quad(1\leq i<k),

where

m_{i}^{-}=\begin{cases}n_{1}^{-}-1&i=1,\\
n_{i}^{-}&i>1.\end{cases}

Consider the degree-preserving map
f\colon C^{\prime}\to C
given by

f(y^{\prime}_{i})=y_{i}\quad(1\leq i\leq k),\qquad f(x^{\prime}_{i})=\begin{cases}Ux_{1}&i=1,\\
x_{i}&i>1.\end{cases}

We check that
\deg x^{\prime}_{0}=\deg x_{0}-2
and
\deg x^{\prime}_{i}=\deg x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, so
\deg x^{\prime}_{\ell}=\max_{0\leq i\leq k}\deg x^{\prime}_{i}
. Since
K_{C^{\prime}}=K_{C}-1
, the inductive hypothesis gives

\mathrm{dim}H^{\ast}(C^{\prime})_{\mathrm{tor}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}m_{i}^{-}+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}n_{i}^{+}=-1+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}n_{i}^{-}+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}n_{i}^{+}.

The map
f
is injective, and
C/f(C^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]/(U)
with zero differential. This yields the exact triangle

\cdots\longrightarrow H^{\ast}(C^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\;\;f_{\ast}\;\;}H^{\ast}(C)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]/(U)\longrightarrow\cdots,

from which one checks that
f_{\ast}
is injective. Hence we obtain the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow H^{\ast}(C^{\prime})_{\mathrm{tor}}\xrightarrow{\;\;f_{\ast}\;\;}H^{\ast}(C)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]/(U)\longrightarrow 0.

Therefore

\mathrm{dim}H^{\ast}(C)_{\mathrm{tor}}=\mathrm{dim}H^{\ast}(C^{\prime})_{\mathrm{tor}}+\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]/(U)=\left(-1+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}n_{i}^{-}+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}n_{i}^{+}\right)+1=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}n_{i}^{-}+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}n_{i}^{+}.

Thus the lemma holds for
C
in this case as well, completing the proof.
∎

Definition 4.62
.

For an element
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{p}]
, written as
\mathbf{n}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}}n_{\alpha}\cdot\alpha
, its
support
is

\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{n})=\{\,\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\mid n_{\alpha}\neq 0\,\}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

Two elements
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{p}]
are said to be
disjointly supported
if

\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{m})\cap\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{n})=\emptyset.

Theorem 4.63
.

Let
Y
be a Seifert fibered rational homology sphere, and let
\mathfrak{s}
be a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
. Then, for all sufficiently large primes
p
, we have

\delta^{(p)}_{0}(Y,\mathfrak{s})=\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s})+\mathrm{dim}\,HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s}).

In particular, if
\mathrm{dim}\,\widehat{HF}(Y,\mathfrak{s})>1
, then

\delta^{(p)}_{0}(Y,\mathfrak{s})>\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s})

for all sufficiently large
p
.

Proof.

Since we are assuming
p
to be large, we may take
p
so that it does not divide
|H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|
, ensuring that our results apply. Observe that
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
has the following general form, where
f_{i}^{\pm}=U^{\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\pm}}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}
for some nonzero elements
\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{p}]
with
\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\pm}\geq 0
, which are pairwise disjointly supported since
p
is large:

\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})\simeq\mathrm{hocolim}\left[\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 104.36969pt\raise-10.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 174.27165pt\raise-31.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
