192.18297pt\raise-15.75pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.90474pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{n-1}^{-}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 192.18297pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 234.09428pt\raise-31.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}x_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 217.02734pt\raise-8.89386pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.49504pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{n-1}^{+}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 208.05598pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 252.0056pt\raise-15.75pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.90474pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{n}^{-}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 252.0056pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 293.91692pt\raise-31.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{p}x_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 276.6283pt\raise-8.89386pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.49504pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{n}^{+}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 266.3498pt\raise-7.98456pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right].

Choose
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
such that
\deg(x_{k})=\max_{0\leq j\leq n}\deg(x_{j})
; without loss of generality we perform a degree shift so that
\deg(x_{k})=0
. Define

N:=\mathrm{dim}\,HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s}).

As in
4.59
and
4.60
, it suffices to prove:

•

there exists a local homology class of degree
2N
in
H^{\ast}(\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}))
;

•

no local homology class of degree less than
2N
exists.

Following the computation techniques in
Section

4.8
, when analyzing the homology of
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
we will ignore its higher
A_{\infty}
-operations inherited from
\mathcal{R}_{p}
and simply regard
\mathcal{R}_{p}
as quasi-isomorphic to its homology. This makes
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
an ordinary chain complex over the ring

H^{\ast}(\mathcal{R}_{p})=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,R,S]/(R^{2}).

Suppose first that there exists a local homology class
[\alpha]\in H^{\ast}(\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}))
with
\deg\alpha<2N
. Let
\alpha
be a cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
representing
[\alpha]
. We may write

\alpha=\sum_{i=0}^{n}r_{i}x_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}y_{j}

for some homogeneous elements
r_{i},s_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,R,S]/(R^{2})
. Since
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
are cycles and their homology classes (after setting
R=S=0
) are
U
-torsion, the element

\alpha_{0}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}r_{i}x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})

is also a cycle, and its homology class is local with
\deg\alpha_{0}=\deg\alpha<2N
.

Now

0=\partial\alpha_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(U^{\mathbf{n}_{j}^{+}}r_{j}+U^{\mathbf{n}_{j}^{-}}r_{j-1}\big)y_{j},

so
U^{\mathbf{n}_{j}^{+}}r_{j}+U^{\mathbf{n}_{j}^{-}}r_{j-1}=0
for all
j=1,\dots,n
. Consider the projection

\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,R,S]/(R^{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,S];\qquad R\longmapsto 0.

Then

U^{\mathbf{n}_{j}^{+}}\mathrm{pr}(r_{j})+U^{\mathbf{n}_{j}^{-}}\mathrm{pr}(r_{j-1})=0\quad\text{for all }j=1,\dots,n,

and hence

U^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbf{n}_{k+i}^{-}}\cdot\mathrm{pr}(r_{k})\;=\;(-1)^{j}U^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbf{n}_{k+i}^{+}}\cdot\mathrm{pr}(r_{k+j})\quad\text{for }j=1,\dots,n-k.

Since the
\mathbf{n}_{i}^{\pm}
are pairwise disjointly supported, the monomials
U^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbf{n}_{k+i}^{-}}
and
U^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbf{n}_{k+i}^{+}}
are relatively prime in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[U,S]
. Thus
U^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbf{n}_{k+i}^{+}}
divides
\mathrm{pr}(r_{k})
, in particular
U^{\mathbf{n}_{k+1}^{+}+\cdots+\mathbf{n}_{n}^{+}}
divides
\mathrm{pr}(r_{k})
. A similar argument shows that
U^{\mathbf{n}_{1}^{-}+\cdots+\mathbf{n}_{k}^{-}}
also divides
\mathrm{pr}(r_{k})
. Since these factors are relatively prime, their product

U^{\mathbf{n}_{1}^{-}+\cdots+\mathbf{n}_{k}^{-}+\mathbf{n}_{k+1}^{+}+\cdots+\mathbf{n}_{n}^{+}}

divides
\mathrm{pr}(r_{k})
. Therefore

\begin{split}2N>\deg\alpha_{0}=\deg r_{0}&=\deg\mathrm{pr}(r_{0})\\
&\geq\deg U^{\mathbf{n}_{1}^{-}+\cdots+\mathbf{n}_{k}^{-}+\mathbf{n}_{k+1}^{+}+\cdots+\mathbf{n}_{n}^{+}}\\
&=2\big(|\mathbf{n}_{1}^{-}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{k}^{-}|+|\mathbf{n}_{k+1}^{+}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{n}^{+}|\big),\end{split}

which implies

|\mathbf{n}_{1}^{-}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{k}^{-}|+|\mathbf{n}_{k+1}^{+}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{n}^{+}|<N.

However, by
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Theorem 8.3]
and
4.61
, we have

N=\mathrm{dim}HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s})\;=\;|\mathbf{n}_{1}^{-}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{k}^{-}|+|\mathbf{n}_{k+1}^{+}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{n}^{+}|,

a contradiction. Hence no local homology class of degree less than
2N
can exist.

Finally, consider the cycle

\beta=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}U^{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{i}\mathbf{n}_{j}^{-}\big)+\big(\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\mathbf{n}_{j}^{+}\big)}\cdot x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}).

Since

\deg\beta=2\big(|\mathbf{n}_{1}^{-}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{k}^{-}|+|\mathbf{n}_{k+1}^{+}|+\cdots+|\mathbf{n}_{n}^{+}|\big)=2N,

this shows that there exists a local homology class of degree
2N
in
H^{\ast}(\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}))
. The theorem follows.
∎

Remark 4.64
.

A careful reader will notice that, if
\mathfrak{s}
is the canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure of
Y
, the condition that
p
be “sufficiently large” in
4.63
can in fact be quantified as
p>N_{Y}
, where
N_{Y}
is the integer defined in
2.8
. This agrees with the assumption
p>118
in
4.60
, since for
Y=\Sigma(3,5,19)
we have
N_{Y}=118
.

Remark 4.65
.

When
Y
is a Seifert fibered homology sphere, it follows from
[
baraglia2024brieskorn
, Proposition 3.6]
that
\delta^{(p)}_{0}(Y)=\delta^{(p)}_{\infty}(Y)
. Thus,
4.63
specializes to
[
baraglia2024brieskorn
, Theorem 6.1]
, except that we replace
\mathrm{rk}(HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s}))
with
\mathrm{dim}\,HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s})
and restrict to the case when
p
is sufficiently large. However, we note that although
[
baraglia2024brieskorn
, Theorem 6.1]
is stated for all primes
p
, there appears to be a counterexample when
p
is small.

Indeed, let
Y=\Sigma(3,5,19)
with its unique
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
. As computed in
4.59
, we have

\delta^{(2)}_{\infty}(Y,\mathfrak{s})-\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s})=\delta^{(2)}_{0}(Y,\mathfrak{s})-\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s})=2.

On the other hand, the quotient
Y/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
has two
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures (both self-conjugate). Their graded roots can be computed directly using the algorithm in
Section

2.3
, see also
Figure

2
. This yields

\mathrm{rk}(HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s}))=10\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathrm{rk}(HF_{red}(Y/\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{0}))=4

for either
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
on
Y/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Hence,

\mathrm{rk}(HF_{red}(Y,\mathfrak{s}))-\mathrm{rk}(HF_{red}(Y/\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{0}))=6\;\;>\;\;2=\delta^{(2)}_{\infty}(Y,\mathfrak{s})-\delta(Y,\mathfrak{s}),

contradicting the statement of
[
baraglia2024brieskorn
, Theorem 6.1]
. This contradiction persists even if one replaces
\mathrm{rk}
with
\mathrm{dim}
in the above formulas.

\vdots
\vdots

Figure 2.
Left: the graded root of
Y/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with respect to its canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure.
Right: the graded root of
Y/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with respect to the other
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure.

5.
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lattice chain homotopy type

Throughout this section, we fix a Seifert fibered rational homology sphere
Y
such that
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{can}}_{Y}
is self-conjugate. We denote by
\Gamma
the unique almost rational negative definite plumbing graph satisfying
Y\cong\partial W_{\Gamma}
. We also use the notation introduced in
Section

2.2
and
Section

2.3
.

5.1.
The coefficient of the canonical class at the central node

Recall from
Section

2.3
that
\Gamma
has
\nu
arms. The central node of
\Gamma
, whose weight is
e_{0}
, is denoted by
v_{c}
, and the
i
-th node of the
l
-th arm, whose weight is
-k^{l}_{i}
, is denoted by
v^{l}_{i}
. For any integers
i,l
satisfying
1\leq i\leq l\leq\nu
, consider the matrix

A^{l}_{i}:=\begin{pmatrix}-k^{l}_{i}&1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
1&-k^{l}_{i+1}&1&\cdots&0&0\\
0&1&-k^{l}_{i+2}&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&-k^{l}_{s_{l}-1}&1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&-k^{l}_{s_{l}}\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
A^{l}_{1}
is the intersection matrix for the
l
-th arm of
\Gamma
. Before moving on, we compute its determinant.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
p^{l}_{i},q^{l}_{i}
be the unique coprime positive integers satisfying

\frac{p^{l}_{i}}{q^{l}_{i}}=[k^{l}_{i},\dots,k^{l}_{s_{l}}].

Note that
q^{l}_{1}=q_{l}
,
p^{l}_{1}=p_{l}
, and
q^{l}_{i}=p^{l}_{i+1}
for all
i=1,\dots,s_{l}-1
. Then we have

\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{i}=(-1)^{s_{l}-i+1}p^{l}_{i}.

Proof.

We proceed by induction. First, when
i=s_{l}
or
i=s_{l}-1
, the lemma is obvious. Suppose that the lemma holds for
i=m+1
and
i=m+2
for some integer
m
satisfying
1\leq m\leq s_{l}-2
. Then we get

\begin{split}\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{m}&=-k^{l}_{m}\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{m+1}-\mathrm{det}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c|c@{}}1&\begin{matrix}1&0&\cdots&0&0\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
0\end{matrix}&A^{l}_{m+2}\end{array}\right)\\
&=-(k^{l}_{m}\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{m+1}+\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{m+2})\\
&=(-1)^{s_{l}-m+1}\left(k^{l}_{m}p^{l}_{m+1}-p^{l}_{m+2}\right).\end{split}

On the other hand, since

\frac{p^{l}_{m}}{q^{l}_{m}}=[k^{l}_{m},\dots,k^{l}_{s_{l}}]=k^{l}_{m}-\frac{1}{[k^{l}_{m+1},\dots,k^{l}_{s_{l}}]}=k^{l}_{m}-\frac{q^{l}_{m+1}}{p^{l}_{m+1}}=\frac{k^{l}_{m}p^{l}_{m+1}-q^{l}_{m+1}}{p^{l}_{m+1}},

we see that

p^{l}_{m}=k^{l}_{m}p^{l}_{m+1}-q^{l}_{m+1}=k^{l}_{m}p^{l}_{m+1}-p^{l}_{m+2}.

Therefore, we deduce that
\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{m}=(-1)^{s_{l}-m+1}p^{l}_{m}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Then, with respect to the ordered basis

V(\Gamma)=\{v_{c},v^{1}_{1},\dots,v^{1}_{s_{1}},\dots,v^{\nu}_{1},\dots,v^{\nu}_{s_{\nu}}\},

the intersection matrix of
W_{\Gamma}
is given by

Q_{\Gamma}=\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c|c|c|c|c}e_{0}&\begin{matrix}1&0&\cdots&0&0\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}1&0&\cdots&0&0\end{matrix}&\cdots&\begin{matrix}1&0&\cdots&0&0\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
0\end{matrix}&A^{1}_{1}&O&\cdots&O\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
0\end{matrix}&O&A^{2}_{1}&\cdots&O\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
0\end{matrix}&O&O&\cdots&A^{\nu}_{1}\end{array}\right).

Then the central coefficient
m_{v_{c}}(K)
of the canonical class
K
(of
\Gamma
) is the first component of the vector
Q_{\Gamma}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{K}_{\Gamma}
, where

\mathbf{v}^{K}_{\Gamma}=(-e_{0}-2,\,k^{1}_{1}-2,\dots,k^{1}_{s_{1}}-2,\dots,k^{\nu}_{1}-2,\dots,k^{\nu}_{s_{\nu}}-2)^{T},

i.e., for each node
v\in V(\Gamma)
, the
v
-component of
\mathbf{v}^{K}_{\Gamma}
is defined as
K(v)=-w(v)-2
.

To compute the first component of
Q_{\Gamma}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{K}_{\Gamma}
, we have to compute the entries on the first row (i.e., the row corresponding to the central vertex
v_{c}
) of the adjugate matrix
\mathrm{adj}(Q_{\Gamma})
. We label the rows and columns of
Q_{\Gamma}
with the corresponding nodes of
\Gamma
. Then it is straightforward to see that

\mathrm{adj}(Q_{\Gamma})_{v_{c},v_{c}}=\prod_{l=1}^{\nu}\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{1}=(-1)^{|V(\Gamma)|-1}p_{1}\cdots p_{\nu}.

Fix any integers
l,i
satisfying
1\leq l\leq\nu
and
1\leq i\leq s_{l}
. The following fact is obvious:

\mathrm{adj}(Q_{\Gamma})_{v_{c},v^{l}_{i}}=-[\mathrm{adj}(A^{l}_{1})]_{1,i}\cdot\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}\mathrm{det}A^{l^{\prime}}_{1}=(-1)^{|V(\Gamma)|-s_{l}-1}[\mathrm{adj}(A^{l}_{1})]_{1,i}\cdot\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}p_{l^{\prime}}.

Since we may write

(A^{l}_{1}\text{ with the $1$st row and $i$th column deleted})=\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c|c@{}}X&Y\\
\hline\cr O&A^{l}_{i+1}\\
\end{array}\right)

for some matrices
X,Y
, where
X
is upper triangular with all diagonal entries equal to
1
, we get

[\mathrm{adj}(A^{l}_{1})]_{1,i}=(-1)^{i-1}\mathrm{det}A^{l}_{i+1}=(-1)^{s_{l}}p^{l}_{i+1},

which implies that

\mathrm{adj}(Q_{\Gamma})_{v_{c},v^{l}_{i}}=(-1)^{|V(\Gamma)|-1}p^{l}_{i+1}\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}p_{l^{\prime}}.

Since
\mathrm{det}Q_{\Gamma}=(-1)^{|V(\Gamma)|}|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|
, we deduce that

-|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|\cdot m_{v_{c}}(K)=-(e_{0}+2)\prod_{l=1}^{\nu}p_{l}+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}\left(\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}p_{l^{\prime}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s_{l}}(k^{l}_{i}-2)p^{l}_{i+1}\right),

where we define
p^{l}_{s_{l}+1}=1
and
p^{l}_{s_{l}+2}=0
. To simplify this expression, recall from the proof of
5.1
that

k^{l}_{i}p^{l}_{i+1}-p^{l}_{i}-p^{l}_{i+2}=0\qquad\text{for each }i=1,\dots,s_{l}.

Taking the sum over all
i=1,\dots,s_{l}
and simplifying then gives

\sum_{i=1}^{s_{l}}(k^{l}_{i}-2)p^{l}_{i+1}=p^{l}_{1}-p^{l}_{2}-p^{l}_{s_{l}+1}+p^{l}_{s_{l}+2}=p_{l}-q_{l}-1.

Hence we obtain

\begin{split}-|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|\cdot m_{v_{c}}(K)&=-(e_{0}+2)\prod_{l=1}^{\nu}p_{l}+\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}(p_{l}-q_{l}-1)\left(\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}p_{l^{\prime}}\right)\\
&=-\left[e_{0}\prod_{l=1}^{\nu}p_{l}+\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}q_{i}\left(\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}p_{l^{\prime}}\right)\right]+(\nu-2)\prod_{l=1}^{\nu}p_{l}-\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}q_{l}\left(\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}p_{l^{\prime}}\right)\\
&=|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|+(\nu-2)\prod_{l=1}^{\nu}p_{l}-\sum_{l=1}^{\nu}q_{l}\left(\prod_{l^{\prime}\neq l}p_{l^{\prime}}\right)\\
&=|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|+|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|\cdot N_{Y},\end{split}

which implies that
m_{v_{c}}(K)=-N_{Y}-1
. We record this as a lemma, as it is very useful.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
Y
be a Seifert fibered rational homology sphere such that the canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
is self-conjugate. Let
\Gamma
be the negative definite almost rational plumbing graph satisfying
Y\cong\partial W_{\Gamma}
, let
K
be the canonical class of
\Gamma
, and let
v_{c}
be the central node of
\Gamma
. Then

m_{v_{c}}(K)=-N_{Y}-1,

where
N_{Y}
denotes the number defined in
2.8
.

This lemma has a very important corollary.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
Y
be a Seifert fibered rational homology sphere such that the canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
is self-conjugate. Then
N_{Y}
is an integer.

Proof.

Recall from
2.7
that, since
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
is self-conjugate, we have
m_{v}(K)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
v\in V(\Gamma)
. From
5.2
, we know that
m_{v_{c}}(K)=-N_{Y}-1
. Hence
-N_{Y}-1\in\mathbb{Z}
, which implies that
N_{Y}
is also an integer.
∎

Now we consider the cycles
(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))_{i\geq 0}
induced by taking the central node
v_{c}
as the base node; see
Section

2.2
for the definition. The following lemma shows that their weights are symmetric under the reflection
i\leftrightarrow N_{Y}+1-i
in the region
0\leq i\leq N_{Y}+1
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
Y
be a Seifert fibered homology sphere such that the canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
is self-conjugate, and let
\Gamma
be the negative definite almost rational plumbing graph satisfying
Y\cong\partial W_{\Gamma}
. Then, for any integer
i
satisfying
0\leq i\leq N_{Y}+1
, we have

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}+1-i)).

16
16

16

This implies
\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}-i)=-\Delta_{Y,\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i)
; the special case when
Y
is a homology sphere was proven in
[
can2014calculating
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Proof.

Recall that
m_{v_{c}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))=0
. By
5.2
, we have

m_{v_{c}}(-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))=-m_{v_{c}}(K)-m_{v_{c}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))=N_{Y}+1-i=m_{v_{c}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}+1-i)).

Since
\Gamma
is almost rational and
K\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
, it follows from
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 9.1]
that

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))\geq\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}+1-i)).

On the other hand, since
k_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}=K
, we have

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(-K-x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(K(-K-x)+(K+x)\cdot(K+x)\big)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(K(x)+x\cdot x\big)=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x)

for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
. Therefore,

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))\leq\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}+1-i)).

Since this holds for all integers
i
with
0\leq i\leq N_{Y}+1
, the reverse inequality also follows by replacing
i
with
N_{Y}+1-i
. The lemma follows.
∎

We now consider the
spherical Wu class

\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
, where
\mathfrak{s}
is any self-conjugate
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
. It is defined as the unique element of
H^{2}(W_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})\subset\mathbb{Q}V(\Gamma)
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y})|_{Y}=c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y})
;

•

There exists a function
\lambda_{\mathfrak{s}}\colon V(\Gamma)\to\{0,1\}
such that

\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y})=\sum_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\lambda_{\mathfrak{s}}(v)v.

Note that
\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
is a characteristic element and
\mathrm{sp}(\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}))|_{Y}=\mathfrak{s}
. Thus, if we consider the spherical Wu class for the canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
on
Y
, then since
k_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}=K
, there exists a unique cycle
x^{can}_{Y}\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
satisfying

\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y})=K+2x^{can}_{Y},

which we call the
Wu cycle
.

From now on, we fix the following terminology: if
N_{Y}
is even, write
\hat{N}_{Y}=\frac{N_{Y}}{2}+1
; if
N_{Y}
is odd, write
\hat{N}_{Y}=\frac{N_{Y}+1}{2}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

We have
m_{v_{c}}(x^{can}_{Y})=\hat{N}_{Y}
.

Proof.

By
5.2
, we obtain

m_{v_{c}}(x^{can}_{Y})=\frac{m_{v_{c}}(\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}))-m_{v_{c}}(K)}{2}=\frac{N_{Y}+1+\lambda_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(v_{c})}{2}.

Since
\lambda_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(v_{c})
is either
0
or
1
and
m_{v_{c}}(x^{can}_{Y})
is an integer, the right-hand side equals
\hat{N}_{Y}
. Thus
m_{v_{c}}(x^{can}_{Y})=\hat{N}_{Y}
.
∎

Finally, we consider
constant-weight sequences
of elements of
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
.

Definition 5.6
.

A sequence
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
of elements of
\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
is called
constant-weight
if
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{i})
is independent of
i
, and for each
i=1,\dots,n-1
, there exists some node
v_{i}\in V(\Gamma)\smallsetminus\{v_{c}\}
such that
x_{i+1}=x_{i}\pm v_{i}
. We also say that such a sequence is between
x_{1}
and
x_{n}
, since its reverse sequence is again a constant-weight sequence. Moreover, we say that the sequence

\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{n})

is a constant-weight sequence.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose there exists an integer
i\geq 0
and a cycle
x\in\mathbb{Z}V(\Gamma)
satisfying
m_{v_{c}}(x)=i
and
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i))=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x)
. Then there exists a constant-weight sequence between
x
and
x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(i)
.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 9.1]
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

We have

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y}))=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x^{can}_{Y})
.

Proof.

Observe from
5.4
and the invariance of
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}
under the involution
x\leftrightarrow-K-x
that

m_{v_{c}}(-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y}))=m_{v_{c}}(N_{Y}+1-\hat{N}_{Y}),\qquad\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y}))=\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}+1-\hat{N}_{Y})).

Hence, by
5.7
, there exists a constant-weight sequence between
-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
and
x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}+1-\hat{N}_{Y})
.

Claim.
There exists a constant-weight sequence between
-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
and
x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
.

To prove the claim, we divide into two cases. If
N_{Y}
is odd, then
N_{Y}+1-\hat{N}_{Y}=\hat{N}_{Y}
, so the claim is immediate. If
N_{Y}
is even, then
\hat{N}_{Y}=(N_{Y}+1-\hat{N}_{Y})+1
. By
5.4
and
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 9.1(c)]
, the computation sequence from
x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(N_{Y}+1-\hat{N}_{Y})
to
x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
is a constant-weight sequence. Composing this with the previously constructed sequence proves the claim.

Next, consider the connected component
C\subset\mathbb{R}V(\Gamma)
of the sublevel set of
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}
consisting of cubes of weight at most
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y}))
, which contains the cycle
x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
; see
[
nemethi2008lattice
, Section 3.1]
for a precise definition. Since there exists a constant-weight sequence between
x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
and
-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
, it follows that
-K-x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})
is also contained in
C
. Following the argument of
[
dai2018pin
, Lemma 2.1]
, we see that
C
is the unique component (of the given sublevel set) that is setwise fixed under the involution
x\leftrightarrow-K-x
, and that
x^{can}_{Y}\in C
. This implies

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x^{can}_{Y})\leq\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})).

On the other hand, since
m_{v_{c}}(x^{can}_{Y})=\hat{N}_{Y}
by
5.5
, it follows from
[
nemethi2005ozsvath
, Lemma 9.1(a)]
that

\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x^{can}_{Y})\geq\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}}(\hat{N}_{Y})).

Therefore, equality holds, and the lemma follows.
∎

5.2.
A
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lattice homotopy type

From now on, in addition to the assumptions made in the previous subsection, we further assume that
|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|
is odd; this implies that
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
is the unique self-conjugate
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
. As discussed in
Section

4.3
, we construct the equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
computation sequence associated to
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
for any
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y)
with
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})=\mathfrak{s}
:

\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0)),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,0}),\,\dots,\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,n_{0}-1}),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(1)),\,\dots

In this subsection, we will modify this sequence to obtain a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path that carries the lattice homology.

Definition 5.9
.

Given a self-conjugate
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
on
Y
, a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path
for
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is a finite sequence

\mathfrak{s}_{-n},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{-2},\mathfrak{s}_{-1},\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}

of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures, together with a sequence of nodes

v_{-n},\dots,v_{-2},v_{-1},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}

of
V(\Gamma)
, satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathfrak{s}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
.

•

For each
i=1,\dots,n
, we have
\mathfrak{s}_{i}|_{Y}=\mathfrak{s}_{-i}|_{Y}=\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
.

•

For each
i=1,\dots,n-1
,
\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}
agrees with
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
outside the interior of
D_{v_{i}}
, and

\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{s}_{i+1})=\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{s}_{i})\pm PD[S_{v_{i}}].

•

If
v_{i}\neq v_{c}
, then

\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\mathrm{ind}^{t}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\big)=\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\mathrm{ind}^{t}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathcal{D}\mkern-11.0mu/}_{W_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}}\big).

•

For each
i=1,\dots,n
, we have
\mathfrak{s}_{-i}=\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
.

Remark 5.10
.

In
5.9
, we refer to the sequence
\{v_{i}\}
as the sequence of nodes associated with the given
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path. For simplicity, we usually do not specify the associated sequence of nodes when discussing
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant paths, unless it is necessary to do so.

We also need to define the notion of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
J
-almost rational paths carrying the lattice homology. To do so, we require a sequence

\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m}

of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathfrak{t}_{m}=\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
and
\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\overline{\mathfrak{t}_{1}}
;

•

For each
i=1,\dots,m-1
, the structure
\mathfrak{t}_{i+1}
agrees with
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
outside the interior of
D_{v}
for some
v\in V(\Gamma)
, and satisfies

\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{t}_{i+1})=\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{t}_{i})\pm PD[S_{v}];

•

The sequence
\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{t}_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{t}_{m})
is a constant-weight sequence.

Such a sequence always exists, since its nonequivariant analogue exists by
[
dai2018pin
, Lemma 3.2]
, and this can be lifted to a sequence of equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures by
4.12
.

Definition 5.11
.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path

\gamma=\{\mathfrak{s}_{-n},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{-2},\mathfrak{s}_{-1},\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}\},

we glue in the sequence
\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m}
discussed above to obtain a new sequence

\gamma^{\prime}=\{\mathfrak{s}_{-n},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{-2},\mathfrak{s}_{-1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1},\mathfrak{t}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m-1},\mathfrak{t}_{m}=\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}\}.

This induces a
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-linear map

\mathbb{H}(\gamma,\mathfrak{s})\longrightarrow HF^{-}(Y,\mathfrak{s}).

We say that
\gamma

carries the lattice homology
of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
if this map is an isomorphism; see
[
dai2023lattice
, Theorem 4.9]
and the surrounding discussion for more details.
17
17

17

The notion of “carrying the lattice homology” is defined in
[
dai2023lattice
]
for any sequence of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures whose consecutive terms differ by
PD[S_{v}]
for some node
v
.

Then, when
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
, we show that a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path carrying the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
always exists. Note that every
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lift of
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
is self-conjugate.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
be any
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lift of
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
. Then there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path for
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
that carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{s}))
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we denote
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})=\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
by
\mathfrak{s}
. We have the following
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
computation sequence for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
:

\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(0)),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,0}),\,\dots,\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{0,n_{0}-1}),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(1)),\,\dots.

By the observations in
2.8
, this sequence continues to carry the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
after removing all terms beyond
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(N_{Y}))
. After this truncation, we focus on its latter half:

\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(\hat{N}_{Y})),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\hat{N}_{Y},0}),\,\dots,\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\hat{N}_{Y},n_{\hat{N}_{Y}}-1}),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(\hat{N}_{Y}+1)),\,\dots,\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\mathfrak{s}}_{N_{Y}-1,n_{N_{Y}-1}-1}),\,\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\mathfrak{s}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(N_{Y})).

For simplicity, we rewrite this sequence as

\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{M}.

By
5.5
,
5.8
and
5.7
, there exists a constant-weight sequence
\mathfrak{r}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{r}_{s}
such that

\mathfrak{r}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathfrak{r}_{s}=\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathfrak{s}}(\hat{N}_{Y})).

Then the following sequence is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant sequence for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
:

\gamma_{0}=\{\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}=\overline{\mathfrak{r}_{s}},\dots,\overline{\mathfrak{r}_{1}},\mathfrak{r}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{r}_{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{M}\}.

To show that this path carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
, observe from
5.4
that for any constant-weight sequence
\mathfrak{u}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{u}_{M^{\prime}}
with
\mathfrak{u}_{1}=\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{M^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, the sequence

\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}=\mathfrak{u}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{u}_{M^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{M}

carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
. Since the sequence
\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m}
in
5.11
is constant-weight, we see that

\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}=\overline{\mathfrak{r}_{s}},\dots,\overline{\mathfrak{r}_{1}}=\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m}=\mathfrak{r}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{r}_{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{1}

is also a constant-weight sequence. Hence the composed sequence

\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}=\overline{\mathfrak{r}_{s}},\dots,\overline{\mathfrak{r}_{1}}=\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m}=\mathfrak{r}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{r}_{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{M}

carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
. Therefore,
\gamma_{0}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path that carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
, as desired.
∎

From now on, we impose the following additional condition on
Y
: the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
Y
is free. This condition implies that, for the singular orbits
\{(p_{i},q_{i})\}_{i=1}^{\nu}
of the Seifert action on
Y
, the integers
p_{1},\dots,p_{\nu}
are all odd. Combined with the assumption that
|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|
is odd, this shows that
N_{Y}
is even.

Remark 5.13
.

For simplicity, given
\mathbf{n}=n_{+}\cdot[0]+n_{-}\cdot[1]\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, we adopt the following notation:

\mathbb{R}^{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n_{+}}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{-}^{n_{-}},\quad\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbf{n}}=\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n_{+}}\oplus\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{-}^{n_{-}},\quad\mathbb{H}^{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbb{H}_{+}^{n_{+}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{-}^{n_{-}}.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant self-conjugate
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
on
Y
and a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path
\gamma
for
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, written as

\mathfrak{s}_{-n},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{-2},\mathfrak{s}_{-1},\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n},

we denote its latter half, i.e., the sequence
\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}
, by
\gamma_{0}
. Following the constructions leading to
4.34
, we obtain a finite
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spectrum
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
by gluing various representation spheres
V_{i}^{+}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, and cylinders
W_{j}^{+}\wedge[0,1]
,
j=1,\dots,n-1
. We may also suspend
V_{i}^{+}
and
W_{j}^{+}
by
(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+}
for some fixed
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, so that
V_{1}^{+}=(\mathbb{C}^{2\mathbf{m}})^{+}
for some
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Consider the “identity map”

f\colon(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbf{m}})^{+}\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}V_{1}^{+},

which is an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant homotopy equivalence. Note that while
V_{1}^{+}
carries only an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-action,
(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbf{m}})^{+}
carries a
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-action. Also consider the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant map

\beta\colon(\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{+}\longrightarrow S^{1}\vee j\cdot S^{1},

defined by identifying the point at infinity of
(\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{+}
with the basepoint. Here,
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts on
S^{1}\vee j\cdot S^{1}
as follows: the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-subaction is trivial, and
j
acts by swapping
S^{1}
and
j\cdot S^{1}
.

Then we define the map
18
18

18

This is an example of an
induced map
; for more details on induced spaces and induced maps, see
Section

5.3
.

\mathrm{Ind}(f)\colon\Sigma^{\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbf{m}})^{+}=(\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{+}\wedge(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbf{m}})^{+}\xrightarrow{\beta\wedge f}(S^{1}\vee j\cdot S^{1})\wedge V_{1}^{+}=\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}}V_{1}^{+}\vee j\cdot\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}}V_{1}^{+},

which is a
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant (stable) map. Using this, we make the following definition.

Definition 5.14
.

Consider the inclusion
\mathrm{inc}\colon V_{1}^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
, which induces the doubled map

\mathrm{inc}\vee j\cdot\mathrm{inc}\colon V_{1}^{+}\vee j\cdot V_{1}^{+}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})\vee j\cdot\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0}),

a
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant map. We then define the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant lattice homotopy type of the given
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path
\gamma
by

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\gamma)=\mathrm{Cone}\big((\mathrm{inc}\vee j\cdot\mathrm{inc})\circ\mathrm{Ind}(\Sigma^{-\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}}f)\big).

Remark 5.15
.

We may regard
V_{1}^{+}
as the “boundary” of
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
. The map
\mathrm{Ind}(\Sigma^{-\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}}f)
can then be viewed as a “parametrization” of the boundary
V_{1}^{+}\vee j\cdot V_{1}^{+}
of
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})\vee j\cdot\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
. Thus, taking its mapping cone can be interpreted as “gluing”
V_{0}^{+}
with
j\cdot V_{0}^{+}
so as to connect
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
with its copy
j\cdot\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
in a
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant way.

Then we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant self-conjugate
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
, and let
\gamma
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path for
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
. Then there exists a virtual
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representation
V
together with a
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant map

\mathcal{T}\colon V^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}(\gamma)\longrightarrow SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}),

which is an
S^{1}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence, possibly after modifying the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-action on the codomain via the automorphism

S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{(z,[n])\mapsto((-1)^{n}z,[n])}S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Proof.

Write the given path
\gamma
as

\mathfrak{s}_{-n},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{-2},\mathfrak{s}_{-1},\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}.

By following the arguments of
5.12
, we obtain a constant-weight sequence

\mathfrak{s}_{-1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1},\mathfrak{t}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m-1},\mathfrak{t}_{m}=\mathfrak{s}_{1}

such that the concatenated sequence

\mathfrak{s}_{-n},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{-2},\mathfrak{s}_{-1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1},\mathfrak{t}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m-1},\mathfrak{t}_{m}=\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}

carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
.

Following the constructions leading to
4.34
, this concatenated sequence defines a
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-spectrum
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. By
4.36
, there exists a virtual
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representation
V
and a
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant map

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\colon V^{+}\wedge\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\longrightarrow SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}),

which is an
S^{1}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence, possibly after modifying the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-action on the codomain via the automorphism

S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{(z,[n])\mapsto((-1)^{n}z,[n])}S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Furthermore, since the sequence
\mathfrak{t}_{1},\mathfrak{t}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m-1},\mathfrak{t}_{m}
is constant-weight, the corresponding part of the construction of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is simply a cylinder (i.e., of the form
W^{+}\wedge[0,1]
for some virtual
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-representation
W
). Removing this cylinder and directly identifying its two boundaries yields
\mathcal{H}(\gamma)
. Hence, there is an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant homotopy equivalence

\mathcal{T}_{0}\colon\mathcal{H}(\gamma)\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\prime}.

Finally, setting
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{V^{+}}\wedge\mathcal{T}_{0})
gives the desired map.
∎

Thus, we define the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant lattice homotopy type of
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
as follows.

Definition 5.17
.

Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
be any
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lift of
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
. The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path
\gamma
constructed in the proof of
5.12
carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
. We define the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant homotopy type of
\mathcal{H}(\gamma)
to be the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant lattice homotopy type
of
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, and denote it by

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}).

Note that since we have assumed
|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|
is odd, it follows from
4.20
that the two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lifts of the unique self-conjugate
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure (which we have already assumed to be the canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
) are both self-conjugate, and correspond to the two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Spin structures on
Y
.

Remark 5.18
.

A priori,
5.17
depends on the choice of a constant-weight sequence between
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(\mathrm{Wu}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}(x_{\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{{\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}})}(\hat{N}_{Y}))
made in the proof of
5.12
. However, since a constant-weight sequence contributes a subspace of the form
V^{+}\wedge[0,1]
to
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
, it follows that choosing a different constant-weight sequence does not change the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant homotopy type of
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
, and hence also does not change the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant homotopy type of
\mathcal{H}(\gamma)
.

As in the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant case, the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant lattice homotopy type can also be read off from a planar graded root with additional structure.

Definition 5.19
.

Consider the reflection map
T\colon(x,y)\mapsto(-x,y)
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root
(R,\lambda_{V},\lambda_{A})
is called
symmetric
if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

The embedded graph
R
(in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
) is setwise
T
-invariant;

•

For each leaf
v
of
R
, we have
\lambda_{V}(v)=\lambda_{V}(T(v))
;

•

For each simple angle
(v,v^{\prime})
of
R
, note that
(T(v^{\prime}),T(v))
is also a simple angle of
R
; we then require

\lambda_{A}(T(v^{\prime}),T(v))=\lambda_{A}(v,v^{\prime})+\lambda_{A}(v)-\lambda_{A}(v^{\prime}).

Two symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded roots are said to be equivalent if they become identical after a
T
-equivariant isotopy, possibly combined with swapping the two elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Lemma 5.20
.

Given any
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root
\mathcal{R}=(R,\lambda_{V},\lambda_{A})
, exactly one of the following two statements holds:

•

R
is not equivalent to any symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root. In this case, we say that
\mathcal{R}
is
nonreflective
.

•

R
is equivalent to some symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R})
as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root, and
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R})
is unique up to equivalence of symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded roots. In this case, we say that
\mathcal{R}
is
reflective
.

Proof.

We may list the leaves of
R
as
v_{0},\dots,v_{n}
, so that for each
i=1,\dots,n
, the pair
(v_{i-1},v_{i})
forms a simple angle. It is then straightforward to see that
R
is reflective if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\lambda_{V}(v_{i})=\lambda_{V}(v_{n-i})
for all
i=0,\dots,n
;

•

\lambda_{A}(v_{i-1},v_{i})+\lambda_{V}(v_{i-1})-\lambda_{V}(v_{i})=\lambda_{A}(v_{n-i},v_{n-i+1})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Moreover, if these conditions are satisfied, there is a unique (up to equivalence) way to construct a symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root with these leaves, angles, and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labels. This proves the lemma.
∎

Then the following lemma is clear.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
be any
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lift of
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
. Then the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
is reflective.

Proof.

The claim follows from
5.4
, together with the fact that
N_{Y}
is even.
∎

Definition 5.22
.

By
5.21
, the symmetrization
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
exists. Since it is unique up to equivalence of symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded roots, we call it the
symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root of
(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
and denote it by
R^{s}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
.

We now describe a process for constructing a
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant homotopy type from the symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
. Observe that if
\mathcal{R}=(R,\lambda_{V},\lambda_{A})
is a symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root and
w
is a
T
-invariant vertex of
R
with minimal
y
-coordinate among all
T
-invariant vertices of
R
, then exactly one of the following holds:

•

w
is a leaf, and
R
has an odd number of leaves;

•

There exists a unique leaf
v
such that
(T(v),v)
forms a simple angle at
w
, and
R
has an even number of leaves.

If the first case holds, we call
w
the
central leaf
of
\mathcal{R}
; if the second case holds, we call
(T(v),v)
the
central angle
of
\mathcal{R}
.

List the leaves of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
as
v_{0},\dots,v_{n}
, so that for each
i=1,\dots,n
, the pair
(v_{i-1},v_{i})
forms a simple angle. Recall from the construction of
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
in
Section

4.4
that each leaf
v_{i}
corresponds to
V_{v_{i}}^{+}
and each angle
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
corresponds to
W_{(v_{i},v_{i+1})}^{+}\wedge[0,1]
;
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
is obtained by gluing these together.

First suppose that
n
is odd, say
n=2k-1
. Then
v_{k}
is the central leaf of
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
. Denote by
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
the subspace of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
consisting of
V_{v_{i}}^{+}
for
k\leq i\leq n
and
W_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{+}\wedge[0,1]
for
k\leq i\leq n-1
. After stabilizing all
V_{v_{i}}^{+}
and
W_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{+}
by
(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+}
for some fixed
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, we may assume that
V_{v_{k}}^{+}=(\mathbb{C}^{2\mathbf{m}})^{+}
for some
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
. Then, following the discussion preceding
5.14
, we obtain a
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-spectrum, denoted by
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}))
.

Next suppose that
n
is even, say
n=2k
. Then
(v_{k},v_{k+1})
is the central angle of
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
. Denote by
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
the subspace of
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
consisting of
V_{v_{i}}^{+}
for
k+1\leq i\leq n
,
W_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{+}\wedge[0,1]
for
k+1\leq i\leq n-1
, and
W_{v_{k},v_{k+1}}^{+}\wedge\left[\tfrac{1}{2},1\right]
. After stabilizing all
V_{v_{i}}^{+}
and
W_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{+}
by
(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})^{+}
for some fixed
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, we may assume that
W_{v_{k},v_{k+1}}^{+}\times\left\{\tfrac{1}{2}\right\}=(\mathbb{C}^{2\mathbf{m}})^{+}
for some
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
. Then, following the discussion preceding
5.14
, we obtain a
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-spectrum, which we again denote by
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}))
.

Lemma 5.23
.

\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}))
is
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariantly homotopy equivalent to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Proof.

Since the construction of
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}))
only depends on
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
and the construction of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
only depends on
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
and
\mathcal{H}(\gamma_{0})
are
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariantly homotopy equivalent. This is essentially the same as
4.37
.
∎

5.3.
The weak lifting lemma

Throughout this subsection, we fix a topological group
G
and a finite-index normal subgroup
N
containing the identity component
G_{0}
of
G
. This ensures that
G/N
is a finite discrete group. We omit coefficient rings from the notation unless they are required to state results in full generality. We also adopt the following notation: given a pointed
N
-space
X
, we denote the induced
G
-space by

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}X=X\vee g_{1}X\vee\cdots\vee g_{n}X,

where
G=g_{1}N\sqcup\cdots\sqcup g_{n}N
with
g_{1}=1
and
n=|G/N|
.

Before moving on, we briefly survey some properties of induced spaces and induced maps, following
[
Ada84
, Section 5]
. Given a pointed
G
-space
X
and a pointed
N
-space
Z
, any
N
-equivariant pointed map
f\colon Z\to X
induces a
G
-equivariant map

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f\colon\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z\longrightarrow X,

defined by
(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)(g_{i}x)=g_{i}f(x)
for any
x\in X
.

Furthermore, any
N
-equivariant pointed map
f\colon X\to Z
induces a
G
-equivariant
stable map

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f\colon X\to\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z
as follows:

•

Consider the canonical
G
-equivariant embedding
G/N\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}(V)
, where
V=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}g_{i}
, and define the “duplication” map

\beta\colon V^{+}\longrightarrow V^{+}/(V^{+}\smallsetminus\nu(G/N))\simeq\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}V^{+},

where
\nu(G/N)
is a regular neighborhood of
G/N
in
V
, setwise invariant under
G
.

•

Then we define

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f\colon S^{1}\wedge X\xrightarrow{\;\beta\wedge f\;}\left(\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\cdot S^{1}\right)\wedge Z=\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z,

which can be made
G
-equivariant.

Conversely, given a
G
-equivariant pointed map
f\colon\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z\longrightarrow X
, we restrict to
N
-equivariance and precompose with the inclusion
g_{1}Z\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z
to obtain an
N
-equivariant map
\mathrm{Res}^{N}_{G}f\colon Z\longrightarrow X
.
Similarly, given a
G
-equivariant pointed map
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z
, we restrict to
N
-equivariance and postcompose with the collapsing map

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z\longrightarrow Z,

which collapses
g_{2}Z,\dots,g_{n}Z
to the basepoint, to obtain an
N
-equivariant map
\mathrm{Res}^{N}_{G}f\colon X\longrightarrow Z
.

The operations
\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}
and
\mathrm{Res}^{N}_{G}
are inverses of each other up to equivariant homotopy and therefore induce bijections

[X,Z]^{N}\simeq[X,\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z]^{G},\qquad[Z,X]^{N}\simeq[\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z,X]^{G}.

Remark 5.24
.

For simplicity, in this subsection we will often conflate honest maps with stable maps. This causes no issues, since our focus is on pullback maps between reduced cochain complexes: pullbacks along equivariant stable maps are also well defined in
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(-)
.

Observe that since
N\hookrightarrow G
induces
BN\to BG
, the pullback
C^{\ast}(BG)\to C^{\ast}(BN)
endows
C^{\ast}(BN)
with the structure of a
C^{\ast}(BG)
-algebra. Given any
G
-space
X
, the map

X\times_{N}EG\longrightarrow X\times_{G}EG

is a finite covering. Hence we obtain the pullback

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)\to\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X)

and the
transfer map

\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}X\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X)\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X).

Both of these are
C^{\ast}(BG)
-module maps.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
X
be a pointed
G
-space,
Z
a pointed
N
-space, and
f\colon X\to Z
an
N
-equivariant pointed map. Consider the induced map

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z.

There is a canonical homotopy equivalence

\mathrm{eqv}_{Z}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(Z)\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z)

of
C^{\ast}(BG)
-modules. Then the following square is homotopy commutative:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{eqv}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}X}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)}

Proof.

Consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{eqv}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\mathrm{id}_{X})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{eqv}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}X}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)}

The upper left triangle (which is actually a square) and the right triangle clearly homotopy commute. Thus it remains to show that the bottom triangle also homotopy commutes.

To this end, by replacing
X
with its
G
-Borel construction, we may assume that the
G
-action on
X
is free outside the basepoint (which is
G
-invariant). Under this assumption, equivariant cochain complexes can be canonically identified, up to homotopy equivalence, with the cochain complexes of the quotient spaces. Hence the bottom triangle reduces to the following diagram, where
p_{!}
denotes fiberwise integration along the
G/N
-fibers of
X/N\to X/G
:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}(X/N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{!}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}(X/N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{transfer}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}(X/G)}

Since transfer maps are precisely the fiberwise integration maps for finite coverings, this triangle commutes. The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
X
and
X^{\prime}
be pointed
G
-spaces which are
N
-equivariantly weakly homotopy equivalent, and (non-equivariantly) weakly homotopy equivalent to a sphere. Let
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be an
N
-equivariant pointed map which is a (non-equivariant) homotopy equivalence. Suppose that
G
acts trivially on
H^{\ast}(X)
and
H^{\ast}(X^{\prime})
. Then there exists a homotopy equivalence

f^{\ast}_{G}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X^{\prime})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)

making the following diagram homotopy commutative:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}X}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}_{G}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X^{\prime})}

Proof.

Consider the following diagram, where
\mathrm{Th}
denotes Thom (quasi-)isomorphisms:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Th}_{N}X}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}X}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Th}_{G}X}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(BN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{transfer}}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(BG)}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Th}_{N}X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Th}_{G}X^{\prime}}

Since transfer maps and Thom quasi-isomorphisms are both fiberwise integration maps over the base
BG
, the upper parallelogram and the lower parallelogram are homotopy commutative. Furthermore, since
f
is a non-equivariant homotopy equivalence, the Thom class for the Borel
X^{\prime}
-bundle over
BN
can be pulled back along
f
to obtain a Thom class for the Borel
X
-bundle over
BN
, so the left triangle is also homotopy commutative. Therefore, setting

f^{\ast}_{G}=(\mathrm{Th}_{G}X^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\mathrm{Th}_{G}X,

where
(\mathrm{Th}_{G}X^{\prime})^{-1}
denotes a homotopy inverse of
\mathrm{Th}_{G}X^{\prime}
, proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.27

(Weak lifting lemma)
.

Let
X
and
X^{\prime}
be pointed
G
-spaces, let
Z
be a pointed
N
-space, and let
f\colon Z\to X
and
f^{\prime}\colon Z\to X^{\prime}
be pointed
N
-equivariant maps. Suppose the following conditions hold:

•

\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)
and
\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime})
are (non-equivariantly) weakly homotopy equivalent to a sphere;

•

There exists a pointed
N
-equivariant map
g\colon X\to X^{\prime}
, which is a (non-equivariant) homotopy equivalence, such that
f^{\prime}
is
N
-equivariantly homotopic to
g\circ f
.

Then
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
are quasi-isomorphic as
C^{\ast}(BG;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-modules.

Proof.

Since
\Sigma X
is the mapping cone of
C_{f}\colon\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)\to\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z
induced by
f
, and similarly
\Sigma X^{\prime}
is the mapping cone of
C_{f^{\prime}}\colon\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z
induced by
f^{\prime}
, we obtain from
4.38
that

\begin{split}\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})&\simeq\mathrm{Cone}\!\left((C_{f})^{\ast}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\right)[1],\\
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(X^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}_{2})&\simeq\mathrm{Cone}\!\left((C_{f^{\prime}})^{\ast}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\right)[1].\end{split}

Thus, to prove the lemma, it suffices to construct a quasi-isomorphism

F\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}_{2})

that makes the following diagram homotopy commutative:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(C_{f})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{(C_{f^{\prime}})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}_{2})}

Consider the collapsing map

c\colon\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z=\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Sigma Z\longrightarrow\Sigma Z,

which is
N
-equivariant. Define
C^{0}_{f}=\mathrm{Res}^{N}_{G}C_{f}=c\circ C_{f}
and
C^{0}_{f^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Res}^{N}_{G}C_{f^{\prime}}=c\circ C_{f^{\prime}}
. Then

C_{f}=\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}C^{0}_{f},\qquad C_{f^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}C^{0}_{f^{\prime}}.

Next, consider the map
\tilde{g}\colon\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)\to\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime})
induced by
g
together with a choice of an
N
-equivariant homotopy between
f^{\prime}
and
g\circ f
. Since
g
is (non-equivariantly) a homotopy equivalence,
\tilde{g}
is also a homotopy equivalence. The following diagram is therefore
N
-equivariantly homotopy commutative:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{C_{f}}
\scriptstyle{C^{0}_{f}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{\Sigma Z}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C_{f^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{C^{0}_{f^{\prime}}}

In particular,
C^{0}_{f}
is
N
-equivariantly homotopic to
C^{0}_{f^{\prime}}\circ\tilde{g}
. Since
\tilde{g}
is an
N
-equivariant map that is a (non-equivariant) homotopy equivalence, precomposing with the equivalence

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(\Sigma Z;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}\Sigma Z;\mathbb{Z}_{2})

and applying
5.25
, we reduce the problem to making the following diagram homotopy commutative:

\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(\Sigma Z;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(C^{0}_{f})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{(C^{0}_{f^{\prime}})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{N}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}^{G}_{N}\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{G}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}_{2})}

Here, the middle vertical map is the pullback along
\tilde{g}\colon\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f)\to\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{N}f^{\prime})
induced by
g\colon X\to X^{\prime}
. By
5.26
, there exists a quasi-isomorphism
F
making the right square homotopy commutative. Furthermore, since
C^{0}_{f}
is
N
-equivariantly homotopic to
C^{0}_{f^{\prime}}\circ\tilde{g}
, the left triangle is also homotopy commutative. The lemma follows.
∎

We can now prove that the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant lattice homotopy type and the
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant Seiberg–Witten Floer homotopy type have quasi-isomorphic
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-coefficient cochain complexes over
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Note that we are not claiming that they are
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariantly homotopy equivalent. Nevertheless, this weaker statement suffices for our purposes.

Lemma 5.28
.

Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
be a self-conjugate
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
Y
. Suppose there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant almost
J
-invariant path
\gamma
for
(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
which carries the lattice homology of
(\Gamma,\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))
. Then the
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-modules

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\gamma);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\qquad\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{2})

are quasi-isomorphic, up to a degree shift and reparametrization of
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
by pullback along the automorphism

\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{(x,[n])\mapsto(x\cdot(-1)^{n},[n])}\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},

where
-1
denotes the order-two element of the identity component of
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
.

Proof.

Write the given path
\gamma
as

\mathfrak{s}_{-n},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{-1},\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}.

Denote its latter half, i.e.
\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}
, by
\gamma_{0}
. Then we have an
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant inclusion

i\colon\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\gamma_{0})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\gamma).

By construction,
\mathrm{Cone}(i)
is homotopy equivalent to a sphere. Recall from
5.16
that, after suitable reparametrization (of
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
) and suspension, there exists an
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant map

\mathcal{T}\colon\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\gamma)\longrightarrow SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})

which is a (non-equivariant) homotopy equivalence.
19
19

19

It is in fact an
S^{1}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence, but this refinement is irrelevant here.

Consider the composite

\mathcal{T}\circ i\colon\mathcal{H}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\gamma_{0})\longrightarrow SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}).

Then

\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{T}\circ i)\simeq\mathrm{Cone}(i)\simeq\text{(sphere)}.

Therefore, applying
5.27
yields the desired quasi-isomorphism.
∎

5.4.
The
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lattice chain model

We define
\mathfrak{R}=(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U,Q,\theta],d)
with
dU=Q^{3}
, where
\deg\theta=\deg Q=1
and
\deg U=2
. By
,
\mathfrak{R}
is quasi-isomorphic to
C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-dga.
20
20

20

The authors first learned of this fact through a private conversation with Matthew Stoffregen.
It follows that the group automorphism
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
induces the automorphism of
\mathfrak{R}\simeq C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
given by

\varphi\colon\mathfrak{R}\xrightarrow{U\mapsto U+\theta^{2}}\mathfrak{R}.

Thus, for any
\mathfrak{R}
-module
C
, we may compose its
\mathfrak{R}
-module structure, i.e., the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-dga morphism
\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(C)
, with
\varphi
to obtain a new
\mathfrak{R}
-module structure. We call this process
twisting
. Note that
\varphi
is precisely the pullback map along the automorphism

\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{(x,[n])\mapsto(x\cdot(-1)^{n},[n])}\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},

where
-1
denotes the unique order-two element in the identity component of
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
.

Lemma 5.29
.

Under the identification
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\simeq\mathfrak{R}
of quasi-isomorphic
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-dgas, there is a quasi-isomorphism

C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\simeq\mathfrak{R}/(Q)

of
\mathfrak{R}
-bimodules.

Proof.

The claim follows from the fact that
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is formal and that its homology, as a graded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-algebra, is freely generated by a single degree-2 element.
∎

Lemma 5.30
.

For any finite-dimensional
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-representation
V
, there is an isomorphism

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V^{+});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)\simeq(\mathfrak{R}x_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{R}x_{-},d)

of
\mathfrak{R}
-bimodules, where
dx_{+}=dx_{-}=Q(x_{+}+x_{-})
. Furthermore, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and the corresponding inclusion map
U_{\alpha}\colon S^{0}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha})^{+}
, consider the doubled map

U_{\alpha}\vee j\cdot U_{\alpha}\colon\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(S^{0})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}((\mathbb{C}_{\alpha})^{+}),

which is
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant. Its pullback is

(U_{\alpha}\vee j\cdot U_{\alpha})^{\ast}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}((\mathbb{C}_{\alpha})^{+});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(S^{0});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right).

Then, under the identifications of both domain and codomain with
(\mathfrak{R}x_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{R}x_{-},d)
, the map
(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}U_{\alpha})^{\ast}
is given up to homotopy by

x_{+}\longmapsto(U+\alpha\theta^{2})x_{+},\qquad x_{-}\longmapsto(U+\alpha\theta^{2})x_{-}+Q^{2}x_{+}.

Proof.

Since

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V^{+});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)\simeq\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V^{+};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

and
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V^{+};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U]
as
C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-modules, it follows from
5.29
that, as
\mathfrak{R}
-modules,

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V^{+});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)\simeq\mathfrak{R}/(Q).

The first part of the lemma then follows from the fact that
(\mathfrak{R}x_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{R}x_{-},d)
is a free resolution of
\mathfrak{R}/(Q)
.

Next, observe that under the identification of both the domain and codomain of
(U_{\alpha}\vee j\cdot U_{\alpha})^{\ast}
with
\mathfrak{R}/(Q)
, we have

(U_{\alpha}\vee j\cdot U_{\alpha})^{\ast}(1)=U+\alpha\theta^{2}

by
4.41
. To prove the second part of the lemma, it remains to verify that the stated map is a chain map and that it induces multiplication by
U+\alpha\theta^{2}
in homology. This is a straightforward computation, which we leave to the reader.
∎

Lemma 5.31
.

Let
S^{0}
denote the trivial
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-representation sphere. Consider the induced (stable) map
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{id}
of the identity map
\mathrm{id}\colon S^{0}\to S^{0}
, and its pullback

(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{id})^{\ast}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(S^{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

Under the identification of its domain with
(\mathfrak{R}x_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{R}x_{-},d)
(as in
5.30
) and of its codomain with
\mathfrak{R}
via the Thom isomorphism, this pullback is given up to homotopy by

(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathrm{id}))^{\ast}(x_{+})=(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathrm{id}))^{\ast}(x_{-})=1.

Furthermore, if we consider the induced map in the reverse direction, i.e.,

\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{id}\colon\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{0}\longrightarrow S^{0},

then its pullback is given by

(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathrm{id}))^{\ast}(1)=x_{+}+x_{-}.

Proof.

This is a straightforward computation.
∎

Remark 5.32
.

In
5.30
, the
Q^{2}
terms in the pullback maps are nonsymmetric with respect to the symmetry
x_{+}\leftrightarrow x_{-}
, which may seem counterintuitive. However, one can verify that moving the
Q^{2}
terms to the “opposite” side yields a homotopic map. To see this, consider the endomorphism
F
of
(\mathfrak{R}x_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{R}x_{-},d)
defined by

F(x_{+})=Q^{2}x_{-},\qquad F(x_{-})=Q^{2}x_{+}.

We claim that
F
is nullhomotopic. Indeed, define a (non-dg)
\mathfrak{R}
-linear endomorphism
H
by

H(x_{+})=Qx_{+}.

Then

\begin{split}(dH+Hd)(x_{+})&=d(Qx_{+})+H(Qx_{+}+Qx_{-})=Q^{2}x_{-},\\
(dH+Hd)(x_{-})&=d(Qx_{-})+H(Qx_{+}+Qx_{-})=Q^{2}x_{+}.\end{split}

Hence
F=dH+Hd
is nullhomotopic.

Now we are ready to define the
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lattice chain model directly from symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded roots. For simplicity, for
\mathbf{n}=n_{+}\cdot[0]+n_{-}\cdot[1]
with
n_{+},n_{-}\geq 0
, set

U_{Q}^{\mathbf{n}}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }n_{+}=n_{-}=0,\\
n_{+}U^{n_{+}-1}&\text{if }n_{+}>0\text{ and }n_{-}=0,\\
n_{-}(U+\theta^{2})^{n_{-}-1}&\text{if }n_{+}=0\text{ and }n_{-}>0,\\
n_{+}U^{n_{+}-1}(U+\theta^{2})^{n_{-}}+n_{-}U^{n_{+}}(U+\theta^{2})^{n_{-}-1}&\text{if }n_{+},n_{-}>0.\end{cases}

For brevity, denote the
\mathfrak{R}
-module
(\mathfrak{R}x\oplus\mathfrak{R}y,d)
from
5.30
by
\mathfrak{M}
.

Recall that
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
, defined in
5.22
, may have either a central vertex or a central angle. Suppose first that it has a central vertex. In this case, we may label its leaves as

v_{-n},\dots,v_{-1},v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n},

where each pair of consecutive leaves forms a simple angle. Note that
v_{i}
is the reflection of
v_{-i}
along the
y
-axis for all
-n\leq i\leq n
. We then define
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
to be the following
\mathfrak{R}
-bimodule:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{2}^{-}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{+}}

The maps
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{\pm}
are defined as follows:

•

\mathfrak{f}_{0}(x_{+})=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{0},v_{1})}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{0}(x_{-})=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{0},v_{1})}+Q^{2}U_{Q}^{\lambda_{A}(v_{0},v_{1})}
.

•

For
2\leq i\leq n
, set

\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{-}(x_{+})=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i-1},v_{i})}x_{+},\qquad\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{-}(x_{-})=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i-1},v_{i})}x_{-}+Q^{2}U_{Q}^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i-1},v_{i})}x_{+}.

•

For
1\leq i\leq n
, set

\begin{split}\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{+}(x_{+})&=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i-1},v_{i})+\lambda_{V}(v_{i-1})-\lambda_{V}(v_{i})}x_{+},\\
\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{+}(x_{-})&=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i-1},v_{i})+\lambda_{V}(v_{i-1})-\lambda_{V}(v_{i})}x_{-}+Q^{2}U_{Q}^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i-1},v_{i})+\lambda_{V}(v_{i-1})-\lambda_{V}(v_{i})}x_{+}.\end{split}

Now suppose that
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
has a central angle. Then we may label its leaves as

v_{-n},\dots,v_{-1},v_{1},\dots,v_{n}

where any pair of consecutive leaves form a simple angle; note that
v_{i}
is the reflection of
v_{-i}
along the
y
-axis for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Then we define
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
as the following
\mathfrak{R}
-bimodule.

\textstyle{\mathfrak{R}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{-}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{n-1}^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{n-1}^{+}}

Here, the maps
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
and

\mathfrak{f}_{0}(1)=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{-1},v_{1})}x_{+}+(U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{-1},v_{1})}+Q^{2}U_{Q}^{\lambda_{A}(v_{-1},v_{1})})x_{-}
