, we define

\begin{split}\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{+}(x_{+})&=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i},v_{i+1})+\lambda_{V}(v_{i})-\lambda_{V}(v_{i+1})}x_{+},\\
\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{+}(x_{-})&=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i},v_{i+1})+\lambda_{V}(v_{i})-\lambda_{V}(v_{i+1})}x_{-}+Q^{2}U_{Q}^{\lambda_{A}(v_{i},v_{i+1})+\lambda_{V}(v_{i})-\lambda_{V}(v_{i+1})}x_{+}.\end{split}

Definition 5.33
.

The
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant lattice cochain
of
(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is defined to be the
\mathfrak{R}
-bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
.

As in the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariant case, the lattice cochain
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
computes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-coefficient
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant cochain complex of
SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(-Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Lemma 5.34
.

For any self-conjugate
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
satisfying
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})=\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
, the
\mathfrak{R}
-bimodules

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\left(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)

are quasi-isomorphic, up to a degree shift and possibly a twisting.

Proof.

By
4.21
,
5.23
, and
5.28
, it remains to show that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
and
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}));\mathbb{Z}_{2})
are quasi-isomorphic. We only present the case where
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
has an invariant leaf; the case of an invariant angle is analogous and omitted.

Suppose that
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}})
has an invariant leaf. Label its leaves

v_{-n},\dots,v_{-1},v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n},

where each pair of consecutive leaves forms a simple angle. Consider the following
\mathfrak{R}
-module, denoted by
\mathcal{C}
:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{2}^{-}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{+}}

Here the maps
\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{\pm}
are the same as in the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, except that
\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{-}
is now given by

\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{-}(x_{+})=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{0},v_{1})}x_{+},\qquad\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{-}(x_{-})=U^{\lambda_{A}(v_{0},v_{1})}x_{-}+Q^{2}U_{Q}^{\lambda_{A}(v_{0},v_{1})}x_{+}.

Applying
4.38
together with
5.30
and
5.31
, we find that
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}));\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is quasi-isomorphic to the
\mathfrak{R}
-module

\mathfrak{N}=\bigl[\mathfrak{R}\xleftarrow{g_{1}}\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{g_{2}}\mathcal{C}\bigr],

where

•

g_{1}(x_{+})=g_{1}(x_{-})=1
;

•

g_{2}
is the inclusion of the codomain of
\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{-}
into
\mathcal{C}
.

Observe that
\mathfrak{N}
contains the acyclic submodule

\mathcal{A}=\bigl[\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{g_{1}\oplus g_{2}}\mathrm{Im}(g_{1}\oplus g_{2})\subset\mathfrak{R}\oplus\mathcal{C}\subset\mathfrak{N}\bigr].

It follows that
\mathfrak{N}/\mathcal{A}
coincides with
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, except that in the map
\mathfrak{f}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{R}
the
Q^{2}
term arises from
x_{+}
rather than
x_{-}
. By
5.32
, this does not affect the quasi-isomorphism class. The lemma follows.
∎

5.5.
The chain-level
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-local equivalence group

Recall that while
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is an
E_{\infty}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, when regarded as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra (i.e., a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-dga), it is homotopy equivalent to
\mathfrak{R}
. Consequently, their derived categories of left, right, or bimodules are equivalent. Hence, whenever the full
E_{\infty}
-structure is not required (for example, when computing the homology of an
E_{\infty}
-module), we will treat
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
\mathfrak{R}
as the “same”
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-dga. In particular, we will define the chain-level local equivalence group using the derived category of perfect
\mathfrak{R}
-modules, closely following the constructions in
Section

4.7
. Note that

H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{R})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,V]/(Q^{3}),

where
\deg V=4
and
V
corresponds to
U^{2}
, since
U
itself is not a cocycle in
\mathfrak{R}
.

Consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-dga
\mathfrak{R}_{0}:=(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,U,U^{-1}],d)
, where

dQ=0,\qquad dU^{n}=\begin{cases}nQ^{3}U^{n-1}&\text{if }n\neq 0,\\
0&\text{if }n=0.\end{cases}

Although
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
does not appear to admit a natural structure of an
\mathfrak{R}
-algebra in the
E_{\infty}
sense, when regarded as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, it carries the structure of an
A_{\infty}

\mathfrak{R}
–
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
-bimodule. Moreover,

H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,V,V^{-1}]/(Q^{3}).

Throughout, all maps are assumed to be degree-preserving unless stated otherwise.

Definition 5.35
.

An
\mathfrak{R}
-module
M
is said to be of
weak SWF-type
if

M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}\simeq\mathfrak{R}_{0}[n]

as an
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
-module for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Given two
\mathfrak{R}
-modules
M,N
of weak SWF-type, an
\mathfrak{R}
-module map
f\colon M\to N
is called
local of level
i
for
i\in\{0,1,2\}
if

f\otimes\mathrm{id}\colon M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}\longrightarrow N\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}

is homotopic to
Q^{i}\cdot f^{\prime}
for some
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
-module quasi-isomorphism
f^{\prime}
. Two
\mathfrak{R}
-modules
M,N
of weak SWF-type are said to be
weakly locally equivalent
if there exist local maps
M\to N[n]
and
N\to M[m]
of level
0
for some integers
m,n
.

An
\mathfrak{R}
-module of weak SWF-type is said to be of
SWF-type
if it is perfect and weakly locally equivalent to
\mathfrak{R}
. Finally, two
\mathfrak{R}
-modules
M,N
of SWF-type are said to be
locally equivalent
if there exist local maps
M\to N
and
N\to M
, both of level
0
.

Then, by following the arguments in the proofs of various lemmas in
Section

4.7
with minimal modifications, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 5.36
.

The following statements hold.

(1)

Consider the set

\mathfrak{C}^{ch,\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}:=\frac{\{\mathfrak{R}\text{-modules of SWF-type}\}}{\text{local equivalence}},

endowed with the group operation given by tensor product. Then
\mathfrak{C}^{ch,\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is an abelian group.

(2)

The monoidal functor

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\colon\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^{op}_{C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})}

induces a group homomorphism

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\colon\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}},

and hence also a group homomorphism

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\colon\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

by composing with the Borel construction map
\mathfrak{B}\colon\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

(3)

For any space
X
of type
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-SWF and its additive inverse
X^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, the
\mathfrak{R}
-modules

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{\vee}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X^{\vee};\mathbb{Z}_{2})

are locally equivalent.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from the arguments of
4.51
and
4.53
and
4.54
.
∎

However, unlike the
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
case, here we encounter the notion of
levels
of local maps. Among these, only local maps of level
0
correspond to the “true” local maps in the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant sense.

Lemma 5.37
.

Let
M,N,L
be
\mathfrak{R}
-modules of SWF-type, and let
f\colon M\to N
and
g\colon N\to L
be
\mathfrak{R}
-module maps. Choose integers
i,j\in\{0,1,2\}
such that
i+j\leq 2
. Then any two of the following statements imply the third:

•

f
is a local map of level
i
;

•

g
is a local map of level
j
;

•

g\circ f
is a local map of level
i+j
.

Proof.

If the first two statements hold, then so does the third.
Suppose that the first and third statements hold. Then there exist quasi-isomorphisms

f_{0}\colon M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}\longrightarrow N\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0},\qquad h_{0}\colon M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}\longrightarrow L\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}

such that
f\otimes\mathrm{id}\sim Q^{i}f_{0}
and
(g\circ f)\otimes\mathrm{id}\sim Q^{i+j}h_{0}
. Take a homotopy inverse
f_{0}^{-1}
of
f_{0}
. Then

Q^{i}(g\otimes\mathrm{id})\sim((g\circ f)\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ f_{0}^{-1}\sim Q^{i+j}\bigl(h_{0}\circ f_{0}^{-1}\bigr).

Since

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathfrak{R}_{0}}(M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0},\,N\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathfrak{R}_{0}}(\mathfrak{R}_{0}[m],\mathfrak{R}_{0}[n])\cong\mathfrak{R}[n-m]

for some integers
m,n
, the homotopy classes of maps between
M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}
and
N\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}
can be viewed as elements of
H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,V,V^{-1}]/(Q^{3})
. Thus, we obtain

Q^{i}\cdot[g\otimes\mathrm{id}]=Q^{i+j}\cdot[h_{0}\circ f_{0}^{-1}]

in
H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})
. Since
i+j\leq 2
, it follows that

[g\otimes\mathrm{id}]=Q^{j}\cdot[h_{0}\circ f_{0}^{-1}]+Q^{j+1}c

for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,V,V^{-1}]/(Q^{3})
.

Because
f_{0}
and
h_{0}
are quasi-isomorphisms, their composition
h_{0}\circ f_{0}^{-1}
is also a quasi-isomorphism, hence corresponds to a homogeneous invertible element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,V,V^{-1}]/(Q^{3})
. The only invertible homogeneous elements in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,V,V^{-1}]/(Q^{3})
are powers of
V
, so we may write
[h_{0}\circ f_{0}^{-1}]=V^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since no homogeneous element
c
can satisfy
\deg Q^{j+1}c=\deg Q^{j}V^{k}
, it follows that
c=0
. Hence

g\otimes\mathrm{id}\sim Q^{j}\cdot(h_{0}\circ f_{0}^{-1}).

Therefore
g
is a local map of level
j
. The case when the second and third statements hold is analogous.
∎

Lemma 5.38
.

Let
X,Y
be spaces of type
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-SWF, and let
f\colon X\to Y
be a local map of level
i
for some
i\in\{0,1,2\}
. Then the induced pullback

f^{\ast}\colon\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})

is also a local map of level
i
.

Proof.

Since the map
\mathfrak{R}\to\mathfrak{R}_{0}
factors through
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,U],d)
with
dU=Q^{3}
, and the factoring map

\mathfrak{R}\xrightarrow{\;\theta\mapsto 0\;}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,U],d)

is induced by the map

C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

which “forgets” the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariance, we obtain the following natural quasi-isomorphisms of
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
-modules (up to mild abuse of notation):

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}\;\simeq\;\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

The claim follows immediately from this observation.
∎

Recall that we defined the Frøyshov invariants
\delta,\delta^{(p)}_{0}
for
\mathcal{R}_{p}
-modules of SWF-type in
Section

4.7
. The dga morphism

C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow C^{\ast}(B(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

induced by the inclusion
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, is identified with

\mathfrak{R}\xrightarrow{Q=0}\mathcal{R}_{2}=(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[U,\theta],d=0),

which describes the canonical
\mathfrak{R}
-algebra structure on
\mathcal{R}_{2}
induced by the inclusion
S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. As in the proof of
5.38
, for any space
X
of type
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-SWF we have a natural quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{R}_{2}
-modules:

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{R}_{2}\simeq\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

Equivalently, there is a commutative diagram of abelian groups in which the top map is the “forgetful map” that retains only the
(S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-subaction of the given
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-action:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{R}_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.}

Thus, we may abuse notation and write

\delta(M)=\delta(M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{R}_{2}),\qquad\delta^{(2)}_{0}(M)=\delta^{(2)}_{0}(M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{R}_{2}),

so that for any
X\in\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
we have

\begin{split}\delta(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))&=\delta(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))=\delta(X),\\
\delta^{(2)}_{0}(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))&=\delta^{(2)}_{0}(\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))=\delta^{(2)}_{0}(X).\end{split}

Finally, we consider the relation between
\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and the strict families local equivalence group. We begin with the functor

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\colon\mathcal{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^{op}_{C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})}

defined in
Section

2.5.1
. Since

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})=C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(\mathfrak{B}(-);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

we obtain a well-defined group homomorphism

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\colon\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.

Following the notion of
k
-stable local triviality for elements of
\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 5.39
.

Two elements
[(X,r)],[(Y,s)]\in\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
are said to be
locally equivalent
if
r-s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
X
is locally equivalent to
Y[r-s]
.

Furthermore, given an integer
k\geq 0
, an element
[(X,r)]\in\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is said to be
k
-stably locally trivial
if
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and there exist local maps of level
k
between
X[r]
and
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

Then we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.40
.

Let
k\in\{0,1,2\}
. For any
k
-stably locally trivial element
X\in\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
,
its image

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

is also
k
-stably locally trivial.

Proof.

We begin by recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
:

\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=\mathrm{Im}\!\left(\mathfrak{B}\colon\mathfrak{C}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}^{sp}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\right).

Hence any element
X\in\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
can be written as
X=(\mathfrak{B}(X_{0}),r)
for some space
X_{0}
of type
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-SWF. Since
X
is
k
-stably locally trivial, we know that
r\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For simplicity, we assume
r=0
, so that
X
is given as the fibration

X_{0}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}E\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow B\mathbb{Z}_{2};

the general case can be treated in the same way. Note that
(X_{0})^{S^{1}}
may be taken to be
V^{+}
for some finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-representation
V
.
For convenience, denote the induced
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector bundle
V\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}E\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by
E
, and let
E_{n}
denote its restriction to the
n
-skeleton of
B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Likewise, denote the restriction of the
X_{0}
-bundle
X_{0}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}E\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
to
(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{n}
by
X_{n}
.

By the definition of
k
-stable local triviality, for any integer
N\geq 0
there exists, after a suspension by some
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-vector bundle over
(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{N}
of sufficiently high rank, a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector bundle
F_{N}
containing
E_{N}
as a subbundle such that each fiber of
F_{N}/E_{N}
is given by
\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, together with a bundle map

f_{N}\colon X_{N}\longrightarrow F_{N}^{+}

whose
S^{1}
-fixed locus map

F_{N}^{S^{1}}\colon E_{X}(L)=(X\!\mid_{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{N}})^{S^{1}}\longrightarrow F_{X}(L)

is induced by the inclusion
E_{N}\hookrightarrow F_{N}
.

By considering the inclusions
\ast=(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{0}\hookrightarrow(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{N}\hookrightarrow B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and taking
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-equivariant singular cochains, we obtain the following commutative diagram of
E_{\infty}
-algebras over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(indeed,
C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-algebras), where
s
denotes the rank of
E
:

\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{N}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{0}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]}

Observe that
C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is itself an
E_{\infty}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, that
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is a module over it, and that
f_{N}
is a map of such modules. Hence the homotopy class of
f_{N}^{\ast}
is determined by the cohomology class

[f_{N}^{\ast}(1)]\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

Since
X_{0}
is a finite
\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spectrum, the restriction map

\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})

is an isomorphism whenever
N
is sufficiently large. Choosing such an
N
, we obtain a cohomology class

\alpha\in\widetilde{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})

mapping to
[f_{N}^{\ast}(1)]
. This class
\alpha
then induces (up to homotopy) a
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-module map

f\colon C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s],

making the following diagram homotopy-commutative:

\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{N}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{0}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]}

Recall that we are identifying
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
with the commutative differential graded algebra
\mathfrak{R}
. Then tensoring with
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
gives the following homotopy commutative diagram. Here,
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
denotes the differential graded algebra
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,U],d)
with
dU=Q^{3}
, which is quasi-isomorphic to (and hence identified with)
C^{\ast}(B\mathrm{Pin}(2);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

\textstyle{\mathfrak{R}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{R}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}^{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}}

It is clear that the left vertical map is the identity. Moreover, since tensoring with
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
over
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
has the effect of forgetting the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-equivariant cochains, the right vertical map can also be identified with the identity.

Furthermore, since
f_{0}
can be written as the inclusion
V\hookrightarrow V\oplus\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, the bottom map is multiplication by
Q^{k}
under the identification

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}=\mathfrak{R}_{0}[-s].

It follows that, under the identification

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})[-s]\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}=\mathfrak{R}_{0}[-s],

the top map
f\otimes\mathrm{id}
is homotopic to multiplication by
Q^{k}
. Thus
f
is a local map of level
k
.
A similar argument also shows that a local map of level
k
exists in the reverse direction. The lemma follows.
∎

5.6.
Example: an explicit computation for
\Sigma(3,5,19)

Consider
Y=\Sigma(3,5,19)
, which is a Seifert homology sphere. In
Section

4.8
, we computed

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(SWF_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(Y);\mathbb{Z}_{p})

for various primes
p
, up to quasi-isomorphism and twisting. In this subsection, we will compute

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y);\mathbb{Z}_{2})

up to quasi-isomorphism and twisting.

Define an
\mathfrak{R}
-module
M=(C,d)
, where
C
is freely generated over
\mathfrak{R}
by elements
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
for
|i|\leq 5
and
0<|j|<5
. The differential
d
is defined on generators as follows:

\begin{split}dx_{0}&=(U+\theta^{2})y_{1}+(U+\theta^{2}+Q^{2})y_{-1},\\
dx_{1}&=Uy_{1}+(U+\theta^{2})y_{2}+Q^{2}(y_{-1}+y_{-2})+Q(x_{1}+x_{-1}),\\
dx_{-1}&=Uy_{-1}+(U+\theta^{2})y_{-2}+Q(x_{1}+x_{-1}),\\
dx_{2}&=Uy_{2}+U^{2}y_{3}+Q^{2}y_{-2}+Q(x_{2}+x_{-2}),\\
dx_{-2}&=Uy_{-2}+U^{2}y_{-3}+Q(x_{2}+x_{-2}),\\
dx_{3}&=(U+\theta^{2})y_{3}+U(U^{2}+\theta^{4})y_{4}+Q^{2}y_{-3}+Q^{2}(U^{2}+\theta^{4})y_{-4}+Q(x_{3}+x_{-3}),\\
dx_{-3}&=(U+\theta^{2})y_{-3}+U(U^{2}+\theta^{4})y_{-4}+Q(x_{3}+x_{-3}),\\
dx_{4}&=Uy_{4}+U^{2}(U^{2}+\theta^{4})y_{5}+Q^{2}y_{-4}+Q(x_{4}+x_{-4}),\\
dx_{-4}&=Uy_{-4}+U^{2}(U^{2}+\theta^{4})y_{-5}+Q(x_{4}+x_{-4}),\\
dx_{5}&=(U+\theta^{2})y_{5}+Q^{2}y_{-5}+Q(x_{5}+x_{-5}),\\
dx_{-5}&=(U+\theta^{2})y_{-5}+Q(x_{5}+x_{-5}),\\
dy_{j}&=Q(y_{j}+y_{-j})\quad\text{for all }j.\end{split}

Here the degree is given by
\deg x_{0}=0
. Then we have the following theorem.

Theorem 5.41
.

After a possible twisting (but no degree shift), the
\mathfrak{R}
-module

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y);\mathbb{Z}_{2})

is quasi-isomorphic to
M
as an
A_{\infty}

\mathfrak{R}
-bimodule.

Proof.

Since
Y
is a homology sphere, we have
|H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|=1
, which is odd. As
Y
carries a unique
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure, namely
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
, the canonical
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure of
Y
is self-conjugate. Moreover, because
3,5,19
are all odd, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
Y
given by the subaction of the Seifert
S^{1}
-action is free. Hence, by
5.34
, the cochain complex
\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
can be determined directly from the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-labelled planar graded root of
(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, where
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
is any of the two self-conjugate
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lifts of
\mathfrak{s}^{can}_{Y}
, already computed in
Section

4.8
. This computation yields

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\simeq M[m]

for some degree shift
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, possibly up to twisting. Thus it remains to show that
m=0
.

To prove this, we compare the Frøyshov invariants of
Y
and
M
. By construction,
\delta(M)=\tfrac{1}{2}\deg x_{0}=0
. On the other hand, since
Y
bounds a smooth contractible
4
-manifold
[
fintushel1981exotic
]
, we have
\delta(Y)=0
. Therefore, applying
4.56
, we obtain

0=\delta(Y)=\delta(M[m])=-\tfrac{m}{2}+\delta(M)=-\tfrac{m}{2},

which implies
m=0
, as desired.
∎

Remark 5.42
.

Consider the projection map
p\colon M\rightarrow\mathfrak{R}
defined by

p(x_{0})=1,\qquad p(x_{i})=p(y_{i})=0\quad\text{for all }i\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3,\pm 4,\pm 5\}.

It is straightforward to see that
p
is a local map of level
0
.
On the other hand, there is no local map of level
0
from
\mathfrak{R}
to
M
.
However, the following map is a local map of level
1
:

f(1)=Qx_{0}+(U+\theta^{2})y_{-1}\colon\mathfrak{R}\longrightarrow M.

This illustrates a more general phenomenon, which will be discussed in
.

For the reader’s convenience, we include here the chart of

\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}}
.

6.
Dehn twists and stabilizations

6.1.
The connected sum argument

In this subsection, we develop the “connected sum technique”, which allows us to obstruct a boundary Dehn twist of a 4-manifold
X
from being isotopic to the identity rel. boundary by considering the problem to an analogous one for a connected sum
X\#\cdots\#X
.
Whenever we have an embedded
3
-sphere
S
with trivial normal bundle in a
4
-manifold
X
, we denote the Dehn twist of
X
along
S
by
T_{S}
, corresponding to the unique nontrivial element of
\pi_{1}\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(S^{3})\cong\pi_{1}SO(4)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In fact, the notion of Dehn twist generalizes to higher dimensions, and the following results hold for general
n
-manifolds
X
with
n\geq 3
, where the Dehn twist of
X
along an embedded
(n{-}1)
-sphere
S
refers to the one corresponding to the unique nontrivial element of
\pi_{1}SO(n)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Throughout this subsection, we write
T\sim T^{\prime}
to indicate that the diffeomorphisms
T
and
T^{\prime}
are smoothly isotopic rel. boundary.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth, simply-connected
n
-manifold with
n\geq 3
, possibly with boundary. Choose a point
p\in\mathrm{int}(X)
, and let
X^{0}:=X\smallsetminus\nu(p)
, where
\nu(p)
is an open ball neighborhood of
p
, so that

\partial X^{0}=\partial X\sqcup S^{n-1}.

Let
Y
be a closed, smooth
(n{-}1)
-manifold, and fix a class
\phi\in\pi_{1}\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(Y)
based at the identity. Suppose we are given embeddings

f,g\colon Y\hookrightarrow X^{0}

that are isotopic in
X
and have orientable normal bundles. Then there exists an element
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, depending only on
X
and
\phi
, such that the Dehn twists
T_{X^{0},f(Y),\phi}
and
T_{X^{0},g(Y),\phi}
along
f(Y)
and
g(Y)
, respectively, defined via
\phi
, satisfy the relation

T_{X^{0},f(Y),\phi}\sim T_{X^{0},g(Y),\phi}\circ T_{S^{n-1}}^{\alpha}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial X^{0}.

Proof.

While this is essentially
[
auckly2015stable
, Proposition 5.2]
, we include the proof here in our setting for the sake of self-containedness. Denote by
D
the closure of
\nu(p)
; note that
D\cap f(Y)=D\cap g(Y)=\emptyset
. Let
\mathrm{Emb}^{0}(D^{n},X)
denote the space of smooth embeddings of the
n
-dimensional closed disk
D^{n}
into the interior of
X
. Then we have the following Serre fibration:

\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X,D\sqcup\partial X)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X,\partial X)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Emb}^{0}(D^{n},X).

This yields the associated long exact sequence on homotopy groups:

\pi_{1}\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X,D\sqcup\partial X)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X,\partial X)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}\mathrm{Emb}^{0}(D^{n},X).

We have canonical identifications

\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X,D\sqcup\partial X)\cong\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X^{0},\partial X^{0}),\qquad\pi_{1}\mathrm{Emb}^{0}(D^{n},X)\cong\pi_{1}\mathrm{Fr}(\mathrm{int}(X)),

where
\mathrm{int}(X)
denotes the interior of
X
, and
\mathrm{Fr}(\mathrm{int}(X))
its frame bundle. Since
X
is simply-connected, so is
\mathrm{int}(X)
, and we have a natural surjection

\pi_{1}SO(n)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}\mathrm{Fr}(\mathrm{int}(X)).

The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
n
be a positive integer with
n\geq 3
, and denote by
S^{n}_{4}
the
n
-sphere with 4 open
n
-balls removed. Let
S_{1},\dots,S_{4}
denote the four boundary components of
S^{n}_{4}
. Then the composition

T_{S_{1}}\circ T_{S_{2}}\circ T_{S_{3}}\circ T_{S_{4}}

is smoothly isotopic to the identity rel.
\partial S^{n}_{4}
.

Proof.

Denote by
S^{n}_{3}
the
n
-sphere with three open
n
-balls removed. Consider two copies
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
S^{n}_{3}
, and denote their boundary components by
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
and
S^{\prime}_{1},S^{\prime}_{2},S^{\prime}_{3}
, respectively. Then we have

S^{n}_{4}\cong X\sqcup_{S_{3}=S^{\prime}_{3}}X^{\prime};

under this identification, the four components of
\partial S^{n}_{4}
are
S_{1},S_{2},S^{\prime}_{1},S^{\prime}_{2}
. By
6.1
, there exists some
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
such that

T_{S_{1}}\circ T_{S_{2}}\sim T_{S_{3}}^{\alpha}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial X.

Since
X^{\prime}
is another copy of
X
, we likewise have

T_{S^{\prime}_{1}}\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{2}}\sim T_{S^{\prime}_{3}}^{\alpha}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial X^{\prime}.

Hence, in
S^{n}_{4}
, we obtain

T_{S_{1}}\circ T_{S_{2}}\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{1}}\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{2}}\sim T_{S_{3}}^{\alpha}\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{3}}^{\alpha}\sim\mathrm{id}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial S^{n}_{4},

since
S_{3}=S^{\prime}_{3}
and Dehn twists along an embedded
(n{-}1)
-sphere have order
2
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
n,m
be positive integers with
n\geq 3
, and denote by
S^{n}_{2m}
the
n
-sphere with
2m
open
n
-balls removed. Let
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{2m}
be its boundary components, and let
T_{S_{i}}
denote the Dehn twist along
S_{i}
. Then

T_{S_{1}}\circ T_{S_{2}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{S_{2m}}.

is smoothly isotopic to the identity rel.
\partial S^{n}_{2m}
.

Proof.

If
m=1
, the corollary is immediate, since
S_{1}
is isotopic to
S_{2}
. If
m=2
, the result follows from
6.2
. Now suppose that the corollary holds for some
m\geq 2
. Consider the decomposition

S^{n}_{2(m+1)}=S^{n}_{2m}\sqcup_{S=S^{\prime}}S^{n}_{4},

where
S
and
S^{\prime}
are boundary components of
S^{n}_{2m}
and
S^{n}_{4}
, respectively. By the inductive hypothesis, the composition
F
of Dehn twists along all boundary components of
S^{n}_{2m}
is smoothly isotopic to the identity relative to
\partial S^{n}_{2m}
. Similarly, let
G
be the composition of Dehn twists along all boundary components of
S^{n}_{4}
. By
6.2
,
G
is smoothly isotopic to the identity relative to
\partial S^{n}_{4}
. Then, applying the same argument as in
6.2
, we conclude that the composition of Dehn twists along the
2(m+1)
boundary components of
S^{n}_{2(m+1)}
is smoothly isotopic to the identity relative to
\partial S^{n}_{2(m+1)}
. This completes the induction.
∎

Using
6.3
, we can prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
Y
be a closed 3-manifold bounding a simply-connected smooth oriented 4-manifold
X
. Let
Z
be a closed 4-manifold obtained by a connected sum of copies of either
S^{2}\times S^{2}
,
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, or
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
. Choose a class
\phi\in\pi_{1}\mathrm{Diff}(Y)
and denote the resulting boundary Dehn twist by

T_{X\#Z,Y,\phi}\in\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X\#Z,Y)
.
Suppose that

T^{k}_{X\#Z,Y,\phi}\in\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X\#Z,Y)

is isotopic rel.
Y
to the identity for some integer
k
. Then, for any integer
n\geq 0
, the diffeomorphism

T^{k}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y,\,\phi\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\phi}\in\mathrm{Diff}^{+}\big(X^{\#2n}\#Z,\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y\big)

is also isotopic to the identity rel.
Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y
, where
T_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y,\,\phi\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\phi}
denotes the boundary Dehn twist applied to each copy of
Y
corresponding to the class
\phi
.

Proof.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{2n}
be
2n
copies of
X
. For each
i=1,\dots,2n
, choose collar neighborhoods

f_{i}\colon Y\times[0,1]\hookrightarrow X_{i},

and let
B_{i}
be an open 4-ball embedded in
f_{i}(Y\times[0.2,0.8])
. Denote by
S_{i}
the boundary of
B_{i}
, and by
S^{\prime}_{i}
the
i
th component of the boundary of
S^{4}_{2n}
, the
2n
-punctured 4-sphere. Define

Y^{+}_{i}=f_{i}(Y\times\{0.1\}),\qquad Y^{-}_{i}=f_{i}(Y\times\{0.9\}),\qquad X^{0}_{i}=X_{i}\smallsetminus B_{i},

and write

X^{\#2n}=\big(X^{0}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup X^{0}_{2n}\sqcup S^{4}_{2n}\big)\mathbin{/}\sim,

where
\sim
identifies each
S_{i}
with
S^{\prime}_{i}
. By
6.1
, there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
such that for each
i
,

T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{+}_{i},\phi}\sim T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{i},\phi}\circ T_{S_{i}}^{\alpha}\sim T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{i},\phi}\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{i}}^{\alpha}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial X^{\#2n}.

Since
T_{S^{\prime}_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{2n}}\sim\mathrm{id}_{S^{4}_{2n}}
rel.
\partial S^{4}_{2n}
by
6.3
, we deduce:

\begin{split}T_{X^{\#2n},\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y,\,\phi\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\phi}&\sim T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{+}_{1},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{+}_{2n},\phi}\\
&\sim T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{1}}^{\alpha}\circ\cdots\circ T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{2n}}^{\alpha}\\
&\sim T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\circ\big(T_{S^{\prime}_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{S^{\prime}_{2n}}\big)^{\alpha}\\
&\sim T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial X^{\#2n}.\end{split}

Now observe that, as discussed in the proof of
[
auckly2015stable
, Theorem 5.3]
, since the boundary Dehn twists on punctured
S^{2}\times S^{2}
,
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, and
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
extend smoothly to their interiors, it follows from
6.1
that the following map is well-defined, regardless of where we attach
Z
:

\pi_{0}\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X^{\#2n},\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y)\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X^{\#2n}\#Z,\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y),\qquad f\longmapsto f\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}.

Therefore, we have

\begin{split}T^{k}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y,\,\phi\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\phi}&\sim\left(T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y,\,\phi\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\phi}\right)\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}\\
&\sim\left(T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\right)^{k}\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}\\
&\sim\left(T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\right)\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial(X^{\#2n}\#Z),\end{split}

and we may assume that
Z
is attached to
X_{1}\smallsetminus f_{1}(Y\times[0,1])\subset X^{\#2n}
, which is diffeomorphic to
X_{1}
itself. Moreover, by the assumption of the lemma,

T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}\sim\mathrm{id}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial(X^{\#2n}\#Z).

Hence,

\begin{split}T^{k}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,\,Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y,\,\phi\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\phi}&\sim\left(T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\right)\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}\\
&\sim\left(T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{1},\phi}\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}\right)\circ T^{k}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,Y^{-}_{2},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T^{k}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\\
&\sim T^{k}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,Y^{-}_{2},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T^{k}_{X^{\#2n}\#Z,Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\\
&\sim\left(T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2},\phi}\circ\cdots\circ T^{k}_{X^{\#2n},Y^{-}_{2n},\phi}\right)\#\mathrm{id}_{Z}\qquad\text{rel.\ }\partial(X^{\#2n}\#Z).\end{split}

As before, we may now assume that
Z
is attached to
X_{2}\smallsetminus f_{2}(Y\times[0,1])\subset X^{\#2n}
, which is diffeomorphic to
X_{2}
, and repeat the argument
(2n-1)
more times to conclude the proof.
∎

6.2.
Family Spin structures and an algebraic obstruction

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
X
be a connected, compact, smooth, oriented 4-manifold with boundary

\partial X=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}Y

for some closed, oriented 3-manifold
Y
, and suppose that
b_{1}(X)=0
. Let
\rho\colon B\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to B\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X)
be a homotopy coherent smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
X
. Assume that the homotopy monodromy preserves each component of
\partial X
and the Spin structure
\mathfrak{s}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathfrak{s}
on
\partial X
for some Spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
Y
.
Further assume that the restriction
\rho|_{\partial X}\colon B\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to B\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(\partial X)
is induced by a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
Y
. Suppose also that
\mathfrak{s}
admits a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lift
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
and a non-equivariant extension to a Spin structure
\mathfrak{s}_{X}
on
X
. Then the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Spin structure

\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
on
\partial X
extends to a fiberwise Spin structure on the smooth
X
-bundle associated to
\rho
, whose restriction to each fiber is
\mathfrak{s}_{X}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{s}_{X}
is invariant under the homotopy monodromy of
\rho
, it defines a fiberwise Spin structure on
\rho|_{(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{1}}
, where we choose a simplicial complex structure on
B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and let
(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{1}
denote its 1-skeleton. To extend this to a fiberwise Spin structure on all of
\rho
, we must ensure the vanishing of a sequence of obstruction classes. Following the proof of
[
KPT24
, Lemma 2.4]
, we see that these obstruction classes are given by

o_{i}(\rho,\mathfrak{s}_{X})\in H^{i}(B\mathbb{Z}_{2};\pi_{i-1}B\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})),\quad i\geq 1.

Since
\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})=H^{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is a discrete space, it follows that
o_{i}(\rho)=0
for all
i\neq 2
. Furthermore, by arguing as in the proof of
[
KPT24
, Corollary 2.5]
, we see that the image of
o_{2}(\rho,\mathfrak{s}_{X})
under the map

H^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}_{2};H^{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))\longrightarrow H^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}_{2};H^{0}(\partial X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))=H^{2}\left(B\mathbb{Z}_{2};\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right),

is equal to the boundary obstruction class
o_{2}(\rho|_{\partial X},\mathfrak{s})
. This map is clearly injective, since the local systems
H^{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
H^{0}(\partial X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
are trivial. The class
o_{2}(\rho|_{\partial X},\mathfrak{s})
vanishes because
\mathfrak{s}
admits a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant lift. Therefore,
\mathfrak{s}_{X}
extends to a fiberwise Spin structure on
\rho
.

We now classify fiberwise Spin structures on
\rho
whose restriction to each fiber is
\mathfrak{s}_{X}
. A standard obstruction theory argument shows that such structures are classified by elements of

H^{1}(B\mathbb{Z}_{2};H^{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))\cong H^{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Similarly, fiberwise Spin structures on
\rho|_{\partial X}
whose restriction to each fiber is
\mathfrak{s}
are classified by elements of
H^{0}(\partial X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{n}
. Since the pullback map

i^{*}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong H^{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow H^{0}(\partial X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{n}

is given by the diagonal embedding
1\mapsto(1,\dots,1)
, we obtain a canonical bijection between fiberwise Spin structures on
\rho
whose restriction to each fiber is
\mathfrak{s}_{X}
and fiberwise Spin structures on
\rho|_{\partial X}
whose restriction to each fiber is
\mathfrak{s}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathfrak{s}
.

Moreover, by appealing to the discussion of the classification of equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structures via equivariant
H^{2}
-classes in
Section

4.2
, we see that the latter are in canonical bijection with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Spin structures on
\partial X
whose restrictions to each component are identical. Therefore, the lemma follows.
∎

We now prove an algebraic analogue of
6.4
, which will be used directly to prove
1.1
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
Y
be a Seifert fibered
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homology sphere, equipped with a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action arising as a subaction of the Seifert
S^{1}
-action. Let
X
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homology ball bounded by
Y
, and suppose that the boundary Dehn twist
T^{k}_{X,Y}\in\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(X,Y)
induced by the Seifert action on
Y
is smoothly isotopic to the identity rel. boundary after
s
stabilizations for some
s\in\{0,1,2\}
and some odd integer
k
, i.e.,

T^{k}_{X,Y}\#\mathrm{id}_{(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#s}}\sim\mathrm{id}_{X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#s}}\qquad\text{rel.\ boundary}.

Then, for any integer
n\geq 1
and any self-conjugate
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Spin structure
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
on
Y
, there exists a local map of level
s
of the form

C^{\ast}\big(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\big)\longrightarrow\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2n}C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\big(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{2}\big).

Here, the tensor product is taken over
C^{\ast}(B(\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

Proof.

We will assume
k=1
; the general case can be proven in a very similar way. See
[
KPT24
, Remark 3.6]
for an explanation. Let
Y_{n}
denote the
2n
-fold disjoint union
Y\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Y
, and consider the Borel construction

E_{Y_{n}}\colon Y_{n}\longrightarrow Y_{n}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}E\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow B\mathbb{Z}_{2},

induced by the given
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
Y_{n}
. Let us also consider the stabilized manifold

X^{\mathrm{st}}=X^{\#2n}\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#s}.

By
6.4
and
[
KPT24
, Proposition 3.5]
, there exists a smooth
X^{\mathrm{st}}
bundle
E_{X^{\mathrm{st}}}
over
B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
whose associated
Y_{n}
bundle is precisely
E_{Y_{n}}
. Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Spin structure on
Y
. Abusing notation, we also denote by
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
the induced
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant Spin structure
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
on
Y_{n}
. Then, by
6.5
, there exists a fiberwise Spin structure
\mathfrak{s}_{X^{\mathrm{st}}}
on
E_{X^{\mathrm{st}}}
that restricts to the fiberwise Spin structure on
E_{Y_{n}}
induced by
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Thus it follows from
2.40
that the element

\mathfrak{B}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y_{n},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}))\in\mathfrak{F}^{sp,str}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

is
s
-locally trivial. Hence, by
5.40
, its singular
\mathrm{Pin}(2)
-cochain complex

C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}(\mathfrak{B}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y_{n},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}));\mathbb{Z}_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}^{ch}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

is also
s
-locally trivial. Since

SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y_{n},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}})\simeq\bigwedge_{i=1}^{2n}SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}),

we obtain

\begin{split}C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\!\big(\mathfrak{B}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y_{n},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}));\mathbb{Z}_{2}\big)&\simeq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2n}C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)}\!\big(\mathfrak{B}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}));\mathbb{Z}_{2}\big)\\
&\simeq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2n}C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{2}).\end{split}

This establishes the lemma.
∎

6.3.
Proof of the main theorem

In this section, we denote by
M=(C,d)
the
\mathfrak{R}
-bimodule defined in
5.41
.
For convenience, we adopt the following notation. A
monomial
(in
\mathfrak{R}
) is an element of the form
Q^{i}U^{j}\theta^{k}
, where
i
,
j
, and
k
are integers. Recall that
M
has a basis set

\mathcal{B}=\{x_{i}\mid-5\leq i\leq 5\}\cup\{y_{j}\mid-5\leq j\leq 5,\ j\neq 0\}.

Given an element
x\in M^{\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}n}
and a sequence
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in\mathcal{B}
, note that
x
admits a unique expression of the form

x=\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}\in\mathcal{B}}K_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\,m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{n},

where each
K_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\in\mathfrak{R}
is a polynomial. Then we write
K_{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}}
uniquely as a sum of pairwise distinct monomials:

K_{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}S_{i}.

We call
K_{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}}
the
coefficient of
b_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{n}
in
x
, and denote it by
\mathrm{Coef}(x;b_{1},\dots,b_{n})
. Moreover, given a monomial
m
, we say that
m
is contained in
\mathrm{Coef}(x;b_{1},\dots,b_{n})
if
m\in\{S_{1},\dots,S_{p}\}
.

Remark 6.7
.

For making computations as simple as possible, we extend the notion of local maps of level
k
to
\mathfrak{R}
-module maps between right
\mathfrak{R}
-modules as follows. Given two right
\mathfrak{R}
-modules
M,N
such that
M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}
and
N\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}
are quasi-isomorphic as right
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
-modules to some degree shifts of
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, we say that a (right)
\mathfrak{R}
-module map
f\colon M\rightarrow N
is a
local map of level
k
if the map

f\otimes\mathrm{id}\colon M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}\longrightarrow N\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathfrak{R}_{0}

is homotopic to
Q^{k}\cdot f^{\prime}
for some right
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
-module quasi-isomorphism
f^{\prime}
.

It is then clear that, for any
E_{\infty}

\mathfrak{R}
-modules
M,N
of SWF-type, if an
E_{\infty}

\mathfrak{R}
-module map
f\colon M\rightarrow N
is a local map of level
k
, then it is also a local map of level
k
as a right
A_{\infty}

\mathfrak{R}
-module map.

Lemma 6.8
.

Given an integer
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, an
\mathfrak{R}
-module map

f\colon\mathfrak{R}\longrightarrow M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M

is local of level
i
if and only if the coefficient of
x_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{0}
in
f(1)
is
Q^{i}+\theta y
for some
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}[Q,\theta]
of degree
i-1
.

Proof.

As observed in
5.42
, the projection
p
of
M
onto
\mathfrak{R}\cdot x_{0}
defines a local map of level 0. Since the coefficient of
x_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{0}
in
f(1)
equals the value of
(p\otimes\cdots\otimes p)\circ f(1)
, the result follows from
5.37
. Note that
U
or
V
does not appear in
y
since
\deg U=2
while
\deg y=i-1\leq 1
.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

There does not exist a local map
f\colon\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M
of level 2.

Proof.

Write
f(1)
as
\alpha
, which is a cocycle in
M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M
. From
6.8
, we know that

\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},x_{0},x_{0})=Q^{2}+\lambda_{1}Q\theta+\lambda_{2}\theta^{2}

for some
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Since
d\alpha=0
, we compute

\begin{split}0&=\mathrm{Coef}(d\alpha;y_{1},x_{0},x_{0})\\
&=(Q^{2}+\lambda_{1}Q\theta+\lambda_{2}\theta^{2})(U+\theta^{2})+U\cdot\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{1},x_{0},x_{0})+Q\cdot\sum_{i=\pm 1}\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},x_{0},x_{0})\pmod{Q^{3}}.\end{split}

To cancel the
Q^{2}\theta^{2}
term in the product
(Q^{2}+\lambda_{1}Q\theta+\lambda_{2}\theta^{2})(U+\theta^{2})
, the same term must appear in
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{1},x_{0},x_{0})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{-1},x_{0},x_{0})
. Hence,
Q\theta^{2}
must be contained in either
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{1},x_{0},x_{0})
or
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{-1},x_{0},x_{0})
.

The same statements also apply to
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{\pm 1},x_{0})
and
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},x_{0},y_{\pm 1})
. Hence, we see that there exist unique indices
i,j,k\in\{-1,1\}
such that the term
Q\theta^{2}
is contained

•

in
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},x_{0},x_{0})
,
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{j},x_{0})
, and
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},x_{0},y_{k})
,

•

but not in
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{-i},x_{0},x_{0})
,
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{-j},x_{0})
, and
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},x_{0},y_{-k})
.

We then compute:

\begin{split}0&=\mathrm{Coef}(d\alpha;y_{i},y_{-j},x_{0})\\
&=(U+\theta^{2})\cdot\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{-j},x_{0})+U\cdot\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{i},y_{-j},x_{0})+Q\cdot\sum_{\ell=\pm 1}\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{\ell},y_{-j},x_{0})\\
&\quad+(U+\theta^{2})\cdot\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},x_{0},x_{0})+U\cdot\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},x_{-j},x_{0})+Q\cdot\sum_{\ell=\pm 1}\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},y_{\ell},x_{0})\pmod{Q^{2}}\\
&=\theta^{2}\cdot\big(\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{-j},x_{0})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},x_{0},x_{0})\big)+U\cdot(\text{something})\\
&\quad+Q\cdot\big(\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},y_{j},x_{0})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{-i},y_{-j},x_{0})\big)\pmod{Q^{2}}.\end{split}

To cancel the
Q\theta^{4}
term in
\theta^{2}\cdot\big(\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{-j},x_{0})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},x_{0},x_{0})\big)
, the term
\theta^{4}
must be contained in
\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},y_{j},x_{0})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{-i},y_{-j},x_{0})
.
For simplicity, for each
s\in\{1,2,3\}
and
t\in\{-1,1\}
, define the mod 2 values
c_{s,t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by:

\begin{split}c_{1,t}&=\begin{cases}1&\text{if the term }\theta^{4}\text{ is contained in }\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{ti},y_{tj},x_{0}),\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\\
c_{2,t}&=\begin{cases}1&\text{if the term }\theta^{4}\text{ is contained in }\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{ti},x_{0},y_{tk}),\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\\
c_{3,t}&=\begin{cases}1&\text{if the term }\theta^{4}\text{ is contained in }\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{tj},y_{tk}),\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Then the above computation implies that
c_{1,1}+c_{1,-1}=1
. A similar argument yields
c_{2,1}+c_{2,-1}=1
and
c_{3,1}+c_{3,-1}=1
.

Now, by considering the coefficients of
d\alpha
for
y_{i}\otimes y_{j}\otimes y_{k}
, we obtain

0=\mathrm{Coef}(d\alpha;y_{i},y_{j},y_{k})=\theta^{2}\big(\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{j},y_{k})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},x_{0},y_{k})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{i},y_{j},x_{0})\big)\pmod{U,Q}.

By extracting the coefficient of
\theta^{6}
, we deduce that
c_{1,1}+c_{2,1}+c_{3,1}=0
. Similarly, since we also have

0=\mathrm{Coef}(d\alpha;y_{-i},y_{-j},y_{-k})=\theta^{2}\big(\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;x_{0},y_{-j},y_{-k})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{-i},x_{0},y_{-k})+\mathrm{Coef}(\alpha;y_{-i},y_{-j},x_{0})\big)\pmod{U,Q},

we obtain
c_{1,-1}+c_{2,-1}+c_{3,-1}=0
. But then we have

\begin{split}1&=(c_{1,1}+c_{1,-1})+(c_{2,1}+c_{2,-1})+(c_{3,1}+c_{3,-1})\\
&=(c_{1,1}+c_{2,1}+c_{3,1})+(c_{1,-1}+c_{2,-1}+c_{3,-1})\\
&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{Z}_{2},\end{split}

a contradiction. The lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

There does not exist a local map
f\colon\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M
of level 2.

Proof.

Suppose that such a map
f
exists. Consider the local map
p\colon M\to\mathfrak{R}
of level
0
, defined in
5.42
. Then, by
5.37
, the composed map

\mathfrak{R}\xrightarrow{f}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{id}\otimes p}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M

is also a local map of level
2
, contradicting
6.9
.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

As discussed in
5.42
, there exists a map
f\colon\mathfrak{R}\to M
which is local of level
1
. Taking its tensor square gives a local map of level
2
from
\mathfrak{R}
to
M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M
. Hence, we needed to take the tensor product of at least three copies of
M
to obstruct the existence of a local map of level
2
from
\mathfrak{R}
, which is exactly what we did in
6.9
.

Now we can prove the main theorem.

Proof of
1.1
.

Consider the Mazur manifold
X
bounded by
Y=\Sigma(3,5,19)
. Since
Y
is Seifert fibered, we can define the boundary Dehn twist
T_{X,Y}
via the Seifert action on
Y
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let

f_{i}:=T_{X,Y}^{2i+1}.

Since
T_{X,Y}
is orientation-preserving, acts trivially on
H_{\ast}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, and
X
is simply connected, it is topologically isotopic
[
Orson-Powell:2022-1
, Corollary C]
and stably smoothly isotopic
[
saeki2006stable
,
gabai2023pseudo
,
gabai3
]
(see also
[
KMPW24
, Theorem 2.5]
) to the identity rel. boundary.
Also, since
Y
is a Brieskorn homology sphere, no nontrivial power of
T_{X,Y}
is smoothly isotopic to the identity rel. boundary by
[
KPT24
, Theorem 1.1]
.
21
21

21

While the original proof relies on
[
baraglia2024brieskorn
, Theorem 6.1]
, it can now be replaced with
4.63
.
Thus,
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
are not smoothly isotopic rel. boundary whenever
i\neq j
.

Now suppose that
f_{i}\#\mathrm{id}
is smoothly isotopic to the identity in
X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}
rel. boundary. By
5.41
and
5.36
, we see that

\widetilde{C}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(SWF_{\mathrm{Pin}(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\text{ is locally equivalent to }M.

Thus, it follows from
6.6
and
5.37
that there exists a local map
\mathfrak{R}\to M^{\otimes 4}
of level 2. However, we have shown in
6.10
that such a map does not exist, a contradiction. Therefore,
f_{i}
is not smoothly isotopic to the identity in
X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})^{\#2}
rel. boundary. The theorem follows.∎

Appendix A
Atiyah–Segal–Singer’s equivariant index theorem for manifolds with boundary

A.1.
Equivariant index theorem

We use the equivariant index theorem of Atiyah–Segal–Singer for
4
-manifolds with boundary
[
donnelly1978eta
]
, applied to
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
Dirac operators. This theorem expresses the index as the sum of an integral involving certain combinations of differential forms and a boundary correction term. Note that the integral part coincides with that of the equivariant version of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem
[
atiyah1968indexII
,
atiyah1968indexIII
,
atiyah1968lefschetz
,
atiyah1970spin
,
berline2003heat
]
.

Let
X
be a compact smooth
4
-manifold with boundary, equipped with a smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action. Suppose that
\partial X=Y
is the disjoint union of rational homology
3
-spheres (possibly empty). We assume that the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action preserves each component.
Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure on
X
, equipped with a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant Riemannian metric
g
that is a product metric near the boundary. By definition of an equivariant
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
structure, there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action on the principal
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
bundle
P
that covers the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action on
X
.

For each
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-fixed point
x
in the interior of
X
, recall that we may write the action of
\gamma=[1]\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
locally around
x
as follows.

•

If
x
is an isolated fixed point, then there exist integers
k_{1},k_{2}
such that
0<k_{1},k_{2}<p
, and the action of
\gamma
on
\mathfrak{s}
near
x
is given by

[(x,y,z)]\longmapsto\bigl[\,((-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}\zeta_{2p}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}x,\;(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}\zeta_{2p}^{k_{1}-k_{2}}y,\;\zeta_{p}^{m}\zeta_{2p}z)\,\bigr].

•

If
x
is contained in a
2
-dimensional component of
X^{\gamma}
, then there exists an integer
k
such that
0<k<p
, and the action of
\gamma
on the fiber of
S
at
x
is given by

[(x,y,z)]\longmapsto\bigl[\,((-1)^{k+1}\zeta_{2p}^{k}x,\;(-1)^{k+1}\zeta_{2p}^{-k}y,\;\zeta_{p}^{m}\zeta_{2p}z)\,\bigr].

•

Note that, in both cases,
m
is the equivariance number
n_{\mathrm{eqv}}^{x}(\mathfrak{s})
of
\mathfrak{s}
at
x
, as defined in
4.5
, and
x,y\in SO(2)
and
z\in U(1)
.
